\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\mathscr{I}
denotes the natural inclusion, moreover the left vertical map in the diagram is an isomorphism with inverse
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}
.

Proof.

By untangling the definitions we see that for
\mathscr{F}:\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
we have

(30)

\mathbb{D}\mathscr{F}(\theta_{i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\underset{(\theta_{i^{\prime}}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{i})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{i}}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(\theta_{i^{\prime}}).

We claim that the inclusion
\mathscr{I}_{\theta_{i}}:\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{i}}\hookrightarrow\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{i}}
is coinitial: indeed, for a given
\theta_{i^{\prime}}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{i}
we have
\mathscr{I}/\theta_{i^{\prime}}\cong\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{i^{\prime}}}
, and the geometric realization of the latter category is isomorphic to the geometric realization of
\theta_{i^{\prime}}
, which is contractible. It follows that
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathscr{I}_{*}\mathscr{I}^{*}\mathbb{D}
, so we have

\mathbb{D}\mathscr{I}_{!}\cong\mathscr{I}_{*}\mathscr{I}^{*}\mathbb{D}\mathscr{I}_{!}\cong\mathscr{I}_{*}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}},

meaning that the top square in the diagram (
29
) commutes, the commutativity of the bottom square follows by a dual argument.

To prove the last claim observe that
\mathbb{D}^{\theta}_{\mathrm{el}}
and
\mathbb{D}^{\theta}
described in
2.18
are in fact the Verdier duality functors of
[
1
]
. Note that for
c_{k}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta
we have an isomorphism

|(\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n/\theta})_{<i}|\cong|\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\partial c_{k}}|\cong S^{k-1},

so this is a Verdier poset in the notation of loc. cit., it now follows from
[
1
, Theorem A]
that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}
is an isomorphism.

This does not yet prove that
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}
is its inverse, we will prove
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)
by induction on
k
, the case
k=0
being trivial (since
\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/c_{0}}\cong\{\mathrm{id}_{c_{0}}\}
). Assume we have proved the claim for
k<n
, denote
P\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/c_{n}}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{c_{n}}\}
, since
P^{\triangleright}\cong\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/c_{n}}
is Verdier, it follows that
P
is Gorenstein. By definition we have

(31)

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\underset{(c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/c_{n}}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{i\circ i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta).

We can view (
31
) as a limit cone
\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\triangleleft}_{n,/c_{n}}\cong P^{\triangleleft\triangleright}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
, by
[
1
, Theorem B]
it is also a colimit cocone, meaning that we have

(32)

\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\underset{\binom{\bot}{c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}}\in P^{\triangleleft}}{\mathrm{colim}}\binom{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}{\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{i\circ i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)},

where the notation
\binom{\bot}{c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}}\in P^{\triangleleft}
suggests that the elements of
P^{\triangleleft}
are either of the form
\bot
or
(c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n})\in P
and in the expression
\binom{\mathbb{D}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}{\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{i\circ i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}
the top element shows the value of the colimit diagram on
\bot
, while the bottom – on elements of
P
. Using our inductive assumption we also have for
k<n

(33)

\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{k}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\underset{(c_{q}\overset{u^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/c_{k}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{q}\overset{u\circ u^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta).

Denote by
D
the coCartesian fibration over
P
with fiber over
c_{k}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}
given by
\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{/c_{k}}
. In other words, it is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n/c_{n}})
on
c_{q}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k}
with
k<n
. Denote by
p_{0}:D\rightarrow P^{\mathrm{op}}
the projection to the source of the arrow. Putting everything together we get

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{\binom{\bot}{c_{q}\overset{i^{\prime\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}}\in D^{\triangleleft}}{\mathrm{colim}}\binom{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{q}\overset{i\circ i^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}

\displaystyle\cong\underset{\binom{\bot}{c_{q}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}}\in(P^{\mathrm{op}})^{\triangleleft}}{\mathrm{colim}}\binom{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}{\underset{c_{q}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n},\;i^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime\prime}\cong i_{0}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{q}\overset{i\circ i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}

\displaystyle\cong\underset{\binom{\top}{c_{q}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}}\in(P^{\triangleright})^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\binom{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}{\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{q}\overset{i\circ i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)}

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(c_{q}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/c_{n}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{q}\overset{i\circ i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta)\cong\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{el}}\mathscr{F}(c_{n}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta),

where the first isomorphism follows by substituting (
33
) into (
32
), the second by computing the colimit over
D^{\triangleright}
as a composition of left Kan extension along
p_{0}
and colimit over
(P^{\mathrm{op}})^{\triangleleft}
, the third since the colimit over
\{c_{q}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k}\overset{i^{\prime\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n},\;i^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime\prime}\cong i_{0}\}
is a cotensor with a contractible category and
(P^{\mathrm{op}})^{\triangleright}\cong(P^{\triangleleft})^{\mathrm{op}}
, the fourth by using
(P^{\triangleleft})^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{/c_{n}}
and the last isomorphism follows by definition.
∎

Proposition 2.20
.

There is an equivalence

\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})\cong\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}).

Proof.

We will first define a functor

F:\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}).

We denote by
p:D\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
the Cartesian fibration whose fiber over
\theta_{j\circ u}\xrightarrow{u}\theta_{j}
is
\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta_{j\circ u}}
and such that the Cartesian morphism over the morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j\circ u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

with target
(\theta_{j\circ u^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}})
is given by

\hbox to159.21pt{\vbox to176.77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 79.60168pt\lower-88.38301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-79.60168pt}{-84.72328pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

In particular, a general morphism in
D
is given by a diagram

(34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j\circ u\circ i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Im}(v^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j\circ u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

and it has source
\theta_{j\circ u\circ i}
and target
\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}}
. We define
q:D\rightarrow\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta}
by sending
\theta_{j\circ u\circ i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j\circ u}
to
\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta
and a morphism (
34
) to the composition

\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i)\overset{\widetilde{i}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i^{\prime\prime}\circ a)\cong\mathrm{Im}(u\circ v^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime})\overset{a_{v}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Im}(v\circ u\circ v^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Im}(u^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}),

where the first isomorphism follows since
a
is active and the rest by commutativity of (
34
) and the morphism
a_{v}
is induced by
v
. We first define
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}p_{!}q^{*}\mathscr{F}
. Explicitly, we have

(35)

\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(\theta_{j\circ u}\xrightarrow{u}\theta_{j})\cong\underset{(\theta_{j\circ u\circ i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j\circ u})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{j\circ u}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i)\xrightarrow{j|_{\mathrm{Im}(u\circ i)}}\theta).

Denote by
\mathscr{F}^{j}
the restriction of
\mathscr{F}
to
\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}
, then it is easy to see from (
35
) and definition of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
that

(36)

\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(\theta_{j\circ u}\xrightarrow{u}\theta_{j})\cong\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\mathscr{F}^{j}(\mathrm{Im}(u)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}).

In particular, (
36
) implies that

\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(\theta_{j\circ u}\xrightarrow{u}\theta_{j})\cong\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(\mathrm{Im}(u)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}),

meaning that
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
factors through
\mathrm{TwAr}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})[S_{\mathrm{act}}^{-1}]\cong C^{\prime}
, where the isomorphism follows from
Lemma

2.15
.

Note that since
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
,
\mathscr{F}^{j}
is the right Kan extension of its restriction to
\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}
, so it follows from the first claim of
Lemma

2.19
and (
36
) that
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
viewed as an object of
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta_{j}})
is the left Kan extension of its restriction to
\Theta^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}
. In other words, we have

(37)

\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(\mathrm{Im}(u)\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j})\cong\underset{e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Im}(u)}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathscr{F}^{\prime}(e\overset{i_{0}\circ i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}).

It follows from (
37
) and
Lemma

2.17
that
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is the left Kan extension of its restriction to
C^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}
, so it can be identified with an object
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(C^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}})
. Finally, it follows once again from (
37
) that
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
inverts
S_{\mathrm{el}}
(in the notation of
Lemma

2.16
), hence factors through
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(C^{\prime}_{\mathrm{el}}[S_{\mathrm{el}}^{-1}])\cong\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
, where the isomorphism follows from
Lemma

2.16
; we define
F(\mathscr{F})
to be the image of
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
.

Denote by
p^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta}
the natural projection and by
q^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}
the morphism that sends
\theta_{j\circ i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta
to
\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i)
and a morphism

(38)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j\circ i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Im}(v\circ i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{j^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

to the composition

\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i)\cong\mathrm{Im}(j^{\prime}\circ v\circ i)\cong\mathrm{Im}(j^{\prime}\circ i_{0}\circ a)\xleftarrow{\widetilde{i}}\mathrm{Im}(j^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}).

We define

G:\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})

by
G\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}p^{\prime}_{*}q^{{}^{\prime},*}
. By definition we have

(39)

G\mathscr{G}(\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta)\cong\underset{\theta_{j\circ i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}}{\lim}\mathscr{G}(\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i))

for
\mathscr{G}\in\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
. Denote by
\mathscr{G}^{j}:\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
the functor sending
\theta_{j\circ i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j}
to
\mathscr{G}(\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i))
, then it follows from (
39
) that

(40)

G\mathscr{G}(\theta_{j})\cong\mathbb{D}\mathscr{G}^{j}(\theta_{j}=\joinrel=\theta_{j}).

It follows from the proof of
Lemma

2.19
that
\mathbb{D}
factors through
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{j}})\xrightarrow{\mathscr{I}_{*}}\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{j}})
, so in particular
G\mathscr{G}
does indeed belong to
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
.

Finally, to show that
F
and
G
are inverse to one another we can use (
40
) and (
36
) to show that

G\circ F\mathscr{F}(\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta)\cong\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\mathscr{F}^{j}(\theta_{j}=\joinrel=\theta_{j})\cong\mathscr{F}(\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta),

where the last isomorphism follows from the second claim of
Lemma

2.19
using that
\mathscr{F}^{j}
lies in the image of
\mathscr{I}_{*}
, the proof of
G\circ F\cong\mathrm{id}
is the same, this time using
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\mathbb{D}\cong\mathrm{id}
.
∎

Theorem 2.21
.

There is an equivalence

\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\theta})\cong\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}),

moreover for any
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
we have

\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})\cong\underset{f:\theta\rightarrow\mathscr{E}}{\lim}\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}).

Proof.

Combine
2.9
and
2.20
for the first claim and use the second claim of
Lemma

2.3
for the second.
∎

3.
Steiner complexes

For a collection
\{f_{1},...,f_{l}\}
of 1-morphisms in a category, there is an essentially unique way in which they might be composable – namely, if the target of one always coincides with the source of another. This is no longer the case for higher categories – objects of
\Theta_{n}
represent ways in which morphisms might be composed, however they no longer cover all the options as for example the orientals of
[
27
]
or the lax cubes of
[
22
]
do not belong to
\Theta_{n}
. These more general arrangements of morphisms are called
pasting schemes
, and there have been numerous formalisms aimed at making this vague idea precise – see
[
9
]
for a review of some of those formalisms.

Our work will also make use of pasting schemes outside of
\Theta_{n}
in the definition of the twisted arrows category in
Section

6
, so we will also need to pick a formalism to describe them. We will employ the augmented directed chain complexes with unital loop-free basis of
[
25
]
, which we will simply call
Steiner complexes
following
[
2
]
. Our goal in this section is to properly introduce this notion and prove some of its basic properties, culminating with
3.20
. Admittedly, we will prove more that is necessary for the applications in
Section

6
, however this will also help us prove one of the main results of the paper –
Theorem

4.20
.

Definition 3.1
.

Recall from
[
25
]
that an augmented directed complex (
ADC
for short) is a chain complex (in the classical sense)
(...\rightarrow K_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial_{n-1}}K_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{n-2}}...\xrightarrow{\partial_{0}}K_{0})
of abelian groups and an augmentation
e:K_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that
e\circ\partial_{0}\cong 0
, we also require that each
K_{n}
admits a distinguished commutative submonoid
K_{n}^{+}
such that
K_{n}
is the group completion of
K^{+}_{n}
. A morphism
f:K\rightarrow L
of ADCs is by definition a morphism of underlying chain complexes such that
f_{j}(K_{j}^{+})\subset L^{+}_{j}
. We will call the
dimension
of
K
denoted
\dim K
the maximum
n
such that
K_{n}\neq 0
.

A
basis
for an ADC is the data of elements
P_{n}\subset K_{n}^{+}
for all
n
such that
K_{n}^{+}
is a free commutative monoid on
P_{n}
. Given a basis
P_{n}
, we can write any element
u\in K_{n}
as
u=\sum_{g\in P_{n}}u_{g}*g
with
u_{g}\in\mathbb{Z}
, this induces a partial order on
K_{n}
where we declare
u\geq v
just in case
u_{g}\geq v_{g}
for
g\in P_{n}
. We can then write
\partial_{n-1}u=\partial^{+}u-\partial^{-}u
, where
\partial^{\pm}u\geq 0
. We also denote

u\wedge v\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{g\in P_{n}}\min(u_{g},v_{g})g.

For
u\in K_{n}
we denote
[u]=([u]^{-}_{0},[u]^{+}_{0},...,[u]^{-}_{n-1},[u]^{+}_{n-1},[u]_{n})
the sequence of elements such that
[u]_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}u
and inductively
[u]^{\pm}_{k}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\partial^{\pm}[u]^{\pm}_{k+1}
and call it an
atom
associated to
u
. We denote by
<_{i}
the transitive closure of a relation on
P\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigcup_{n}P_{n}
such that for
i<\min(l,k)
,
u\in P_{l}
and
v\in P_{k}
we have
u<_{j}v
if
[u]^{+}_{i}\wedge[v]^{-}_{i}>0
. We also write
u<_{\mathbb{N}}v
if either
u\leq\partial^{-}v
or
v\leq\partial^{+}u
.

Finally, we call a basis
P

unital
if for all
u\in P
we have
e([u]^{\pm}_{0})=1
and
loop-free
if
<_{j}
is non-reflexive for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e. if its transitive closure is a partial order. We will call a basis
strongly loop-free
if
<_{\mathbb{N}}
is not reflexive (note that by
[
25
, Propositin 3.7.]
every strongly loop-free basis is loop-free). Following
[
2
]
we will call an ADC with a strongly loop-free unital basis a
strong Steiner complex
and denote by
\mathrm{Stein}
the category of Steiner complexes and morphisms of ADCs between them and by
\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
the subcategory of complexes of dimension
\leq n
.

Construction 3.2
.

We associate to an ADC
K
the strict
\omega
-category
K^{*}
such that its set of
n
-morphisms (called
cells
) is the set of sequences
(X_{0}^{-},X_{0}^{+},...,X^{-}_{n-1},X^{+}_{n-1},X_{n})
such that
X_{i}^{\pm}\geq 0
,

(41)

e(X_{0}^{\pm})=1

and

(42)

\partial_{i}X^{+}_{i+1}\cong\partial_{i}X^{-}_{i+1}=X^{+}_{i}-X^{-}_{i},

we will write
u\in X
for a basis element
u
if it appears in the decomposition of
X_{n}
with non-zero coefficient. In particular, atoms
[u]
associated to
u\in P_{n}
define an
n
-morphism in
K^{*}
. The
i
-composition
X*_{i}Y
of
X
and
Y
is defined to be the cell
Z
such that
Z_{j,\pm}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}X_{j,\pm}+Y_{j,\pm}
for
j>i
,
Z_{i,-}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}X_{i,-}
,
Z_{i,+}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}Y_{i,+}
and
Z_{j,\pm}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}X_{j,\pm}=Y_{j,\pm}
for
j<i
. It is proved in
[
25
]
that those morphisms do form an
\omega
-category, and moreover, if
K
had a unital loop-free basis, then
K^{*}
is a free
\omega
-category in an appropriate sense.

Remark 3.3
.

By
[
26
]
we can associate to
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
an object of
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
with
l
-dimensional basis elements given by inert morphisms
c_{l}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta
, the main result of that work is that its basis is strongly loop-free and the set of morphisms of
n
-categories between
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
is equivalent to the set of morphisms of the corresponding ADCs with basis. It follows that we can identify
\Theta_{n}
with a full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
in this manner, which we will do from now on without further mention. Note that under this identification for
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
the
l
-morphisms of the
n
-category
x^{*}
correspond to morphisms
c_{l}\xrightarrow{f}x
of ADCs.

Notation 3.4
.

Given
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
and
k\leq n
we will denote by
x_{k}
the set of basis elements of
x
of dimension
k
. For
k<n
and
\sigma\in\{-,+\}
we will denote by
d_{k}^{\sigma}x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{k}
the subcomplex of
x
generated by the basis elements of dimension
k
which are minimal (resp. maximal) with respect to
\leq_{k}
if
\sigma=-
(resp.
\sigma=+
), it is easy to see that such a complex indeed lies in
\mathrm{Stein}_{k}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that we have a morphism
f:c_{l}\rightarrow x
for some
x\in\mathrm{Stein}
and
(a,b)
is a pair of elements of
x_{l}
such that
a\leq f(\mathrm{id}_{c_{l}})
and
b\leq f(\mathrm{id}_{c_{l}})
, then
\partial^{\epsilon}_{l-1}a\bigcap\partial^{\epsilon}_{l-1}b=\varnothing
. In particular, if

(43)

f(\mathrm{id}_{c_{l}})=\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I}}n_{i}*b_{i}

with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, then all
n_{i}=1
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from
[
9
, Lemma 3.4.5.]
, the second follows from the first since if for some
i
we have
n_{i}>1
, then the pair
(b_{i},b_{i})
contradicts the first claim.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that for a strong Steiner complex
x
and
b\in x_{k}
we have
\partial^{-}b>0
and
\partial^{+}b>0
: indeed, assume that
\partial^{-}b=0
, then

0=\partial\circ\partial b=\partial(\partial^{+}b)=\partial^{+}\partial^{+}b-\partial^{-}\partial^{+}b,

hence we have
\partial^{+}\partial^{+}b=\partial^{-}\partial^{+}b
. It follows that the induced morphism
c_{k-1}\xrightarrow{f}x
sending
[\mathrm{id}_{c_{k-1}}]
to
\partial^{+}b
defines an endomorphism, which contradicts
3.5
.

Definition 3.7
.

Given a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
, it follows from
3.5
that the image of any basis element
c_{j}\overset{b}{\rightarrowtail}x
in
y
can be identified with a collection of basis elements
\{c^{b}_{0},...,c^{b}_{k}\}
of
y
. We will call the morphism
f

active
if for every basis element
c
of
y
there is a basis element
b
of
x
such that
c=c^{b}_{j}
for some
j
and
inert
if
f
takes basis elements to basis elements of the same dimension. Finally, we will call
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}

elementary
if there exists a basis element
b\in x_{m}
for some
m\leq n
such that every other basis element belongs to some
d^{\pm}_{l}b
. We will denote by
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
the full subcategory containing objects
x
that admit an active morphism
a:c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
(which is necessarily unique by
3.5
again). Given
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
we will denote by
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
the full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n,/x}
on inert morphisms
y\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
and by
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
a further subcategory containing
e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
with
e
elementary (note that such morphisms may be identified with basis elements in
x
).

Remark 3.8
.

If
e\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
with
\dim(e)=n
, then for every
k<n
we have
(d^{-}_{k}e)_{k}\cap(d^{+}_{k}e)_{k}=\varnothing
. Indeed, if
c\in(d^{-}_{k}e)_{k}\cap(d^{+}_{k}e)_{k}
and
e_{n}
denotes the unique basis element of
e
in dimension
n
, then we would have
c\leq_{\mathbb{N}}e_{n}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c
, which contradicts the fact that the basis of
e
was assumed to be strongly loop-free.

Definition 3.9
.

For
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{c}_{n}
and
0\leq j\leq n-1
we will call a morphism
c_{n}\xrightarrow{f}x

j
-active
if the image of
i^{\pm}_{j}:c_{j}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}c_{n}
is
d^{\pm}_{j}x
, additionally we define every morphism to be
(-1)-active
.

Lemma 3.10
.

A morphism
f:c_{n}\rightarrow x
for
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}^{c}
is
(n-1)
-active in the sense of
3.9
if and only if it is active in the sense of
3.7
.

Proof.

Assume that there is some basis element
b_{0}
of dimension
n
that is not in the image of
f
. Consider some chain
b_{0}<_{n-1}b_{1}<_{n-1}...<_{n-1}b_{m}
of maximal length, since it is maximal we must have
d^{+}_{n-1}b_{m}\leq d^{+}_{n-1}x
. Since
f
was assumed
(n-1)
-active, there must be some basis element
e_{0}
in the image of
f
such that
d^{+}_{n-1}b_{m}\cap d^{+}_{n-1}e_{0}\neq\varnothing
, it now follows from
3.5
that
b_{m}=e_{0}
. By construction
d^{+}_{n-1}b_{m-1}\leq d^{-}_{m-1}e_{0}
, since it clearly does not lie in
d^{-}_{n-1}x
there must be some
e_{1}
in the image of
f
such that
d^{+}_{n-1}e_{1}
intersects
d^{+}_{n-1}b_{m-1}
, repeating the same argument we get
b_{m-1}=d_{1}
, iterating this process we eventually prove that
b_{0}
lie in the image of
f
.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

Given a
k
-active
f:c_{n}\rightarrow x
morphism for
k<(n-1)
with
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}^{c}
, there exists a
(k+1)
-active morphism
f^{\mathrm{act}}_{k+1}:C^{k+1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow x
in the notation of
4.7
such that

(44)

f^{\mathrm{act}}_{k+1}\circ i_{k+1}\cong f.

Proof.

Recall the order
\leq_{p}
for
0\leq p\leq n-1
from
3.1
, define
x^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}x
to be the image of
f
. For
\epsilon\in\{-,+\}
define
S^{\epsilon}_{n}\subset x_{n}
to contain elements
b
such that there is
b^{\prime}\in(d^{\epsilon}_{k+1}x^{\prime})_{k+1}
for which
b\geq_{k+1}b^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=+
and
b\leq_{k+1}b^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=-
, for
k+1\leq q<n
and
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{-,+\}
define
S^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}\subset x_{q}
to contain elements
b_{0}
such that there is
b_{0}^{\prime}\in(d^{\epsilon}_{k+1}x^{\prime})_{k+1}
for which
b_{0}\geq_{k+1}b_{0}^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=+
and
b_{0}\leq_{k+1}b_{0}^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=-
and such that
b_{0}
is maximal (resp. minimal) with respect to
\leq_{q}
if
\epsilon^{\prime}=+
(resp.
\epsilon^{\prime}=-
). Now define

y^{\epsilon}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{b\in S^{\epsilon}_{n}}b,

for
k+1\leq q<n
define

(y^{\epsilon})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{b\in S^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}}b

and for
q\leq k
define

(y^{\epsilon})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{b\in(d^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}x^{\prime})_{q}}b.

We need to prove that
(y_{n},y_{n-1}^{+},y_{n-1}^{-},...,y_{0}^{+},y_{0}^{-})
defines a morphism in
x^{*}
. First note that for
k\geq 0
(
41
) holds because
e(d^{\pm}_{0}x^{\prime})=1
and if
k=-1
because
e(d^{\pm}_{0}x)=1
, so it remain to prove (
42
), i.e. that

(45)

\partial y^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}=y^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}-y^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1}

. For
q\leq k
it holds because it holds for
x^{\prime}
, for
q>k+1
we can write

(46)

\partial y^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}=\sum_{b\in S^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{q}}(\partial^{+}b-\partial^{-}b),

on the other hand we have

(47)

(y^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}-y^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1})=\sum_{b_{0}\in S^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}}b_{0}-\sum_{b_{1}\in S^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1}}b_{1}=\sum_{b_{0}\in S^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}\setminus S^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1}}b_{0}-\sum_{b_{1}\in S^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1}\setminus S^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}}b_{1}.

Observe that the only terms that do not cancel out in (
46
) are the ones that lie in
S^{\epsilon,\pm}_{q-1}
while the terms in (
47
) correspond to those elements of
S^{\epsilon,\pm}_{q-1}
that lie in
\partial^{\pm}_{q-1}b
for some
b\in x_{q}
, we claim that we may assume that
b
is either maximal or minimal with respect to
\leq_{q}
, so that it lies in
S^{\epsilon,\pm}_{q}
. Indeed, assume that we have (say)
t\leq\partial^{-}_{q-1}b
for
t\in S^{\epsilon,-}_{q-1}
and
b\leq_{q}b^{\prime}
for some basis element
b^{\prime}
, we may assume that
b\leq\partial^{-}_{q}b^{\prime}
, then since
t
was assumed to be minimal with respect to
(q-1)
it follows that
t\leq\partial^{-}_{q-1}b^{\prime}
, hence that there exists some
b^{\prime\prime}\leq\partial^{+}_{q}b^{\prime}
such that
t\leq\partial^{-}_{q-1}b^{\prime}
, iterating this argument we can make it so that
b^{\prime}\in S^{\epsilon,+}_{q}
. An obvious variation of the same argument also proves that we may pick
b\in S^{\epsilon,-}_{q}
and that the claim also holds for
t\in S^{\epsilon,+}_{q-1}
. Finally, it remains to prove (
42
) for
q=k+1
, note that we can assume
k\geq 0
. We will in fact show that
y^{\epsilon,-\epsilon}_{k+1}=(d^{\epsilon}_{k+1}x^{\prime})_{k+1}
and
y^{\epsilon,\epsilon}_{k+1}=(d^{\epsilon}_{k+1}x)_{k+1}
, this will prove the claim since
f
was assumed to be
k
-active. Note that the first of those claims follows directly from the definition of
S^{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}_{k+1}
, so it suffices to prove
d^{\pm}_{k+1}y^{\pm}=d^{\epsilon}_{k+1}x
. For this note that
d_{k+1}^{\pm}y^{\pm}
defines a composable subset of
d_{k+1}^{\pm}x
that contains
d_{k}^{\sigma}d_{k+1}^{\pm}x^{\prime}=d^{\sigma}_{k}x
since
f
was assumed to be
k
-active, hence it must coincide with
d^{\pm}_{k+1}x
by
Lemma

3.10
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Any morphism
f:c_{n}\rightarrow x
for
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
extends to an active morphism
a^{\prime}:D_{n}(1,0,...,0)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
in the notation of
6.4
such that the composition

c_{n}\overset{D([0]\overset{\{1\}}{\rightarrowtail}[1],\mathrm{id}_{[0]},...,\mathrm{id}_{[0]})}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n}(1,...,0)\overset{a^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}x

is equal to
f
.

Proof.

Consider the functor
G:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
corresponding to the diagram

\hbox to542.3pt{\vbox to67.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 271.14653pt\lower-33.54228pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

then inductively using
Lemma

3.11
for
-1\leq k\leq n-2
we can construct morphisms
f_{k}:C^{k+1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow x
such that each
f_{k}
is
(k+1)
-active and together they define a cocone on
G
with vertex
x
. The claim now follows since the colimit of
G
is by construction isomorphic to
D_{n}(1,0,...,0)
, so the cocone defines a morphism
a^{\prime}:D_{n}(1,...,0)\rightarrow x
, the fact that it is active follows since both
f_{n-1}:C^{n-1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow x
and
C^{n-1}_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}D_{n}(1,...,0)
are active (the former by
Lemma

3.10
, the latter by construction).
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

For
\mathscr{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}
denote

\mathrm{PSh}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\{\varnothing,*\})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathscr{C}),

where the inclusion identifies
\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathscr{C})
with the subcategory of (-1)-truncated objects. Every
(y\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
for
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
defines an element
h_{y}\in\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})
sending
e\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}x
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}}(i_{e},i)
, then
h_{y}
belongs to the full subcategory
\mathrm{PFib}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})
generated by pushout of representables. In particular, if
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, then
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
is contractible.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction on
\dim(y)
: if
\dim(y)=0
, then by (
41
) we have
y\cong*
, so that
h_{y}
is representable. Assume we have proved the claim in dimensions
<k
and
y_{k}=\{b_{1},...,b_{l}\}
, we will complete the proof by induction on
l
. If
l=0
, then
\dim(y)<k
and the claim follows by induction, so assume we have proved the claim for
(l-1)
. We may assume that
b_{l}
is maximal among
b_{i}
with respect to
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
, denote by
y^{\prime}
the subcomplex of
y
generated by all the basis elements except
b_{l}
and by
[b_{l}]
the subcomplex generated by
b_{l}
, then it follows from
[
25
, Proposition 5.1.]
that
y^{\prime}\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
and moreover that we have

h_{y}\cong h_{y^{\prime}}\coprod_{h_{d^{-}_{r}[b_{l}]}}h_{[b_{l}]}

for some
r<k
, which concludes the proof since
h_{y^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{PFib}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})
by induction. The last claim now follows since

|\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}|\cong p_{!}(*),

where
p:\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}\rightarrow\mathrm{pt}
is the unique morphism to the final object of
\mathrm{Cat}
and
*\in\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})
denotes the terminal presheaf. By definition
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
if and only if
*\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{/x}
, which implies that it is an iterated pushout of representables, which in particular means that
p_{!}(*)\cong*
since
p_{!}(h_{y})\cong*
by construction and pushout is a contractible colimit.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

Assume we have a cospan
[n]\overset{a_{0}}{\twoheadleftarrow}[l]\overset{a_{1}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
of active injective morphisms in
\Delta
such that for every elementary interval
(k<k+1)
in
[l]
either the restriction
a_{i}|_{(k<k+1)}:[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[q_{k}]
is given by the identity morphism for some
i\in\{0,1\}
, then we have a pushout diagram

(48)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+m-l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

such that additionally
[n+m-l]
can be identified with the set

X\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathrm{Ob}([n])\coprod\mathrm{Ob}([m]))/\sim,

where
\sim
denotes the equivalence relation that identifies
a_{0}(i)
with
a_{1}(i)
for
i\in[l]
such that
x_{0}\leq x_{1}
for
(x_{0},x_{1})\in X^{\times 2}
if and only if either both
x_{i}
lie in
\mathrm{Ob}([n])
or
\mathrm{Ob}([m])
and
x_{0}\leq x_{1}
or there exists
q\in[l]
such that
x_{0}\leq a_{k}(q)
and
a_{t}(q)\leq x_{1}
for
k\neq t
.

Proof.

We will first prove that (
48
) is a pushout, we will do so by induction on the number of elementary segments
(k<k+1)
for which
a_{0}|_{(k<k+1)}
does not equal identity. If this number is 0, then
a_{0}=\mathrm{id}
and the claim is vacuous. In the general case denote by
(t<t+1)
the maximal (with respect to the linear order on
[l]
) subinterval for which the restriction of
a_{0}
is non-trivial, denote this restriction by
a_{t}:[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[v]
note that we can decompose
a_{0}\cong a_{0}^{\prime}\circ a_{0}^{\prime\prime}
such that
a_{0}^{\prime\prime}|_{(k<k+1)}=a_{0}|_{(k<k+1)}
if
k\neq t
and
a_{0}^{\prime}|_{(k<k+1)}=a_{0}|_{(k<k+1)}
if
k=t
and is identity otherwise. We can then construct the following diagram

(49)

\hbox to253.79pt{\vbox to124pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 126.89467pt\lower-61.99712pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where
i_{t}
is the inclusion of the subinterval
(t<t+1)
. Note that the leftmost square in (
49
) is a pushout by induction, we need to prove that the bottom right square is a pushout. By
[
20
, Lemma 4.4.2.1.]
it suffices to prove that the top right square and the outer right rectangle are pushouts. Note that both
a^{\prime}_{1}\circ i_{t}
and
a^{\prime\prime}_{1}\circ i^{\prime}_{t}
are inert by our condition on
a_{1}
, hence both squares in question are of the form

\hbox to136.6pt{\vbox to70.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.30084pt\lower-35.3195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

so it remains to prove that all such squares are pushouts. First, note that by Segal condition we have

(50)

[n]\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[n])}{\mathrm{colim}}[e]\cong[1]\coprod_{[0]}[1]\coprod_{[0]}...\coprod_{[0]}[1].

Applying (
50
) we see that both
[p+v-1]
and

[t-1]\coprod_{[0]}[v]\coprod_{[0]}[p-t]

are isomorphic. This concludes the proof of the first part of the claim, the second easily follows by inspection.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

The partial order
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
is linear if
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
.

Proof.

We will prove the claim by induction on
\dim(x)
: if
\dim(x)=0
, then
x
is a singleton by (
41
), so in particular the claim is true. Assume we have proved the claim for dimensions
<k
and
x_{k}=\{b_{1},...,b_{l}\}
, we will prove the claim by induction on
l
: of
l=0
, then
\dim(x)<k
and it holds by induction. Assume now that
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
and denote by
b
its unique basis element. Denote by
A
the subset of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/x}
containing all basis elements in
d^{-}_{n-1}x
that do not appear in
d^{+}_{n-1}x
, set
B\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{b\}
and denote by
C
the subset containing all basis elements in
d^{+}_{n-1}x
that do not appear in
d^{-}_{n-1}x
, it follows from
Remark

3.8
that the underlying set of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
decomposes as a disjoint union:

|\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}|=A\coprod B\coprod C.

By construction we have
a\leq_{\mathbb{N}}b
for all
a\in A
and
b\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c
for all
c\in C
, so it remains to prove that all the sets
A
,
B
and
C
are linearly ordered. This claim is trivial for the singleton
B
, to prove it for
A
note that it is a subset of
d^{-}_{n-1}x
, which is linearly ordered by
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
by induction on dimension, and that
a\leq_{\mathbb{N}}a^{\prime}
as elements of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/d^{-}_{n-1}x}
implies that also
a\leq_{\mathbb{N}}a^{\prime}
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
. The same argument also applies for
C
, which concludes the proof.

In the general case we again assume that
b_{l}
is maximal with respect to
<_{\mathbb{N}}
, denote by
x^{\prime}
the subcomplex generated by
\{b_{1},...,b_{l-1}\}
and by
[b_{l}]\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
the subcomplex generated by
b_{l}
, so that we have

x\cong x^{\prime}\coprod_{d^{-}_{r}[b_{l}]}[b_{l}]

for some
r<n
. We first claim that it suffices to treat the case
r=(n-1)
. Indeed, using
3.12
we can extend
c_{r}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}d^{-}_{r}[b_{l}]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}d^{+}_{r}x
to an active morphism morphism
D_{r}(1,...,0)\overset{a^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}d^{+}_{r}x
. Denote by
X
the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{r}(1,0,...,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{+}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, so that the unique active morphism
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{\prime}
and the morphism
a^{\prime}
described above now define a morphism
X\overset{a^{\prime\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{\prime}
. Next, define
X^{\prime}
to be the pushout

\hbox to233.15pt{\vbox to69.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 116.57246pt\lower-34.82841pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

then there is a morphism
X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g}x
whose restriction to
X
is given by
X\overset{a^{\prime\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{\prime}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
and to
c_{n-1}
by the composition
c_{n-1}\overset{a_{0}}{\twoheadrightarrow}d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
, where
a_{0}
exists by induction. Denote by
x^{\prime}_{0}
the image of
g
, then it
x^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
since
X^{\prime}
admits an active morphism from
c_{n}
and
x^{\prime}_{0}
shares a common
(n-1)
-boundary with
[b_{l}]
. It follows from these considerations that we may assume
x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}_{0}
and
r=(n-1)
.

Note that all of the categories
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
,
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/d^{-}_{r}[b_{l}]}
and
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
are linearly ordered either by induction on
l
or
k
or by previous considerations. We will first treat the case
n=1
: note that
*\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}d^{+}_{0}x^{\prime}\cong d^{-}_{0}[b_{l}]
is a singleton and moreover the image of the unique element of
*_{0}
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
(resp.
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
) is a maximal (resp. minimal) element, from which it follows that
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
is a linearly ordered set in which each element
z\neq*
in the image of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
is greater that each element
y
in the image of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
. Assume now that
n>1
, in that case both
*_{-}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}d^{-}_{0}[b_{l}]
and
*_{+}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}d^{+}_{0}[b_{l}]
lie in
d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}]
, note that
*_{-}
(resp.
*_{+}
) is the minimal (resp. maximal) element of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
. It follows that if
z<*_{-}
(resp.
z>*_{+}
) for
z\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
, then
z\leq y
(resp.
z\geq y
) in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
for all
y
in the image of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
, so we may assume
z\in[*_{-},*_{+}]
. Denote
[l]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}]}
,
[m]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
and by
[n]
the subinterval
[*_{-},*_{+}]
of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
, in that case we may identify
[l]
with an ordered subset of both
[m]
and
[l]
that contains the endpoints, in other words we have a cospan
[n]\overset{a_{0}}{\twoheadleftarrow}[l]\overset{a_{1}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
of active injective morphisms. We will prove that it satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

3.14
, before doing that we will demonstrate that this suffices to conclude the proof. Note that for
z\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
and
y\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/[b_{l}]}
we have
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}y
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
if and only if there exists an
w\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/d^{-}_{r}[b_{l}]}
such that
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}w\leq_{\mathbb{N}}y
and similarly for
y\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z
. It follows from this and the second claim of
Lemma

3.14
that (if the conditions of the lemma is satisfies) the order
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
coincides with the linear order on
[n+m-l]
.

Finally, we will prove that the conditions of
Lemma

3.14
are satisfied. Note that for any pair of consecutive elements
(y<y^{\prime})
for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
we have
\dim(y^{\prime})=\dim(y)\pm 1
. It follows from our previous description of the order on
[b_{l}]
that a pair of consecutive elements
(z<z^{\prime})\in[l]
remain consecutive in
[m]
unless
z\in A
and
z^{\prime}\in C
, where

A\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\coprod_{0\leq k\leq n-1}A_{k},\;A_{n-1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(d^{-}_{n-1}b_{l})_{n-1},\;A_{k}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\bigcup_{a\in A_{k+1}}(d^{\pm}_{k}a)_{k}\setminus(d^{+}_{k}(b_{l}))_{k})

and similarly

C\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\coprod_{0\leq k\leq n-2}C_{k},\;C_{n-1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(d^{+}_{n-2}b_{l})_{n-2},\;C_{k}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\bigcup_{c\in C_{k+1}}(d^{\pm}_{k}a)_{k}\setminus(d^{-}_{k}(b_{l}))_{k}).

We claim that this is only possible if
\dim(z)=n-1
and
\dim(z^{\prime})=n-2
: assume that
\dim(z)=k<n-1
, we then claim that there exists
y\in(d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}])_{n-1}
such that
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}y
. Indeed, it follows from
Remark

3.8
that
z
belongs to the boundary of some
y^{\prime}\in(d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}])_{n-1}
, assume first that
z
belongs to
d^{+}_{q}y
for some
q
, so that in particular
z\geq_{\mathbb{N}}y^{\prime}
, in that case since
z
does not belong to
d^{+}_{q}[b_{l}]
by construction there must be another element
y^{\prime}\leq_{q}y^{\prime\prime}
such that
z
appears in
d^{-}_{q}y^{\prime\prime}
. Iterating this procedure for all values of
q
gives us a basis element
y
containing
z
in its boundary and such that
z
does not appear in
d^{+}_{q}y
for any
q
, meaning that
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}y
. But then note that since
z^{\prime}\in C
it must belong to
d^{+}_{n-2}y
, meaning that
y\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z^{\prime}
, which contradicts our assumption that
z
and
z^{\prime}
are consecutive.

So assume now that we have consecutive
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z^{\prime}
with
z\in C
,
z^{\prime}\in D
,
\dim(z)=n-1
and
\dim(z^{\prime})=n-2
, to finish the proof it remain to show that they remain consecutive in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x^{\prime}}
. Assume the contrary, pick the maximal chain
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z_{p}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z^{\prime}
, then we must have
\dim(z_{p})=n-1>\dim(z^{\prime})
since otherwise it would lie in
d^{-}_{n-1}[b_{l}]
. It follows that
z
and
z_{p}
are a pair of basis elements of dimension
(n-1)
for which
d^{+}_{n-2}z\cap d^{+}_{n-2}z_{p}\neq\varnothing
(as it must contain
z^{\prime}
). We claim that this is only possible if
z_{p}\leq_{n-1}z
. Note that if this holds, then in particular we have
z\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z_{p}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}z
, where the first inequality holds by assumption and the second since
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
refines all
\leq_{q}
, hence
z=z_{p}
because the basis of
x
is strongly loop-free – a contradiction. It now remains to show that
z_{p}\leq_{n-1}z
. Since it does not belong to
d^{+}_{n-1}x^{\prime}
, there must be
y\in x^{\prime}_{n}
such that
z_{p}\in(d^{-}_{n-1}y)_{n-1}
, we claim that
z^{\prime}\geq_{n-2}y
: indeed, since
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-2}z_{p})_{n-2}
and
z_{p}\in(d^{-}_{n-1}y)_{n-1}
we must have
z^{\prime}\in(d^{-}_{n-1}y)_{n-2}
, but also
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-1}y)_{n-2}
since it belongs to
d^{+}_{n-1}x^{\prime}
by construction, which together implies that
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-2}y)_{n-2}
. Now we may choose some
z^{(0)}_{p}\in(d^{+}_{n-1}y)_{n-1}
such that
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-2}z^{(0)}_{p})_{n-2}
, applying the same reasoning to
z^{(0)}_{p}
we may construct a maximal string
z_{p}\leq_{n-1}z^{(0)}_{p}\leq_{n-1}...\leq_{n-1}z^{(s)}_{p}
such that
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-2}z^{(i)}_{p})_{n-2}
for all
i
. In that case
z^{(s)}_{p}
must lie in
(d^{+}_{n-1}x^{\prime})_{n-1}
, so that
z
and
z^{(s)}_{p}
are both the basis elements in
d^{+}_{n-1}x^{\prime}
for which
z^{\prime}\in(d^{+}_{n-2}z^{(s)}_{p})_{n-2}\cap(d^{+}_{n-2}z)_{n-2}
, which contradicts
3.5
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Given a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
and a basis element
b\in y_{l}
, the set
f^{-1}(b)
containing all basis elements
c
for which
f(c)=b
is either empty or a segment for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Assume that
f^{-1}(b)
is non-empty and take a pair of elements
(c,d)
such that
f(c)=f(d)=b
. In that case by
Lemma

3.15
we may assume
c\leq_{\mathbb{N}}d
, denote by
c\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{s}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}d
the segment containing all the elements
t
such that
c\leq_{\mathbb{N}}t\leq_{\mathbb{N}}d
, we claim that
f(c_{i})=b
for all
i
. Assume the contrary, denote
b_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Im}(f|_{[c_{i}]})
, so that
b_{i}
is the subcomplex of
y
generated by the image of
c_{i}
. Note that since
c_{i}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{i+1}
we either have
b_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}d_{l}^{-}b_{i+1}
or
b_{i+1}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}d^{+}_{l}b_{i}
for some
l
. Note also that all
b_{i}\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
, hence are also linearly ordered by
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
, denote by
m_{i}
the minima element (with respect to
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
) of
b_{i}\cap b_{i-1}
(where we set
b_{-1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}b
), then we have a string
b\leq_{\mathbb{N}}m_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}m_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}m_{s}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}b
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/y}
, this defines a loop, hence since
y
was assumed to be a strong Steiner complex this string must be trivia, meaning that
f(c_{i})=b_{i}=b
for all
i
.
∎

Proposition 3.17
.

\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
admits an active/inert factorization.

Proof.

Assume we have a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
, denote by
y^{\prime}
the subcomplex of
y
containing only the basis elements appearing in the decomposition of the images of the basis elements of
x
, then we have a natural inert morphism
y^{\prime}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}y
and
f
factors as
i\circ a
for some active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y^{\prime}
. We claim that
y^{\prime}\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
: it admits a unital basis by construction, and it is strongly loop-free since any loop for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
in
y^{\prime}
induces a loop in
y
, and hence must be trivial since
y\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
. Finally, if there is an active morphism
c_{n}\overset{a_{0}}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
, then there is an active morphism
c_{n}\overset{a\circ a_{0}}{\twoheadrightarrow}y^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see that such a factorization is also functorial in the sense that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime\prime}}

for any composable strings
x^{\prime\prime}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}x^{\prime\prime}\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
and
w\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}z\overset{a^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}z^{\prime}\overset{a^{\prime\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}z^{\prime\prime}
, where each square in the diagrams is obtained by factorization described above. To prove the claim it remains to show that the only morphisms that are both inert and active are isomorphisms, so assume that
f:x\rightarrow y
is such a morphism. Since
f
is inert it sends basis elements to basis elements and since it is active every basis element in
y
is in the image of at least one basis element of
x
. It follows that for any
b\in y_{l}
the preimage
f^{-1}(b)
is a finite set of basis elements of dimension
\dim(b)
, however by
Lemma

3.16
it must be a segment for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
, which is only possible if
f^{-1}(b)
is a singleton. Since this holds for all such
b
,
f
must be an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

Note that the conclusion of
3.17
does not hold in the bigger category
\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
: we can still define the functorial factorization
x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}y^{\prime}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}y
for any
f:x\rightarrow y
, but there could be morphisms that are both inert and active, for example the functor
F
between categories shown below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

that takes 0 to 0 and both
a
and
b
to 1 is both active and inert (naturally, the source of
F
does not belong to
\mathpzc{Stein}_{1}
).

Lemma 3.19
.

Every subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
for
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
which is a segment for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
is contractible.

Proof.

We will prove the claim by induction on the length of the segment: segments of length 1 are singletons, so the claim is trivial for them. Given a segment
b_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}b_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}b_{m}
denote by
S
the corresponding full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
. Pick some element
b_{r}
of maximal dimension
l
among
b_{i}
, so that
\dim(b_{r-1})<l=\dim(b_{r})
and
\dim(b_{r+1})<l
, denote by
S_{-}
(resp.
S_{+}
) the subcategory of
S
containing
b_{i}
with
i<r
(resp.
i>r
) and denote
S^{\prime}_{\pm}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}S_{\pm}\cap d^{\pm}_{l}[b_{r}]
, in that case
S
is isomorphic to the colimit of

(51)

so it remains to show that all the terms in (
51
) are contractible. This is obvious for
*
,
(S^{\prime}_{-})^{\triangleright}
and
(S^{\prime}_{+})^{\triangleleft}
and follows by induction on length for
S_{\pm}
, so it remains to show that
S^{\prime}_{\pm}
are contractible, however note that they define segments in
d^{\pm}_{l}[b_{r}]\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, so they are again contractible by induction.
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
is a saturated algebraic pattern in the sense of
[
7
]
.

Proof.

\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
admits a factorization system by
3.17
and the notion of elementary objects, so it suffices to prove the following claims:

(1)

denote by
\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}}(x)
the set of active morphisms
x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
, then

\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}}(x)\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}}(e);

(2)

given an active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
denote by
C
the category of pairs
(e_{0}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}x,e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}a_{!}e_{0})
, where
a_{!}e_{0}
appears in the factorization square

(52)

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.06786pt}{-20.89034pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

then the fibers of the natural morphism
m:C\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/y}
sending the pair above to the composition
e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}a_{!}e_{0}\overset{a_{!}i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}y
are contractible;

(3)

every
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
admits an active morphism
a:e\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
with source in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n}
;

(4)

for every
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
we have

x\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\mathrm{colim}}e.

We will now prove the claim in order:

(1)

Note that there is a morphism

F:\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}}(x)\rightarrow\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}}(e)

sending
x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
to the system of
i^{*}a:e\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}a_{!}e
for
i:e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
in the notation of (
52
), we need to define an inverse to
F
. Assume that we have a compatible system
a_{e}:e\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y_{e}
over all
i_{e}:e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
, then define the complex
y
such that the basis of
y_{k}
consists of pairs
(c,b)
, where
c
is a basis element of
x
corresponding to some
i:[c]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
and
b\in(y_{[c]})_{k}
, modulo an equivalence relation that identifies
(c^{\prime},b)
with
(c^{\prime\prime},i_{0}(b))
for any
i:[c^{\prime}]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[c^{\prime\prime}]
, where
i_{0}:y_{[c^{\prime}]}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}y_{[c^{\prime\prime}]}
is the morphism induced by
i
. We define the structure of an ADC on
y
by

\partial(c,b)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c,\partial b).

It is clear that this defines an ADC with unital basis, moreover it clearly admits an active morphism from
x
that sends
c
as above to
\sum_{b\in(y_{[c]})_{\dim(c)}}(c,b)
, so it remains to prove the basis is strongly loop-free. Assume now that we have a loop
(c_{0},n_{0})\leq_{\mathbb{N}}(c_{1},b_{1})\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}(c_{0},b_{0})
, then by construction
c_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}...\leq_{\mathbb{N}}c_{0}
is a loop in
x
, hence it must be trivial since
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, which means that the original loop is entirely contained within some
y_{[c]}
, so it too must be trivial since
y_{[c]}\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
.

(2)

Given
i:e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}y
corresponding to the basis element
b\in y_{l}
denote by
C_{b}
the fiber of
m
over it, by definition it consists of
e_{0}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}x
such that
i
factors through
a_{!}e_{0}
. Note that
C_{b}
is non-empty since
a
was assumed to be active, assume first that
a^{-1}=\varnothing
, this means that there is a basis element
c\in x_{k}
such that
b
belongs to the subcomplex
[a(c)]
generated by elements in the image of
c
. There exists a minimal such
c
in
x
, which defines the initial object of
C_{b}
, which proves that it is contractible. Assume now that
a^{-1}(b)
is non-empty, then
a^{-1}(b)
is coinitial in
C_{b}
, hence
|C_{b}|\cong|a^{-1}(b)|
, but the latter is a segment for
\leq_{\mathbb{N}}
by
Lemma

3.16
and those are contractible by
Lemma

3.19
.

(3)

This follows immediately from the definition since every
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
admits an active morphism
c_{n}\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
.

(4)

This can either be seen directly or deduced from the freeness property of
[
2
]
.

∎

Corollary 3.21
.

Given an active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
and
i:e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
in
\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
, denote by
a_{!}e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}y
its image under
a
, then we have an equivalence

(53)

y\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\mathrm{colim}}a_{!}e

in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
.

Proof.

It follows from (
4
) that for every
z\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
we have

(54)

z\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}z)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/z}}{\mathrm{colim}}e.

Applying this to the right-hand side of (
53
) we get

(55)

\underset{e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x}{\mathrm{colim}}\;a_{!}e\cong\underset{e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{e^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}a_{!}e}{\mathrm{colim}}\;e^{\prime}\cong\underset{e^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}a_{!}e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}y}{\mathrm{colim}}\;e^{\prime},

where the colimit on the right is taken over the category
C
of pairs
(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x,e^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}a_{!}e)
. It then follows from (
2
) that the colimit in (
55
) can be identified with
\underset{e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}y}{\mathrm{colim}}\;y
, which is isomorphic to
y
by (
4
).
∎

Corollary 3.22
.

Given an active morphism
a:\theta\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta^{\prime}
and
i:e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta
in
\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta}
, denote by
\mathrm{Im}_{a}(i)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta^{\prime}
its image under
a
, then we have an equivalence

(56)

\theta^{\prime}\cong\underset{e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Im}_{a}(i)

in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

That (
56
) is a colimit in
\Theta_{n}
follows immediately from
3.21
since
\Theta
is a full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
by
Remark

3.3
, that it remains a colimit in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
follows since the isomorphism

\theta\cong\underset{e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta}{\mathrm{colim}}\;e

is preserved by the inclusion
\Theta_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
– this is essentially a reformulation of the Segal condition.
∎

4.
An alternative model for
\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})

In
2.1
we have identified the stabilization of
\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
with the category of spectrum-valued Segal objects for a certain algebraic pattern, however later in the same section we have proved in
Theorem

2.21
that
\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\theta})
is isomorphic to the category of spectrum-valued presheaves. This fact is not exclusive to the objects of
\Theta_{n}
– in this section we will construct a category
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp})\cong\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}),

more specifically this result is contained in
4.12
.

Unfortunately, the model of
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
presented here does not allow for a direct computation of its space of objects and morphisms since it is presented as a localization
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}))
of a certain simplicial space
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
, where
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}:\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
is the left adjoint to inclusion. The reason we ultimately need this intermediate model is
Lemma

4.2
which allows us to express it in terms of
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\theta^{\prime})
for
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{n}
– the equivalent result of course holds for the category
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
defined in
Section

6
, however we do not know how to prove it directly except by constructing an isomorphism
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
.

In the first part of this section we define
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
and prove some auxiliary results culminating in the isomorphism
4.12
, the second part is dedicated to the computation of
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
for
x\in\mathrm{Stein}_{n}
in
4.17
, which will later be used in
Section

6
. Finally, we use the results obtained so far to prove the first major theorem of this paper –
Theorem

4.20
. We also note in passing that a generalization of
4.21
to torsion-free complexes has been obtained in
[
6
]
using different methods.

Construction 4.1
.

Note that for any morphism
f:[q]\rightarrow[p]
we have an induced functor
f_{*}:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{[q]}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[p]}
sending
[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to the inert part of
f\circ i
, this makes
[q]\mapsto\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}
into a functor
\Delta\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/-}}\mathrm{Cat}
. For any category
\mathscr{C}
we then get a functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/-},\mathscr{C}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
.

Given
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
the subfunctor of

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/-},\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}})}\mathrm{Cat}

sending
[q]
to the subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
such that:

(1)

for
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
denote by
\theta_{i}
the source of
F(i)\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
, then for
k\in[q]
we have
\theta_{\{k\}}\cong c_{n}
, where
[0]\overset{\{k\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
is the inclusion of the element
\{k\}
;

(2)

given any morphism
[l]\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[m]
between
i_{0}:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
and
i_{1}:[m]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
, if
i^{\prime}
preserves the minimal element, the corresponding morphism
h_{i^{\prime}}:\theta_{i_{0}}\rightarrow\theta_{i_{1}}
is inert, and if
i^{\prime}
preserves the maximal element, then the morphism
h_{i^{\prime}}
is an active morphism;

(3)

any natural transformation
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([1]\times\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
that lies in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
satisfies
\alpha_{[0]\overset{\{k\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]}\cong\mathrm{id}
for all
k\in[q]
.

Note that this is indeed a subfunctor since both inert and active morphisms are closed under composition. Finally, denote

\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})|),

where
|-|:\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is the functor of geometric realization and
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}:\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Delta)\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
is the left adjoint to the natural inclusion.

Lemma 4.2
.

We have

\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\cong\underset{\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\theta).

Proof.

Since
|-|
and
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}
preserve colimits it suffices to show that

\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\cong\underset{\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\theta).

To prove this it suffices to show that for
[m]\times\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([q])([m])
the space of factorizations

\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\times[m]\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\Theta_{n,/\theta}\xrightarrow{f_{!}}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}

of
F
for
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
is contractible, however note that it in fact has an initial object given by

\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\times[m]\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\Theta_{n,/s(F([q]=\joinrel=[q],m))}\xrightarrow{F([q]=\joinrel=[q],m)_{!}}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}},

where
s(F([q]=\joinrel=[q]))
denotes the source of the corresponding object of
\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
viewed as a morphism in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.
∎

Construction 4.3
.

We will define certain objects
C_{n}(k)\in\Theta_{n}
by induction on
n
and
k
: we set
C_{n}(0)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}c_{n}
for all
n
, for
k=n=1
we set
C_{1}(1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[3]\in\Delta
, also denote by
i_{1}(1):c_{1}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{1}(1)
the inclusion of thew subinterval
[1,2]
. Assume we have defined
i_{n-1}(1):c_{n-1}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n-1}(1)
, define
C_{n}(1)
to be the object of
\Theta_{n}
with
\mathrm{Ob}(C_{n}(1))\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{0,1,2,3\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(1)}(1,2)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}C_{n-1}(1)
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(1)}(i,i+1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}c_{n}
for
i\in\{0,2\}
, define
i_{n}(1):c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1)
to be the morphism sending the object
i
to
(i+1)
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and restricting to
i_{n-1}(1):c_{n-1}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n-1}(1)
on
c_{n-1}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{c_{n}}(0,1)
. Finally, assume we have defined
i_{n}(k-1):c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k-1)
, we define
C_{n}(k)
to be the pushout

(57)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(k-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}(k-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

taken in the category
\Theta_{n}
(which exists by
Lemma

2.10
), define
i_{n}(k)
to be the composition
i^{\prime}\circ i_{n}(1)
in the notation of (
57
).

Denote by
C^{\mathrm{el}}_{q}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n}
the morphism sending
[0]\overset{\{i\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to
c_{n}
,
[1]\overset{i<i+1}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to
C_{n}(1)
, the inclusion
[0]\overset{\{0\}}{\rightarrowtail}[1]
over
[q]
to the inert morphism
c_{n}\overset{i_{n}(1)}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1)
and
[0]\overset{\{1\}}{\rightarrowtail}[1]
to the unique active morphism
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}C_{n}(1)
. By construction there exists a left Kan extension
C^{\mathrm{int}}_{q}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}C_{q}^{\mathrm{el}}
along
i^{\mathrm{el}}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}
that sends
[l]\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to
C_{n}(l)
.

Remark 4.4
.

By untangling the definitions we see that
C_{1}(k)=[2k+1]
and inductively
C_{n}(k)
has objects given by the set
\{0,1,...2k+1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k)}(i,i+1)=C_{n-1}(i)
for
i\leq k
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k)}(i^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1)=C_{n-1}(2k+1-i)
for
i\geq k+1
. In particular, we see that the natural inclusion
c_{n}\overset{i_{n}^{k}}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k)
admits a section
s_{n}^{k}:C_{n}(k)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}c_{n}
that we also define by induction: for
n=1
we set
s_{1}^{k}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}0
if
i\leq k
and
s_{1}^{k}(i^{\prime})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}1
otherwise, for general
n
we define
s_{n}^{k}
to act by the same formula on objects and define the morphism

C_{n-1}(k)=\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k)}(k,k+1)\rightarrow c_{n-1}=\mathrm{Hom}_{c_{n}}(0,1)

to be
s^{k}_{n-1}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

|\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})|([q])
is isomorphic to the space of morphisms
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
for which the composition
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}\xrightarrow{p}\Theta_{n}
with the forgetful functor is isomorphic to
C_{q}^{\mathrm{int}}
in the notation of
4.3
.

Proof.

Denote by
X([q])
the space described in the statement of the proposition, note that there is a natural morphism
j_{q}:X([q])\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([q])
, we need to prove that
X_{q}
induces an isomorphism upon taking geometric realizations. We will in fact show that
j_{q}
is coinitial, we will do so by induction on
q
starting with the case
q=1
. The objects of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([1])
are given by cospans

(58)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with morphisms given by

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{38.83128pt}{-24.52922pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

we will prove this by induction on
n
. For
n=1
we are given a cospan
[1]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m]\overset{a}{\twoheadleftarrow}[1]
and we need to define an active morphism
a_{0}:[3]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
such that
a_{0}\circ i_{1}(1)\cong i
, clearly there is a unique such morphism given by
a_{0}(1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}i(0)
,
a_{0}(2)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}i(1)
. Assume we have constructed it in dimensions
\leq(n-1)
and we are given (
60
), we need to construct a unique morphism
C_{n}(1)\overset{a_{0}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta
such that
a_{0}\circ i_{n}(1)\cong i
. Suppose that
\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)=\{0,1,...,m\}
and denote
\theta^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i(0),i(1))\in\Theta_{n-1}
, note that
i
induces an inert morphism
i^{\prime}:c_{n-1}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta^{\prime}
. Using
a_{0}\circ i_{n}(1)\cong i
and the fact that
a_{0}
is active we see that

\displaystyle a_{0}(0)=0,

\displaystyle a_{0}(1)=i(0),

\displaystyle a_{0}(2)=i(1),

\displaystyle a_{0}(3)=m,

the morphisms
c_{n-1}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(1)}(0,1)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(0,i(0))
and
c_{n-1}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(1)}(2,3)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i(1),m)
are the unique active morphisms and
C_{n-1}(1)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(1)}(1,2)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i(0),i(1))
is the unique morphism corresponding to the cospan
c_{n-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta^{\prime}\overset{a}{\twoheadleftarrow}c_{n-1}
, we see that these conditions uniquely define
a_{0}
.

Now, assume we have proved the claim for
(q-1)
and we are given a morphism
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
, by induction we may assume that we have unique morphisms
\alpha_{i}:C_{n}(l)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}F([l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])
for all
i\neq\mathrm{id}_{[q]}
defining a natural transformation between functors from
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{[q]}\}
, we need to define a morphism
\alpha_{\mathrm{id}}:C_{n}(q)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}F(\mathrm{id}_{[q]})
. Denote
\theta_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F([q-1]\overset{\delta_{q}}{\rightarrowtail}[q])
,
\theta_{1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F([1]\overset{q-1<1}{\rightarrowtail}[q])
and
\theta\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F(\mathrm{id}_{[q]})
so that we have a commutative square

we can then uniquely fill in the dashed arrow by the universal property of the pushout (

).
