Notation 4.6
.

Denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
the functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
described in
4.5
, so that we have
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}))
.

Construction 4.7
.

We will define a functor
(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})\mapsto C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})
from
\Delta^{n}
to
\Theta_{n}
, we will do so by induction on
n
: for
n=1
we define
C_{1}(k)
to be the object
C_{1}(k)\cong[2k+1]\in\Delta
of
4.3
, given a morphism
f:[k]\rightarrow[l]
we set
C_{1}(f)(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}f(i)
for
i\leq k
and
C_{1}(f)(i^{\prime})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}2l+1-f(i^{\prime})
for
i^{\prime}\geq k+1
. Note that
C_{1}(0)=c_{1}
.

Assume we have defined
C_{n-1}(k_{0},...,k_{n-2})
and moreover that
C_{n-1}(0,...,0)=c_{n-1}
, we first describe the value of
C_{n}(-)
on objects: define
C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})
to be the category with objects
\{0,1,...,2k_{0}+1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})}(i,i+1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}c_{n}
if
i\neq k_{0}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})}(k,k+1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}C_{n-1}(k_{1},...,k_{n-1})
. Given a morphism
f=(f_{0},...,f_{n-1})
such that
f_{i}:[k_{i}]\rightarrow[l_{i}]
we define
C_{n}(f)
on objects by
C_{n}(f)(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}f_{0}(i)
for
i\leq k
and
C_{n}(f)(i^{\prime})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}2l+1-f_{0}(i^{\prime})
for
i^{\prime}\geq k+1
, on morphism
(n-1)
-categories we define the morphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})}(i,i+1)=c_{n}\rightarrow\prod_{f_{0}(i)<j\leq f_{0}(i+1)}c_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(l_{0},...,l_{n-1})}(f(i),f(i+1))

for
i\neq k
to be the diagonal morphism (where the indexing set for the product might be empty) and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{n-1})}(i,i+1)=C_{n-1}(k_{1},...,k_{n-1})\rightarrow

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\prod_{f_{0}(k)<j\leq l}c_{n}\times C_{n-1}(l_{1},...,l_{n-1})\times\prod_{f_{0}(k)<j\leq l}c_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(l_{0},...,l_{n-1})}(f(k),f(k+1))

to have components
C_{n-1}(f_{1},...,f_{n-1}):C_{n-1}(k_{1},...,k_{n-1})\rightarrow C_{n-1}(l_{1},...,l_{n-1})
and

C_{n-1}(s,...,s):C_{n-1}(k_{1},...,k_{n-1})\rightarrow C_{n-1}(0,...,0)=c_{n-1}

(using the inductive assumption on
C_{n-1}(0,...,0)
), where
s:[k_{i}]\rightarrow[0]
is the unique morphism. The functoriality of this construction easily follows from the functoriality of
C_{n-1}(f_{1},...,f_{n-1})
. Finally, note that by construction
C_{n}(0,...,0)
is the category with objects
\{0,1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(0,...,0)}(0,1)=c_{n-1}
, i.e.
C_{n}(0,...,0)=c_{n}
, meaning that all the inductive assumptions are verified for
n
.

Finally, we will define certain morphisms
S_{n}^{i}(k):C_{n}(k)\rightarrow C_{n}^{i}(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}C_{n}(0,...,k,...,0)
, where on the right the only nontrivial value
k
is in position
i
for
0\leq i\leq n-1
. We prove the claim by induction on
n
: for the case
n=1
we define
S_{1}^{0}(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{[2k+1]}
. Assume we have defined the required morphism up to dimension
(n-1)
: if
i=0
then it is easy to see from the definitions that
C^{0}_{n}(k)
is a category with objects
\{0,1,...,2k+1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C^{0}_{n}(k)}(i,i+1)=c_{n-1}
, using the explicit description of
C_{n}(1)
in
Remark

4.4
we define
S_{n}^{0}(1)
to be identity on objects and restrict to
s^{j}_{n-1}:C_{n-1}(j)\rightarrow c_{n-1}
on morphism categories, where the morphism
s^{j}_{n-1}
is also defined in
Remark

4.4
. For general
i>0
the category
C_{n}^{i}(k)
has object
\{0,1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}^{i}(k)}(0,1)=C_{n-1}^{i-1}(k)
and we define
S_{n}^{i}(k)
to sends objects
j\leq k
to 0,
j\geq(k+1)
to 1 and to restrict to act by

\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}(k)}(k,k+1)\cong C_{n-1}(k)\xrightarrow{S^{i-1}_{n-1}(k)}C^{i-1}_{n-1}(k)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{C_{n}^{i}(k)}(k,k+1)

on the morphism category.

Finally, we can define
C_{q}^{\mathrm{int},i}:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n}
by sending
[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to
C_{n}^{i}(l)
and a morphism
[l]\overset{j^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[m]
over
[q]
to the induced morphism
C_{n}^{i}(j^{\prime}):C_{n}^{i}(l)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}^{i}(m)
. It is easy to see with these notations that the morphisms
S_{n}^{i}(k)
defined above define a natural transformation
S^{i}_{n}:C_{q}^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow C_{q}^{\mathrm{int},i}
of functors in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\Theta_{n})
. Denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{i}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
the functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
[q]
to the space of functors
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
for which the composition
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}\xrightarrow{p}\Theta_{n}
with the forgetful functor is isomorphic to
C_{q}^{\mathrm{int},i}
, then precomposing with
S^{i}_{n}
defines a natural transformation
\mathrm{TwAr}^{i}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
, we will often implicitly use it to identify
\mathrm{TwAr}^{i}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
with a simplicial subspace of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume we have a collection
(k_{0},...,k_{l},0,...,0)
of non-negative integers such that
k_{j}=0
for
j>l
and a collection
(0,...,0,h_{l},...,h_{n-1})
such that
h_{i}=0
for
i<l
, then we have a pushout diagram

(61)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}\cong C_{n}(0,...,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(0,...,h_{l},...,h_{n-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{l},...,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}(k_{0},...,k_{l-1},k_{l}+h_{l},h_{l+1},...,h_{n-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}(i^{\prime}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}(i_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}(i^{\prime}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}(i_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
, where
i_{0}
and
i^{\prime}_{0}
correspond to the unique collections of inert maximal-element-preserving morphisms while
i_{1}
and
i^{\prime}_{1}
to the collections of inert minimal-element-preserving morphisms.

Proof.

Denote by
F_{1}:\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C(k_{0},...,0)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
the functor sending
c_{s}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}C(k_{0},...,0)
to
c_{s}
, by
F_{0}:\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C(k_{0},...,0)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
the functor sending all
c_{s}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
to
\varnothing
except for
c_{n}\overset{C_{n}(i_{0})}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
which it sends to
c_{n}
and by
F^{\prime}_{1}:\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C(k_{0},...,0)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
the functor sending all
c_{s}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
to
\varnothing
except for
c_{n}\overset{C_{n}(i_{0})}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
which it sends to
C_{n}(0,...,h_{n-1})
. In that case we have a cospan
F_{1}\xleftarrow{f_{0}}F_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}F_{1}^{\prime}
of natural transformations, where the only non-trivial component of
f_{0}
is
C_{n}(i_{0})
and the only non-trivial component of
f_{1}
is
C_{n}(i^{\prime}_{1})
, moreover it is easy to see that upon taking colimits this cospan induces the upper left corner of (
61
). Using the commutativity of colimits, we see that the pushout of (
61
) is given by

\underset{(c_{s}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0))\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)}}{\mathrm{colim}}G(i),

where
G:\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
is the functor sending all
c_{s}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
to
c_{s}
except for
c_{n}\overset{C_{n}(i_{0})}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(k_{0},...,0)
which it sends to
C_{n}(0,...,h_{n-1})
. However by direct inspection we see that
G
coincides with the functor
i\mapsto\mathrm{Im}_{C_{n}(i_{1})}(i)
, so the claim now follows from
3.22
.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

The simplicial spaces
\mathrm{TwAr}^{i}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
for
0\leq i<n
described in
4.7
satisfy the Segal condition.

Proof.

By construction we may identify
\mathrm{TwAr}^{i}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([q])
with the space of natural transformations
\alpha:F_{0}\rightarrow F_{1}
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n})
, where
F_{0}
is the functor sending
[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
to
C_{n}^{i}(l)
and
F_{1}
is the constant functor with value
\mathscr{E}
. It follows from
Lemma

4.8
that both of those functors are left Kan extensions of their restriction along
i^{\mathrm{el}}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}
, so we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n})}(F_{0},F_{1})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n})}(i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime}_{0},i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime}_{1})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n})}(F^{\prime}_{0},F^{\prime}_{1}),

where
F^{\prime}_{i}
denotes the restriction of
F_{i}
along
i^{\mathrm{el}}
. The claim now follows since

(62)

\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[e]}.

∎

Remark 4.10
.

Given
(\mathscr{E}:\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
we can consider a category
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{Cat}}
given by pullback along
P:\Delta\rightarrow\Theta_{n}
which sends
[n]
to
P([n])
such that
\mathrm{Ob}(P([n]))=\{0,1,...,n\}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{P([n])}(i,i+1)=c_{n-1}
. More explicitly,
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{Cat}}
can be described as the category with the same space of objects as
\mathscr{E}
and with morphisms given by
n
-morphisms of
\mathscr{E}
with composition given by 0-composition of
n
-morphisms. With this notation it is easy to see that
\mathrm{TwAr}^{0}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{Cat}})
, where on the right
\mathrm{TwAr}(-)
denotes the ordinary twisted arrows category. More generally, given
f:c_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
and
0<l<n
we can view
f
as an
(n-l)
-morphism in an
(n-l)
-category

\mathscr{E}(d^{-}_{l-1}f,d^{+}_{l-1}f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(d^{-}_{l-1}f,d^{+}_{l-1}f),

then
\mathrm{TwAr}^{l}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
is the category with objects given by
n
-morphisms
f:c_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
and morphisms given by morphisms in
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}(d^{-}_{l-1}f,d^{+}_{l-1}f)_{\mathrm{Cat}})
(so that the space of morphisms between
f
and
g
is non-empty only if
d^{\pm}_{l-1}f\cong d^{\pm}_{l-1}g
).

Lemma 4.11
.

For
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
we have

(63)

\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\theta_{0})\cong\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta_{0}}.

Proof.

We first note that
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\theta_{0})
satisfies the Segal condition – this follows by the same argument as in the proof of
4.9
:
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\theta_{0})([q])
is the space of natural transformations
\alpha:F_{0}\rightarrow F_{1}
between functors
F_{i}:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n}
, where
F_{1}
is the constant functor with value
\theta_{0}
and
F_{0}:([l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\mapsto C_{n}(l)
, note that we have
F_{i}\cong i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}i^{\mathrm{el},*}F_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
(where the left Kan extension is taken with respect to the category
\Theta_{n}
), so the claim again follows by (
62
). To complete the proof it now suffices to show that the spaces of morphisms in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\theta_{0})
are contractible, i.e. that for any
i^{\prime}:\theta^{\prime}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{0}
and
i^{\prime\prime}:\theta^{\prime\prime}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{0}
such that
i^{\prime}
factors through
i^{\prime\prime}
there is a unique diagram of the form

\hbox to197.5pt{\vbox to75.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 98.74673pt\lower-37.46982pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Proposition 4.12
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp})\cong\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}).

Proof.

Denote by
F:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
the functor
\mathscr{E}\mapsto\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
and by
G:\mathrm{Cat}_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
the functor
\mathscr{E}\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\mathrm{Sp})
, it follows from
Lemma

2.3
for
F
and
Lemma

4.2
for
G
that both of those functors are the right Kan extensions of their restriction to
\Theta_{n}
, so it suffices to construct a natural transformation
\beta:G|_{\Theta_{n}}\rightarrow F|_{\Theta_{n}}
. It follows from
Lemma

4.11
that the functor
G|_{\theta}
is equivalent to the functor
\theta\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta},\mathrm{Sp})
and from
2.9
that
F|_{\theta}
is isomorphic to the functor
\theta\mapsto\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
, so it suffices to extend the isomorphism

(64)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta},\mathrm{Sp})\cong\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})

of
2.20
to a natural transformation of functors. Denote by
\beta_{\theta}:\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
the functor sending
j:\theta_{j}\rightarrow\theta
to
\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i)
and a morphism
g:\theta_{j}\rightarrow\theta_{t}
over
\theta
to the morphism
\beta(\theta_{t})\rightarrow\beta(\theta_{j})
induced by morphisms
\mathrm{Im}(i_{0}):\mathrm{Im}(t\circ i^{\prime})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Im}(t\circ g\circ i)=\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i)
indexed by diagrams

Denote
\beta_{\theta}^{\mathrm{Sp}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Sigma^{\infty}\beta
, then the isomorphism (
64
) is induced by the functor

\beta_{\theta}^{\mathrm{Sp},*}:\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})

sending
\mathscr{F}:\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
to
\mathscr{F}(\beta_{\theta}^{\mathrm{Sp}}(\theta_{j})):\Theta^{\mathrm{inj},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
. Note that the functor
F|_{\Theta_{n}}
is the stabilization of the functor
F_{0}:\Theta^{\mathrm{op}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
given by
F_{0}(\theta)\cong\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
and similarly
G|_{\Theta_{n}}
is the stabilization of
G_{0}:\Theta^{\mathrm{op}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
given by
G_{0}(\theta)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
, so it suffices to show that
\beta^{*}_{\theta}:G_{0}(\theta)\rightarrow F_{0}(\theta)
defines a natural transformation of functors. Denote by
F^{\prime}_{0}:\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
the functor
\theta\mapsto\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta})
, then
F_{0}
is a subfunctor of
F^{\prime}_{0}
, so it suffices to show that
\beta^{*}_{\theta}
defines a natural transformation
G_{0}\rightarrow F^{\prime}_{0}
. Note that all the morphisms
\beta^{*}_{\theta}
as well as all the functors
F^{\prime}_{0}(f)
and
G_{0}(f)
for
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
admits left adjoints, denote by
F_{0}^{{}^{\prime},L}
and
G_{0}^{L}
the functors
\Theta_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
the functors obtained by taking
\theta
to
F_{0}^{\prime}(\theta)
and
G_{0}(\theta)
respectively and a morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
to the left adjoints
F^{\prime}_{0}(f)^{L}
and
G_{0}(f)^{L}
respectively and by
\beta_{\theta,!}
the left adjoints of
\beta^{*}_{\theta}
, it suffices to prove that
\beta_{\theta,!}
defines a natural transformation between
F_{0}^{{}^{\prime},L}
and
G_{0}^{L}
. Finally, note that all the categories
\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{n,/\theta}
and
\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta}
are discrete and all the objects
\beta_{\theta}(\theta_{j})\in\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathscr{S}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
factor through the discrete subcategory
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Set}}(\Theta^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}}_{n,/\theta})
, so in order to prove that
\beta_{\theta,!}
defines a natural transformation it suffices to prove that for any morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
we have a commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In other words, we need to show that for
\theta_{j}\xrightarrow{j}\theta
we have

\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}((f\circ j)\circ i)\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{j})\in\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/\theta_{j}}}{\mathrm{colim}}f_{!}\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i),

which follows since
f_{!}\mathrm{Im}(j\circ i)\cong\mathrm{Im}(f\circ j\circ i)
by construction of
f_{!}
.
∎

The rest of this section will be dedicated to the proof of
4.17
, before that we will need some auxiliary results and constructions.

Construction 4.13
.

Assume we have a string
S=l_{i_{N}}l_{i_{N-1}}...l_{i_{1}}
containing terms
l_{i}
with
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Denote by
m_{q}
the total number of terms
l_{q}
for
0\leq q\leq n-1
in
S
, for
1\leq t\leq m_{q}
denote by
1\leq s_{t}^{q}\leq N
the index such that
i_{s^{q}_{t}}=q
and there are
(t-1)
terms
l_{q}
among
l_{i_{k}}
with
k<s^{q}_{t}
and set
s_{0}^{q}=0
, for
0\leq k\leq n-1
denote by
p_{q}^{k}(t)
the number of
i_{s}=k
with
s\leq s_{t}^{q}
(so that
p_{q}^{q}(t)=t
). Finally, define
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
to be the full subcategory of
\prod_{q=0}^{n-1}[m_{q}]
containing
(x_{0},...,x_{n-1})
such that for all
k
we have

x_{k}\leq\max_{q\leq k}(p_{q}^{k}(x_{q})).

For
0\leq i\leq(n-1)
Call a substring of
S
an
i
-segment if it only contains the term
l_{i}
and is maximal by inclusion. Denote by
\widehat{S}
the string obtained from
S
by replacing every
i
-segment for every
i
as above with a single appearance of the term
l_{i}
, denote by
\widehat{m}_{i}
the number of terms
l_{i}
in
\widehat{S}
. Note that we have an active morphism
a_{i}:[\widehat{m}_{i}]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m_{i}]
which sends
k\in[\widehat{m}_{i}]
to the maximal index of the term
l_{i}
appearing in the
k
th segment, taken together they define a morphism
a:\prod_{i}[\widehat{m}_{i}]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\prod_{i}[m_{i}]
, it is easy to see that
a
takes
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
to
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. Finally, for some subset
\Sigma\in\{0,1,...,n-1\}
we define a
\Sigma
-quadrant
of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
to be the subcategory of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
containing
y\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(y_{0},...,y_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Sq}(S)
such that for some
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(x_{0},...,x_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
we have
y_{k}=a_{k}(x_{k})
for
k\notin\Sigma
and
a_{s}(x_{s})\leq y_{s}\leq a_{s}(x_{s}+1)
for
s\in\Sigma
. We will denote by
\mathrm{Sq}_{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
the simplicial subset (which is generally not a category) containing simplices
[m]\xrightarrow{f}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
whose image lies entirely in some quadrant. Finally, denote by
[N]^{0}
the
spine
of
[N]
, i.e. the simplicial subset containing all 0-simplices and the 1-simplices of the form
(i<i+1)
, then there is a canonical morphism
i_{S}:[N]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
that factors through
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
taking
k\leq N
to
(i_{0}^{k}(S),i_{1}^{k}(S),...,i_{n-1}^{k}(S))
, where
i_{j}^{k}(S)
denotes the number of terms
l_{i_{s}}
with
i_{s}=j
and
s\leq k
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Given a string
S=l_{i^{S}_{N}}l_{i^{S}_{N-1}}...l_{i^{S}_{1}}
as in
4.13
denote by
P
the set of injective morphisms
[N]\xrightarrow{j}\prod_{q=0}^{n-1}[m_{q}]
such that
j(0)=(0,...,0)
and
j(N)=(m_{0},...,m_{n-1})
, then there is a partial order on
P
such that
j
factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\hookrightarrow\prod_{q=0}^{n-1}[m_{q}]
if and only if
j\leq i_{S}
.

Proof.

Note that the set
P
can be identified with the set of strings
S^{\prime}=l_{i^{S^{\prime}}_{N}}...l_{i^{S^{\prime}}_{0}}
with
0\leq i^{\prime}_{k}\leq n-1
, where to such a string corresponds a morphism
[N]\rightarrow\prod_{q=0}^{n-1}[m_{q}]
sending
k
to
(i_{0}^{k}(S^{\prime}),...,i^{k}_{n-1}(S^{\prime}))
, where
i_{j}^{k}(S)
denotes the number of terms
l_{i^{S^{\prime}}_{s}}
with
i^{S^{\prime}}_{s}=j
and
s\leq k
. Assume we have
k\in[N]
such that
p=i^{S^{\prime}}_{k+1}<i^{S^{\prime}}_{k}=q
, define
\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}
to be the string such that
i^{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}_{u}=i^{S^{\prime}}_{u}
for
u\notin\{k,k+1\}
,
i^{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}_{k+1}=q
and
i^{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}_{k}=p
, we declare
\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}<^{0}_{P}S^{\prime}
and in general define
<_{P}
to be the transitive closure of
<_{P}^{0}
, we claim that this satisfies the conditions of the lemma.

Assume that
S^{\prime}<_{P}S
, we need to prove that the corresponding morphism
j_{S}:[N]\rightarrow\prod_{q=0}^{n-1}[m_{q}]
factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. It follows from the definition of
<_{P}
that it suffices to prove the following claim: assume
S^{\prime}=l_{i^{S^{\prime}}_{N}}...l_{i^{S^{\prime}}_{1}}
is such that
j_{S^{\prime}}
factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
and
k\in[N]
is such that
p=i^{S^{\prime}}_{k+1}<i^{S^{\prime}}_{k}=q
, then
j_{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}
also factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. To prove this we will first introduce some notation: for
u\in[n-1]
and
t\in[m_{u}]
denote by
\tau^{u}_{\leq t}S
the maximal substring
S_{0}
of
S
of the form
l_{i^{S}_{v}}l_{i^{S}_{v-1}}...l_{i^{S}_{0}}
containing
\leq t
terms
l_{u}
, then it is easy to see that
(x_{0},...,x_{n-1})
belongs to
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
if and only if for every
u
as above and all
s>u
the string
\tau_{\leq x_{u}}^{u}S
contains
\geq x_{s}
terms
l_{s}
. It follows easily from the definitions that for
x\in[N]
we have
j_{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}(x)=j_{S^{\prime}}(x)
for
x<k
, for
x=k
we have

j_{S^{\prime}}(k)_{s}=\begin{cases}j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{s}\text{ if $s\notin\{p,q\}$}\\
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{p}\text{ if $s=p$}\\
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{q}+1\text{ if $s=q$}\end{cases},j_{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}(k)_{s}=\begin{cases}j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{s}\text{ if $s\notin\{p,q\}$}\\
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{p}+1\text{ if $s=p$}\\
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{q}\text{ if $s=q$}\end{cases}

and for
x>k
we again have
j_{S^{\prime}}(x)=j_{\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}}(x)
. We need to show that
(x_{0},...,x_{n-1})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{0},...,j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{p}+1,...,j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{n-1})
lies in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, note that the conditions on the coordinates
x_{s}
with
s>p
are vacuous since for
t\geq s
the coordinates
x_{s}
coincide with
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{s}
and
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, for
s=p
we see that
\tau^{p}_{\leq j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{p}}S
contains at least
x_{t}
instances of
l_{t}
for
t>p
since
j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, hence so does
\tau^{p}_{\leq j_{S^{\prime}}(k-1)_{p}+1}S
, and for
s<p
we see that
\tau^{s}_{\leq x_{s}}S^{\prime}
contains at least
x_{t}
instances of
l_{t}
for
t>s
since
x_{s}=j_{S^{\prime}}(k+1)_{s}
,
j_{S^{\prime}}(k+1)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S)
and
j_{S^{\prime}}(k+1)_{t}\geq x_{t}
, which concludes the proof of the claim.

Now, assume that
S^{\prime}
is such that
j_{S^{\prime}}
factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, we need to prove that
S^{\prime}\leq_{P}S
, for that we will construct a sequence of strings
S^{\prime}_{l}
for
l\in[N]
such that
S^{\prime}_{l}\leq_{p}S
,
j_{S^{\prime}}
factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
and the first
l
terms of
S^{\prime}_{l}
and
S^{\prime}
coincide (so that
S^{\prime}_{N}=S^{\prime}
), starting with
S^{\prime}_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}S
. Assume we have constructed
S^{\prime}_{l-1}
, we first claim that
p=i^{S^{\prime}}_{l}\leq i^{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}_{l}=q
: indeed, if we have
q<p
then since
j_{S^{\prime}}(l)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
that would mean (since
j_{S^{\prime}}(l-1)_{q}=j_{S^{\prime}}(l)_{q}
) that
\tau^{q}_{\leq j_{S^{\prime}}(l-1)_{q}}S_{l-1}^{\prime}
contains at least
j_{S^{\prime}}(l)_{p}=j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}+1
terms
l_{p}
, however by construction
\tau^{q}_{\leq j_{S^{\prime}}(l-1)_{q}}S_{l-1}^{\prime}=\tau^{q}_{\leq j_{S_{l-1}^{\prime}}(l-1)_{q}}S_{l-1}^{\prime}
consists of the first
(l-1)
terms of
S^{\prime}_{l-1}
and contains exactly
j_{S^{\prime}}(l-1)_{p}=j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}
terms
l_{p}
. Denote by
x>l
the first index for which
i^{S^{\prime}_{l}}_{x}=p
, we claim that for all
l<y<x
we have
i^{S^{\prime}_{l}}_{y}>p
. Indeed, we cannot have
i^{S^{\prime}_{l}}_{y}=p
by definition, so assume that some
i^{S^{\prime}_{l}}_{y}=z<p
, then it follows that
\tau^{z}_{\leq j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{z}}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
contains exactly
j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}
terms
l_{p}
, however since
j_{S^{\prime}}(l)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
,
j_{S^{\prime}}(l)_{z}=j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{z}
and
z<p
it must contain at least
j_{S^{\prime}}(l)_{p}=j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}+1
terms
l_{p}
. It follows that we can define
S^{\prime}_{l}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sigma_{l}\sigma_{l+1}...\sigma_{x}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
, so that
S^{\prime}_{l}\leq_{P}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
and
S^{\prime}_{l}
coincides with
S^{\prime}
up to the
l
th term, it remains to show that
j_{S^{\prime}}
also factors through
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
. Observe that it suffices to prove that
j_{S^{\prime}}(k)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
for
k>l
since
j_{S^{\prime}}(k\leq l)
lie in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}_{l})
by construction. Note that for
z<p
the substrings
\tau^{z}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l}
contain the same number of terms
l_{i}
as
\tau^{z}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
for all
i
by construction, for
z>p
the substrings
\tau^{z}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l}
contain the same number of terms
l_{i^{\prime}}
as
\tau^{z}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
for
i^{\prime}>z
(since
z>p
), and for
z=p
we see that
\tau^{p}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l}
contain the same number of terms
l_{i}
as
\tau^{p}_{\leq t}S^{\prime}_{l-1}
for all
i
and
t>j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}
. The claim follows immediately from these observations since for all
j_{S^{\prime}}(k>l)
we have
j_{S^{\prime}}(k)_{p}\geq j_{S^{\prime}}(l)_{p}>j_{S^{\prime}_{l-1}}(l-1)_{p}
.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Assume we have a string
S=l_{i_{N}}l_{i_{N-1}}...l_{i_{1}}
as in
4.13
and a morphism
F:[N]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
such that the 1-simplex
F(j<j+1)
factors through
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{i_{j+1}}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
of
4.7
, then we can extend
F
to a morphism
\widetilde{F}:\mathrm{Sq}_{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
such that
\widetilde{F}\circ i_{S}\cong F
.

Proof.

Assume first that
S
has the form
l^{q_{0}}_{k_{0}}l^{q_{1}}_{k_{1}}...l^{q_{m}}_{k_{m}}
for some
0\leq k_{0}<k_{1}<...<k_{m}\leq n-1
and
i_{j}>0
, in that case we need to extend
F:[\sum q_{j}]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
to
\widetilde{F}:\prod_{0\leq j\leq m}[q_{j}]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\mathscr{E})
. By possibly reindexing the terms we may assume that
k_{i}=i
, denote
N\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=0}^{m}q_{j}
and for
0\leq i\leq m
denote
s_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=i}^{m}q_{j}
and set
s_{m+1}=0
, so that we have
0=s_{m+1}<s_{m}<...<s_{0}=N
. First, we will define a morphism
F_{0}:[m]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\mathscr{E})
extending
F
: note that we can identify
F
with a natural transformation
\alpha:F^{\prime}\rightarrow F^{\prime\prime}
between functors
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[N]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
, where
F^{\prime\prime}
is the constant functor with value
\mathscr{E}
and
F^{\prime}
sends
[0]\overset{\{i\}}{\rightarrowtail}[n]
to
c_{n}\cong C_{n}(0,...,0)
and
(v<v+1)
for
0\leq v<N
to
C_{n}^{i(v)}(1)
in the notation of
4.7
, where
i(v)
denotes the index
0\leq k\leq m
such that
s_{k+1}\leq v<v+1\leq s_{k}
. By
Lemma

4.8
both
F^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime}
admit left Kan extensions
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime}
and
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime\prime}
to
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[N]}
such that
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime\prime}
is still constant, while
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime}
sends
[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
to
C_{n}(j_{0},...,j_{m},0,...,0)
, where
j_{k}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\max(0,|i([l])\bigcap[s_{k-1},s_{k}]|-1)
. It follows that
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}\alpha
defines an object of
|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})|([N])\cong\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([N])
. Denote by
\alpha_{\mathrm{id}}
the component
C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},0,...,0)\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
, by construction we see that the components
\alpha_{i}:C_{n}(j_{0},...,j_{m},...,0)\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
for
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
as above are given by
C_{n}(j_{0},...,j_{m},...,0)\xrightarrow{C(u)}C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathrm{id}}}\mathscr{E}
, where
u
has components
u_{k}:i([l])\bigcap[s_{k+1},s_{k}]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[s_{k+1},s_{k}]
.

Now assume that we have an injective morphism
f:[N]\rightarrow\prod_{j=0}^{m}[i_{j}]
, note that those correspond to strings
S_{\sigma}=l_{i_{N}}...l_{i_{1}}
with
0\leq i_{k}\leq m
where the total number of terms
l_{j}
equals
q_{j}
. Given an inert morphism
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
and
0\leq x\leq m
, denote by
j^{0}_{x}(i)
(resp.
j^{1}_{x}(i)
) the minimal (resp. maximal)
t
such that
s_{t}^{x}
lies in the image of
i
. We need to construct a natural transformation
\alpha_{\sigma}:F^{\prime}_{\sigma}\rightarrow i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime\prime}
of functors from
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[N]}
to
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
such that
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
lands in the full subcategory
\Theta_{n}
and
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime\prime}
is the constant functor with value
\mathscr{E}
as above. Denote by
F_{0}:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[N]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n}
the constant functor with value
C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)
, we will define
\alpha_{\sigma}
to be given by the composition

F^{\prime}_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}F_{0}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathrm{id}}}i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}F^{\prime\prime},

where
\alpha_{\mathrm{id}}:C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is defined above. Given
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
we define
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}(i)
to be the left Kan extension of
F_{\sigma}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[N]}
sending
i_{0}:[e]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
for
e\in\{0,1\}
to
C_{n}(j^{1}_{0}(i_{0})-j^{0}_{0}(i_{0}),...,j^{1}_{m}(i_{0})-j^{0}_{m}(i_{0}),0,...,0)
: it follows from
Lemma

4.8
that this left Kan extension exists and is given by the functor sending
[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[N]
to
C_{n}(j^{1}_{0}(i)-j^{0}_{0}(i),...,j^{1}_{m}(i)-j^{0}_{m}(i),0,...,0)
. Finally, to define the natural transformation
\alpha^{\prime}
as above it suffices (since
F_{0}
is constant) to define its component
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{id}_{[N]}}
, note that
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\mathrm{id}_{[N]})=C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)=F_{0}(\mathrm{id}_{[N]})
, so we define
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{id}_{[N]}}
to be the identity morphism.

Assume that we are now given a morphism
f:[m]\rightarrow[1]^{n}
, to define
\widetilde{F}(f)
pick some
f_{\sigma}:[N]\rightarrow\prod[q_{j}]
such that
f
factors as
[m]\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}[n]\xrightarrow{f_{\sigma}}[1]^{n}
and define
\widetilde{F}(f):\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
to be the composition

\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}_{*}}\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[N]}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{F}(f_{\sigma})}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}.

We now need to prove that this is correctly defined, i.e. that it does not depend on
f_{\sigma}
, for this note that by definition all
\widetilde{F}(f_{\sigma})
factor through
\Theta_{n,/C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathrm{id},!}}\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
, so we may assume
\mathscr{E}\cong C_{n}(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)\in\Theta_{n}
, in which case the claim follows from
Lemma

4.11
since a morphism in
\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/C(i_{0},...,i_{m},...,0)}
is uniquely determined by its endpoints.

Assume now that we have a morphism
j^{\prime}:[m]\rightarrow[q_{j}]
taking
(i<i+1)
to a morphism of the form
(a_{0},...,a_{i},...,a_{m})<(a_{0},...,a_{i}+b_{i},...,a_{m})
. Factor it as
[n]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[N^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{j}\prod[q_{j}]
, where
j
is an injective morphism such that
\sum_{i=0}^{m}(j_{i}(i+1)-j_{i}(i))=1
,
N^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum b_{i}
and
b(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{i}b_{i}
, it is clear that such a factorization is unique, then the composition
F^{\prime}:[N^{\prime}]^{0}\hookrightarrow[N^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{j}\prod[q_{i}]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{F}}\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
corresponds to the string
S^{\prime}=l_{0}^{q^{\prime}_{0}}...l_{m}^{q^{\prime}_{m}}
of the same form as
S
, hence by our previous construction it defines a morphism
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}:\prod[q^{\prime}_{i}]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\mathscr{E})
. Using the construction of
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
and the fact that
F^{\prime}
factors through
\prod[q^{\prime}_{i}]
we see that
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the composition
\prod[q^{\prime}_{i}]\rightarrow\prod[q_{i}]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{F}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{C}(\mathscr{E})
.

Assume now that we have an arbitrary string
S=l_{i_{N}}...l_{i_{0}}
, we can subdivide as
S=S_{k}S_{k-1}...S_{1}
such that
S_{j}=l_{k^{j}_{0}}^{q^{j}_{0}}...l_{k^{j}_{m_{j}}}^{q_{m_{j}}^{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq k
is of the form considered at the start of the proof and is a maximal by inclusion substring of
S
with that property, then we can extend
F:[N]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
to

\widetilde{F}:X_{1}(S)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\prod[q^{1}_{i}])\coprod_{[0]}(\prod[q^{2}_{i}])\coprod_{[0]}...\coprod_{[0]}(\prod[q^{k}_{i}])\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}).

Note that every term
\prod[q^{k}_{i}]
above is in the image of a quadrant in
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
, so
X_{1}(S)
is a simplicial subset of
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
, moreover note that an injective morphism
[N]\xrightarrow{j}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
corresponding to a string
S^{\prime}
factors through
X_{1}(S)
if and only if
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{k}S^{\prime}_{k-1}...S^{\prime}_{1}
for some strings
S^{\prime}_{j}
such that
S^{\prime}_{j}<_{P}S_{j}
in the notation of
Lemma

4.14
. Given any such string
S^{\prime}
we can apply the same construction to it in order to extend
\widetilde{F}
to
X_{1}(S^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
, note that if we have another
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
X_{1}(S^{\prime})\bigcap X_{1}(S^{\prime\prime})\neq\varnothing
, then by the observations from the previous paragraph the extensions
\widetilde{F}|_{X_{1}(S^{\prime})}
and
\widetilde{F}|_{X_{1}(S^{\prime\prime})}
agree on the intersection, so we can extend
\widetilde{F}
to

X_{2}(S)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigcup_{j:[N]^{0}\xrightarrow{}{X_{1}(S)}}X_{1}(S_{j})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S),

where
S_{j}
denotes the string corresponding to the morphism
j
, iterating this construction we may extend
\widetilde{F}
to
X_{m}(S)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigcup_{j:[N]^{0}\xrightarrow{}{X_{m-1}(S)}}X_{1}(S_{j})
for any
m
, so it suffices to prove that for some
M
we have
X_{M}(S)=\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
. Observe that if some string
S^{\prime}
admits an index
k
such that
i^{S^{\prime}}_{k+1}<i^{S^{\prime}}_{k}
, then
\sigma_{k}S^{\prime}
factors through
X_{1}(S^{\prime})
, so it follows from
Lemma

4.14
every
j_{S}
factors through some
X_{M}(S)
for large enough
M
, meaning that we may extend
\widetilde{F}
to the entirety of
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

The natural inclusion
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
induces an isomorphism

\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)\cong\mathrm{Sq}(S).

Proof.

Consider
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
as a subcategory of some
\prod_{i=0}^{m^{\prime}}[q^{\prime}_{i}]
, then denote by
p^{S}_{i}:\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})\rightarrow[q^{\prime}_{i}]
the composition
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\hookrightarrow\prod_{k=0}^{m^{\prime}}[q^{\prime}_{k}]\xrightarrow{p_{i}}[q^{\prime}_{i}]
, where the last morphism is the projection to the
i
th coordinate. Call a morphism
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})\xrightarrow{j}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
with target
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\hookrightarrow\prod_{i=0}^{m}[q_{i}]

inert
if for any morphism
f:[1]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
such that
p_{k}\circ f:[1]\rightarrow[q^{\prime}_{k}]
are constant for
k\neq i^{\prime}
and inert for
k=i^{\prime}
the composition
j\circ f:[1]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
is such that
p_{k}\circ j\circ f:[1]\rightarrow[q_{k}]
is constant for
k\neq i
and inert for
k=i
(with
i\neq i^{\prime}
in general), it is easy to see that inert morphisms are closed under composition and include identities, hence they form a subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}
. Call an object
\mathrm{Sq}(S)

elementary
if it is isomorphic to
[1]^{l}
for some
l\leq(n-1)
, denote by
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(S)}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(S)}
on inert morphisms from elementary objects
[1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. Denote by
\widehat{S}
the reduced string of
S
defined in
4.13
, denote
a:\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
the morphism defined therein. Given
[1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
denote by
\mathrm{Im}(i)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
the full subcategory containing all objects
(x_{0},...,x_{m})
such that
a\circ i(0,...,0)\leq(x_{0},...,x_{m})\leq a\circ i(1,...,1)
, note that the subcategories of the form
\mathrm{Im}(i)
are the same thing as quadrants described in
4.13
. It follows from this and the definition of
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)
that we have an isomorphism

(65)

\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(S)\cong\underset{[1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i),

as simplicial sets, since
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}
preserves colimits to prove the claim it suffices to show that

(66)

\mathrm{Sq}(S)\cong\underset{[1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i).

We will prove (
66
) by induction on
n
, the case
n=1
being trivial. Assume we have proved the claim for
(n-1)
, we may assume that
S=l_{i^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}...l_{i^{\prime}_{1}}
contains all the terms
l_{k}
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
, since otherwise the claim follows by induction, denote
\widehat{S}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l_{i_{N}}...l_{i_{1}}
. Denote by
m_{q}
the total number of terms
l_{q}
in
\widehat{S}
for
0\leq q\leq n-1
and
s^{q}_{t}
for
1\leq t\leq m_{s}
the index of the
t
th appearance of
l_{q}
. For
i:[e]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}
denote by
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
containing
(x_{0},...,x_{n-1})
with
x_{n-1}\in[m_{n-1}]
in the image of
i
. For
t\in[m_{n-1}]
denote by
\widehat{S}_{t}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l_{i_{j_{k}}}l_{i_{j_{k-1}}}...l_{i_{j_{0}}}
the substring of
\widehat{S}
containing terms
l_{i_{s}}
with
s\geq s^{n-1}_{t}
and
i_{s}\neq n-1
, then it is easy to see that
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{\{t\}}\cong\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
, where
\{t\}:[0]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
is the inclusion of the object
t
, and
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t<t+1}\cong[1]\times\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
. Note that for
t_{0}<t_{1}
the string
\widehat{S}_{t_{1}}
is a substring of
\widehat{S}_{t_{0}}
, hence we have an inert inclusion
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t_{1}})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t_{0}})
. From these considerations we see that there is a functor
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{(-)}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
sending
\{t\}
to
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
,
(t<t+1)
to
[1]\times\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
, the inclusion
\{t\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}(t<t+1)
to
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})\overset{\{0\}\times\mathrm{id}}{\rightarrowtail}[1]\times\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
and
\{t+1\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}(t<t+1)
to the composition
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t+1})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})\overset{\{1\}\times\mathrm{id}}{\rightarrowtail}[1]\times\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S}_{t})
. Since all morphisms in
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}
are sent to inert morphisms, it follows that we have an induced functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{(-)}}
sending
i:[e]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
to
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i}}
, we claim that we have an isomorphism:

(67)

\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i}}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})}.

Indeed, we will show that (
67
) holds already at the level of simplicial sets: given
[1]^{m}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
we need to show that the space of factorizations
[1]^{m}\overset{i^{\prime}_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})
is contractible, however note that it is a singleton if
i_{0}
does not factor through any
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{\{t\}}
for
t\in[m_{n-1}]
and is isomorphic to the walking cospan
\Lambda^{0}_{2}
otherwise, both os those simplicial sets are contractible.

Using (
67
) and commutativity of colimits, we see that the right hand side of (
66
) can be rewritten as

(68)

\underset{[e]\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{[1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{Sq}(i_{0})\circ i).

For
i_{0}:[e]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
as above denote by
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{i_{0}}
the image of
\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i_{0}}
in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
under
a
, we claim that

(69)

\mathrm{Sq}(s)_{i_{0}}\cong\underset{([1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i_{0}})\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{i_{0}}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i).

Indeed, if
i_{0}
is of the form
[0]\overset{\{t\}}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
, then (
69
) follows immediately from the inductive assumption. If
i_{0}
has the form
[1]\overset{t<t+1}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
, then

(70)

\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{t<t+1}\cong[k_{t}]\times\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\{t\}}

for some
k_{t}>0
and

(71)

\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t<t+1}}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t}},

using this and the inductive assumption we get

\displaystyle\underset{([1]^{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t<t+1})\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t<t+1}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}[1])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{([1]^{l^{\prime}}\overset{i_{1}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t})\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i_{0})\times\mathrm{Im}(i_{1})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([1]^{l^{\prime}}\overset{i_{1}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t})\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t}}}{\mathrm{colim}}[k_{t}]\times\mathrm{Im}(i_{1})

\displaystyle\cong[k_{t}]\times\underset{([1]^{l^{\prime}}\overset{i_{1}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t})\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\mathrm{Sq}(\widehat{S})_{t}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Im}(i_{1})

\displaystyle\cong[k_{t}]\times\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{t}\cong\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{t<t+1},

where the first isomorphism follows from (
71
), he second since
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[1]}
has a final object, the third since
-\times-
commutes with colimits, the fourth by the inductive assumption and the fifth by (
70
).

It now follows from (
68
) and (
69
) that it suffices to prove

(72)

\mathrm{Sq}(S)\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{i},

we will prove it using deformation theory: denote by
X
the colimit in the right hand side of (
72
), we need to show that the natural morphism
f:X\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
is an isomorphism. By
[
11
, Corollary 2.6.2.]
and
[
12
, Theorem 5.2.]
it suffices to prove that (
72
) holds in the subcategory
\mathrm{Cat}_{(2,1)}
of
(2,1)
-categories, which is immediate using the explicit description of colimits of discrete categories, and that the relative cotangent complex
L_{f}\cong 0
. By the results of
[
24
]
we see that for
\mathscr{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}

\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{C}),\mathrm{Sp})
, the absolute cotangent complex
L_{\mathscr{C}}
is isomorphic (up to a shift) to the constant functor with value
\mathbb{S}
and for
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
we have

L_{f}\cong\mathrm{CoKer}(f_{!}\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}).

Applying this to our situation, we need to show that in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Sq}(S)),\mathrm{Sp})
we have

(73)

\mathbb{S}\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Sq}(i)_{!}\mathbb{S},

where
\mathrm{Sq}(i):\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{i})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Sq}(S))
denotes the functor induced by the natural inclusion
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. We now need to calculate
\mathrm{Sq}(i)_{!}\mathbb{S}
, note that for
f\in\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Sq}(S))
the value
\mathrm{Sq}(i)_{!}\mathbb{S}(f)
it is isomorphic to
|\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f|\otimes\mathbb{S}
, we will first show that
\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f
are either empty or contractible. Fix some
i:[e]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}]
and
f
as above, note that
f
corresponds to a pair
((x_{0},...,x_{n-1}),(y_{0},...,y_{n-1}))
with
y_{k}\geq x_{k}
. First, note that if the image of
i
does not intersect
[x_{n-1},y_{n-1}]
, then
\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f
is empty. Assume now that
t
is the minimal element of that intersection, in that case denote by
i_{t}:\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\{t\}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
the corresponding inclusion, it is easy to see that we either have
\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f\cong\mathrm{Sq}(i_{t})/f
(if
[x_{n-1},y_{n-1}]\cap\mathrm{Im}(i)=\{t\}
) or
\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f\cong\mathrm{TwAr}([1])\times\mathrm{Sq}(i_{t})/f
(if
[x_{n-1},y_{n-1}]
contains
(t<t+1)
), in either case it suffices to prove that
\mathrm{Sq}(i_{t})/f
is contractible, so we assume
i=i_{t}
from now on.

Note that an object of
\mathrm{Sq}(i_{t})/f
is given by a quadruple
((x_{0},...,x_{n-1}),(a_{0},...,t),(b_{0},...,t),(y_{0},...,y_{n-1}))
such that
x_{i}\leq a_{i}\leq b_{i}\leq y_{i}
, with a morphism from
(x,a,b,y)
to
(x,a^{\prime},b^{\prime},y)
given by
a^{\prime}_{i}\leq a_{i}\leq b_{i}\leq b^{\prime}_{i}
. Note that it contains a final object given by
((x_{0},...,x_{n-2},x_{n-1}),(x_{0},...,x_{n-2},t),(y_{0},...,y_{n-2},t),(y_{0},...,y_{n-2},y_{n-1}))
, meaning that it is indeed contractible. Applying these observations we get

\displaystyle\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Sq}(i)_{!}\mathbb{S}(f)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}|\mathrm{Sq}(i)/f|\otimes\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[y_{n-1}-x_{n-1}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[y_{n-1}-x_{n-1}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle\cong|\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[y_{n-1}-x_{n-1}]}|\otimes\mathbb{S}\cong\mathbb{S},

which proves (
73
) and concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

For
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
we have

\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)\cong\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}.

Proof.

We will define functors
F:\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(X)
and
G:\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
(which necessarily factors through
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)\cong\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
) and prove
G\circ F\cong\mathrm{id}
and
F\circ G\cong\mathrm{id}
. We will start with
G
: note that any morphism
f:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n,/x}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(X)([q])
such that the values at
([0]\overset{\{i\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q])
are given by
\theta_{f_{i}}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}x
for
i\in[q]
induces a string of inclusions
\mathrm{Im}(f_{0})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Im}(f_{1})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}...\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Im}(f_{q})
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
, we define
G(f)
to be this string of inert morphisms, it is easy to see that this indeed defines a functor.

We will now define
F
, we start by describing its value on objects and morphisms.
Given
y_{i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
define its image to be the composition
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y_{i}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
(where the first morphism exists since by definition
y\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{c}_{n}
). Assume now that we are given an inclusion
y_{i^{\prime}_{0}}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}y_{i^{\prime}_{1}}
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
, define
g:c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y_{i^{\prime}_{0}}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}y_{i^{\prime}_{1}}
, then by an iterated application of
Lemma

3.11
for
k=0,1,...,n-1
we get a diagram

(74)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{i^{\prime}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}^{0}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}^{1}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}^{n-1}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n-1}}

such that
g_{k}\circ a_{k}
is
k
-active in the sense of
3.9
and
\mathrm{Im}(g_{0}\circ i_{0})\cong y_{i^{\prime}_{0}}
. This defines a morphism
g_{i^{\prime}}:[n]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)
such that moreover
g_{i^{\prime}}(k<k+1)
lies in
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{k}(x)
, since
\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{Cat}}[n]^{0}\cong[n]
we can extend
g_{i^{\prime}}
to
g^{0}_{i^{\prime}}:[n]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
and we define
F(i^{\prime})
to be the composition
[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[n]\xrightarrow{g^{0}_{i^{\prime}}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
. More generally, given a morphism
f:[m]\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
we can use the same reasoning to define a morphism
g_{f}:[m*n]^{0}\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)
and again define
F(f)
to be the composition
[m]\overset{a_{n}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m*n]\xrightarrow{g_{f}^{0}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
, where
a_{n}(j)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}n*j
. We now need to prove that this actually defines a natural transformation of functors, i.e. that for any
h:[l]\rightarrow[m]
we have a commutative diagram

(75)

\hbox to137.62pt{\vbox to71.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.81079pt\lower-35.3195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Note that (
75
) clearly commutes for inert
h
, so we may assume
h=a
is active, moreover by construction it suffices to check that for any inert morphism
i:[1]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[l]
we have
F(f|_{\mathrm{Im}(a\circ i)})\circ a\circ i\cong F(f\circ a\circ i)
, so we may assume
l=1
. Denote by
S_{m}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l_{n-1}l_{n-2}...l_{0}l_{n-1}...l_{0}
of length
n*m
that contains each term
l_{i}

m
times, then by construction,
Lemma

4.15
and
Lemma

4.16
we can extend
g^{0}_{f}:[m*n]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
to
g^{S}_{f}:\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
such that
g^{0}_{f}
equals the composition
[m]\xrightarrow{\Delta_{m}}\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})\xrightarrow{g^{S}_{f}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
, where
\Delta_{m}(i)=(i,...,i)\in\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})
. Note that for the active morphism
a:[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
the induced morphism
a^{\times n}:[1]^{\times n}\rightarrow[m]^{\times n}
restricts to a morphism
a^{S}:\mathrm{Sq}(S_{l})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})
between the respective subcategories, also note that
\mathrm{Sq}(S_{1})\cong[n]
. Denote by
S^{0}_{m}
the string
l^{n}_{n-1}l^{n}_{n-2}...l^{n}_{0}
and by
i_{S^{0}_{m}}:[n*m]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})
the corresponding injective morphism defined in the proof of
Lemma

4.14
, then the morphism
a^{S}:[n]\cong\mathrm{Sq}(S_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})
can be identified with
[n]\overset{a_{m}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[n*m]\xrightarrow{i_{S_{m}^{0}}}\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})
, where the restriction of
a_{m}
to every elementary interval
[1]\overset{(i<i+1)}{\rightarrowtail}[n]
is isomorphic to
[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
, so it suffices to prove that for each
i
the following diagram

(76)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sq}(S_{1})\cong[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sq}(S_{1})\cong[n]}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{S}_{f\circ a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{S}_{f}}

commutes, where

denotes the inclusion of
(i<i+1)
.

Given
f:[m]\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}
the corresponding morphism
g_{f}:[m*n]^{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)
can be identified (as in the diagram (
74
)) with a natural transformation
\alpha:H_{f}\rightarrow H_{x}
between functors
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n*m]}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, where
H_{x}
is the constant functor with value
x
and
H_{f}
is a functor sending
[0]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[n*m]
to
c_{n}
and
[1]\overset{k,k+1}{\rightarrowtail}[n*m]
to
C^{k\mod m}_{n}(1)
, note that both of those functors admit left Kan extensions along
i^{\mathrm{el}}:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n*m]}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[n*m]}
such that
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}H_{x}
is a constant functor with value
x
and
i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}H_{f}([l]\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[n*m])
is the object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
corresponding to the linear tree of length
l
where the
k
th node is marked with
H_{f}([1]\overset{i^{\prime}(k)<i^{\prime}(k+1)}{\rightarrowtail}[n*m])
, in particular

in the notation of
6.4
. It then follows by construction that the morphism
g^{S}_{f}:\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
factors as

\mathrm{Sq}(S_{m})\xrightarrow{g^{S,^{\prime}}_{f}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(D_{n}(m,...,0))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathrm{id}_{[n*m]},!}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x),

hence to prove the commutativity of (
76
) we may assume
x=D_{n}(m,...,0)
and

is given by the string

c_{n}\cong D_{n}(0,...,0)\overset{D(i_{+}^{0})}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n}(1,...,0)\overset{D(i_{+}^{1})}{\rightarrowtail}...\overset{D(i_{+}^{m})}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n}(m,...,0),

where

is the unique inert morphism that preserves the maximal element (see

for the functoriality of

).

where the original morphism
g_{D}^{0}:[2n]\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)
gives us the morphisms
g_{s,0}
and
h_{n,k}
such that
g_{s,0}
is a morphism in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{s-1}_{\theta}(x)
that corresponds to the data of a cospan
c_{n}\overset{i^{*}_{s-1}}{\rightarrowtail}C^{s-1}_{n}(1)\twoheadleftarrow c_{n}
over
D_{n}(2,...,0)
and a morphism
C^{s-1}_{n}(1)\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}_{i,0}}D_{n}(2,...,0)
that takes the cells
i^{\pm}_{s-1}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(1,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-s}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
for
i\neq n+1-s
and the cell
i^{*}_{s-1}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
t\in\{1,2\}
and such that for
t=1
all
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=*
for
i\neq v
and
\delta_{v}=\pm
for all values of
v>n+1-s
. Similarly,
h_{n,s}
corresponds to the data of a cospan
c_{n}\overset{i^{*}_{s-1}}{\rightarrowtail}C^{s-1}_{n}(1)\twoheadleftarrow c_{n}
over
D_{n}(2,...,0)
and a morphism
C^{s-1}_{n}(1)\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}_{i,0}}D_{n}(2,...,0)
that takes the cells
i^{\pm}_{s-1}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(0,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-s}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
for
i\neq n+1-s
and the cell
i^{*}_{s-1}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
0\leq t\leq 2
and such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=*
for
i\neq v
that can take any values if
t=1
or
v>n+1-s
if
t=0
. We need to prove that the morphism
g^{\prime}_{k,k-1}:C^{k-1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
corresponding to
g_{k,k-1}
is such that it takes the cells
i^{\pm}_{k-1}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(1,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-k}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
for
i\neq n+1-k
and the cell
i^{*}_{k-1}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=*
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
with
v>n+1-k
and the morphism
h_{k,k}:C^{k-1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
corresponding to
h_{k,k}
is such that it takes the cells
i^{\pm}_{k-1}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(0,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-k}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
for
i\neq n+1-k
and the cell
i^{*}_{k-1}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=\pm
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
for
v>n+1-k
or
v=n+1-k
and
t=1
.

We first prove the following claim:
h^{\prime}_{i,1}:C^{0}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
sends the cells
i^{\pm}_{0}
to
((*,*,...,\pm),(0,0))
and
i^{*}_{0}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
0<t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=*
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
with
v>n+1-i
while
g^{\prime}_{s,1}:C_{n}^{s-1}\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
sends the cells
i^{\pm}_{s}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(1,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-s}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
otherwise and the cell
i^{*}_{s}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
0\leq t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=*
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
for
v>n+1-s
if
t=1
or
v=n
for
t=0
. Assume first that
i=n
, in that case the composable pair
(h_{n,1},g_{n,0})
gives rise to a morphism
C_{n}(1,0,...,0,1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
such that it takes
i^{\pm}_{0}
to
((*,...,\pm),(0,0))
,
i^{\pm}_{n-1}
to
((\pm,...,*),(1,0))
and
c_{n}\cong C_{n}(0,...,0)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1,0,...,0,1)
to the composition
c_{n}\overset{i_{n-1}^{*}}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}_{n,0}}D_{n}(2,...,0)
, by construction
h^{\prime}_{n-1,1}:C^{0}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
is given by the composition
C_{n}(1,0,....,0)\overset{(\mathrm{id},...,\mathrm{id},\{1\})}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1,0,....,1)
and
g^{\prime}_{n,1}
is given by the composition
C_{n}(0,...,0,1)\overset{(\{0\},\mathrm{id},...,\mathrm{id})}{\twoheadrightarrow}C_{n}(1,...,0,1)
, from this the required description follows. Assume we have now proved the required description for
h_{i,1}
with
i\geq u
and
g_{i,1}
with
i\geq(u+1)
, then the composable pair
(h_{u-1,1},g_{u,1})
again defines a morphism
C_{n}(1,...,1,...,0)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
(where the only non-zero terms are in positions 1 and
u
) such that it takes
i^{\pm}_{0}
to
((*,...,\pm),(0,0))
,
i^{\pm}_{u-1}
to
((*,...,\pm,...,*),(1,0))
with the only non-trivial term in position
(n-u)
and
c_{n}\cong C_{n}(0,...,0)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1,0,...,0,1)
to the composition
c_{n}\overset{i_{n-1}^{*}}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}_{u,0}}D_{n}(2,...,0)
, by construction
h^{\prime}_{u-1,1}:C^{0}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
is given by the composition
C_{n}(1,0,....,0)\overset{(\mathrm{id},...,\{1\},\mathrm{id},...,\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}(1,...,1,...,0)
and
g^{\prime}_{u,1}
is given by the composition
C_{n}(0,...,1,...,0)\overset{(\mathrm{id},...,\{0\},...,\mathrm{id})}{\twoheadrightarrow}C_{n}(1,...,1,...,0)
, from this the required description follows.

We now claim that
g^{\prime}_{q,k};C^{q-1}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
with
q>k
takes the cells
i^{\pm}_{q}
to
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(1,0))
with
\delta_{n+1-q}=\pm
and
\delta_{i}=*
otherwise and the cell
i^{*}_{q}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
0\leq t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=*
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
for
v>n+1-q
if
t=1
or
v\geq n+1-k
for
t=0
and that
h^{\prime}_{k+1,q}:C^{k}_{n}(1)\rightarrow D_{n}(2,...,0)
with
q\geq k+1
sends the cells
i^{\pm}_{k}
to
((*,...,\pm,...,*),(0,0))
with the only non-trivial
\delta
in position
(n-k)
and
i^{*}_{0}
to the composition of cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(t,0))
with
t\leq 2
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i\neq v}=\pm
,
\delta_{v}=\pm
with either
t=1
and
v>n+1-q
or
t=0
and
v>k+1
. Indeed, since we have already proved the claim for
k=0
, we may assume that it has been proved for
(k-1)
, so that we have the description of morphisms
g^{\prime}_{q,k-1}
and
h_{n,k}
, the proof then follows by the same argument described in the previous paragraph by changing the indices. This concludes the proof that
F
is well-defined.

Now, it is immediate that
G\circ F=\mathrm{id}
, we need to show that
F\circ G\cong\mathrm{id}
, so assume we have
f:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\Theta_{n,/x}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{C}_{\theta}(x)([q])
, denote
\alpha:C_{n}(q)\rightarrow x
the value
f(\mathrm{id}_{[q]})
, then it follows from the construction that the morphism
g_{G(f)}^{S}:\mathrm{Sq}(S_{q})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
appearing in the construction of
F
factors as
\mathrm{Sq}(S_{q})\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(C_{n}(q))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{!}}\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(x)
, hence we may assume
x=C_{n}(q)\in\Theta_{n}
, in which case the result follows immediately from
Lemma

4.11
.
∎

Corollary 4.18
.

We have an isomorphism

\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}x)\in\Theta_{n,/x}}{\mathrm{colim}}\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

4.2
,
Lemma

4.11
and
4.17
.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Given
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
denote by
L_{\mathscr{E}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Sigma^{\infty}(*)\in\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
and for
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
denote

L_{\mathscr{E}/\mathscr{D}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{CoKer}(f_{!}L_{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow L_{\mathscr{D}}),

then
f
is an isomorphism if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

\tau_{\leq n+1}f:\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{D}
is an isomorphism, where
\tau_{\leq n+1}:\mathrm{Cat}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}
is the left adjoint to the inclusion of
(n+1,n)
-categories;

(2)

L_{\mathscr{E}/\mathscr{D}}\cong 0
.

Proof.

The conditions are clearly necessary, so we need to prove they are sufficient. It suffices to prove that
f^{*}:h_{\mathscr{D}}\rightarrow h_{\mathscr{E}}
is an isomorphism, where
h_{\mathscr{E}}:\mathrm{Cat}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is a functor corepresented by
\mathscr{E}
and similarly for
h_{\mathscr{D}}
. For any
C\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}
and
\mathscr{A}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
any morphism
\mathscr{A}\rightarrow C
factors through
\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{A}
, so condition (
1
) implies that the restrictions of
h_{\mathscr{D}}
and
h_{\mathscr{E}}
to
\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}
are isomorphic. Assume we have proved that
f^{*}
restricts to an isomorphism on
\mathrm{Cat}_{(m,n)}
for some
m>n+1
, we will prove that
f^{*}
also defines an isomorphism on
\mathrm{Cat}_{(m+1,n)}
; since Postnikov towers converge for
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
this will conclude the proof.

Given
A\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(m,n)}
using
[
12
, Theorem 5.2.]
we can form a pullback square

\hbox to195.43pt{\vbox to71.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.71489pt\lower-35.60445pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

using our inductive assumption (and the adjunction
\Sigma^{\infty}\dashv\Omega^{\infty}
) we see that it remains to show that for any morphism
p:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n+1}A
we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\tau_{\leq n+1}A})}(p_{!}L_{\mathscr{E}},\Sigma^{m+1}\mathrm{H}\pi_{m}(A))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\tau_{\leq n+1}A})}(p_{!}L_{\mathscr{D}},\Sigma^{m+1}\mathrm{H}\pi_{m}(A)),

which clearly follows from (
2
).
∎

Theorem 4.20
.

For
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
we have an isomorphism

(79)

x\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\mathrm{colim}}e.

Proof.

Denote by
X
the colimit in the right-hand-side of (
79
), we have a natural morphism
f:X\rightarrow x
, by
4.19
it suffices to prove that
L_{f}\cong 0
and that
\tau_{\leq n+1}f
is an isomorphism. By definition the value of
L_{f}
at
y\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
is given by

\mathrm{CoKer}(|(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})_{/y}|\otimes\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}),

so it suffices to prove that
(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x})_{/y}\cong\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}
is contractible, however this follows from
3.13
since
y\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
. That
\tau_{\leq n+1}f
is an isomorphism follows from the freeness property of
[
2
]
.
∎

Corollary 4.21
.

A pushout diagram in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

remains a pushout in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

This follows by the same argument as in
Lemma

4.8
using
3.21
and the fact that the colimit (
79
) is preserved by
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.
∎

5.
\Theta_{n}
-trees

In
Section

3
we have introduced the formalism of strong Steiner complexes and proved a number of results regarding them. In practice, however, it is not easy to prove that a particular ADC defines a strong Steiner complex since it is difficult to demonstrate that its basis is loop-free. In the present section we introduce
\Theta_{n}
-trees – essentially they are given by iterated active/inert pushouts of objects of
\Theta_{n}
. Not all such pushouts lie in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, so we need additional constraints to ensure this is the case. We accomplish this by introducing a subcategory of
healthy trees
in
5.16
, the notion that was inspired by the healthy objects of
\Theta_{n}
defined in
[
4
]
. The main results of this section are
5.15
proving that healthy trees indeed define objects of
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
and
5.23
which shows that they are closed under certain pushouts in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
– the fact that will be used in the next section in construction of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})

Construction 5.1
.

By a
tree
we mean a finite rooted tree, each node except for the root admits one incoming edge and a finite number of outgoing edges, nodes with no outgoing edges are called
leaves
. We will denote nodes of the tree by
c
and edges by
e
, for a given edge
e
we will denote by
s(e)
its source and by
t(e)
its target (which are nodes of
t
). We will define a certain category
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
using
3.2
, but first we define its underlying set as follows: the elements of
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
are given by trees
t
such that each node
c
is marked with
\theta_{c}
and to each edge
e
with
s(e)=c
and
t(e)=c^{\prime}
corresponds an inert morphism
c_{j}\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
for some
j\leq n
and moreover
\theta_{c^{\prime}}\in\Theta_{j}
, we would refer to cells of the form
c_{j}\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
for
c\in T_{0}(x)
as
marked
with
\theta_{t(e)}
. These markings are required to satisfy an additional condition, however to introduce it we will need some notation. Given a marked tree
x
we will denote by
T(x)
the associated tree, by
T(x)_{0}
its set of nodes and by
L(x)\subset T_{0}(x)
the subset of leaves, also given
c\in T_{0}(x)
we will denote
\tau_{\geq c}
the object whose underlying tree is
T(x)_{c/}
and whose decorations are induced from
x
and similarly we will denote by
\tau_{<c}x
the object corresponding to the subtree of
T(x)_{/c}
obtained by removing the maximal element with induced decorations. With these notations we also require that the following holds:

(*)

if
c_{j}\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
is marked with
\theta_{t(e)}
and
d^{\pm}_{q}\tau_{\geq t(e)}x\neq c_{q}
, then, if we denote by
i_{e^{\prime}}
the composition
c_{q}\overset{i_{q}^{\pm}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{j}\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
,
i_{e^{\prime}}
is marked with
d^{\pm}_{q}\theta_{t(e)}
and moreover
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x=d^{\pm}_{q}\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
.

Define
T_{m}(x)
to be the subtree of
T(x)
that only contains nodes
c
such that if
c\in\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
for some marked edge
e^{\prime}
with
i_{e^{\prime}}:c_{k}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c^{\prime}}
, then there does not exist a marked edge
e
such that
i_{e^{\prime}}
factors through
i_{e}:c_{l}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c^{\prime}}
. We associate to
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
an ADC
C(x)
with basis as follows: the basis elements of dimension
j\leq n
are given by
unmarked
cells
c_{j}\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
for
c\in T_{m}(x)
, we will denote an element corresponding to such an inclusion by
[i_{0},c]
. Given such an element we set

(80)

d^{\pm}[i_{0},c]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[i_{0}\circ i^{\pm}_{j-1},c]

if
i_{0}\circ i_{j-1}^{\pm}
is also unmarked. If it is marked denote by
e^{\prime}
the corresponding edge and pick some
i_{e}:c_{k}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c^{\prime}}
such that
t(e^{\prime})
lies in
d^{\pm}_{j-1}\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
, then by (
(*)
‣
5.1
) we can view
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
as a subobject of
\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
which does not depend on the choice of
e
, so in particular we can view unmarked edges of
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
as unmarked edges of
\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
, hence as basis elements of
C(x)
. With these considerations in mind, we set

(81)

d^{\pm}[i_{0},c]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{c_{0}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{0}}}[i^{\prime},c_{0}],

where the first sum is taken over all nodes of
T(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x)
and the second over all
unmarked

(j-1)
-cells. We will define augmentation by setting
e(c_{0}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}1
. We will prove in
5.3
below that this defines an ADC with basis, denote it by
C(x)
, we define morphisms from
x
to
x^{\prime}
in
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
to be the morphisms of
ADCs
between
C(x)
and
C(x^{\prime})
.

Notation 5.2
.

Given
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
we will denote by
B(x)_{\bullet}
the basis of the corresponding ADC
C(x)
, i.e.
B(x)_{j}
for
0\leq j\leq n
is the set of elementary unmarked
j
-cells in some
\theta_{c}
for
c\in T_{0}(x)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

The objects
C(x)
defined in
5.1
for
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
are ADCs with basis.

Proof.

The components
C(x)_{i}
are obviously free and the basis is unital, so all we need to show is that
\partial\circ\partial\cong 0
. Fix some unmarked
c_{j}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
in
T_{m}(x)
, we will assume that both
i\circ i^{-}_{j-1}
and
i\circ i^{+}_{j-1}
are marked since all other cases are similar but easier, denote the corresponding edges by
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
respectively. By definition

(82)

\partial[i,c]=\sum_{c_{0}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{0}}}[i^{\prime},c_{0}]-\sum_{c_{1}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime\prime})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{1}}}[i^{\prime},c_{1}],

where the inner sums are taken over unmarked cells and as in
5.1
we have identified
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
and
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime\prime})}x
with subobjects of some
\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
with
t(e)\in T_{m}(x)
. Also by definition we have

(83)

\partial(\sum_{c_{0}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{0}}}[i^{\prime},c_{0}])=\sum_{c_{0}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-1}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{0}}}(d^{+}[i^{\prime},c_{0}]-d^{-}[i^{\prime},c_{0}]).

Note that all the terms corresponding to
(j-2)
-cells that are both the positive and the negative boundary of some
(j-1)
-cells cancel out, so we can rewrite (
83
) as

(84)

\sum_{c_{0}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{{}^{\prime},+}_{j-2})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-2}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{0}}}[i^{\prime},c_{0}]-\sum_{c_{1}\in T_{0}(\tau_{\geq t(e^{{}^{\prime},-}_{j-2})}x)}\sum_{c_{j-2}\overset{i^{\prime\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c_{1}}}[i^{\prime\prime},c_{1}].

The same reasoning also applies to
e^{\prime\prime}
, the claim now follows from the fact that
e^{{}^{\prime},\pm}_{j-2}=e^{{}^{\prime\prime},\pm}_{j-2}
and the markings on this cell induced from
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
agree by definition of
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Given
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
and
c\in T_{m}(x)
, denote by
\tau_{<c}x
the decorated tree whose underlying tree is
T(x)_{<c}
with decorations induced from
x
, then
\tau_{<c}x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
.

Proof.

We need is to prove that condition (
(*)
‣
5.1
) holds for
\tau_{<c}x
, however note that by definition for any marked cell
e
with a
q
-boundary
e^{\prime}
we have
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x\cong\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}\tau_{<c}x
since by construction
c
does not belong to
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
and similarly
d^{\pm}_{q}\tau_{\geq t(e)}x=d^{\pm}_{q}\tau_{\geq t(e)}\tau_{<c}x
since
c
does not belong to
d^{\pm}_{q}\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Note that it is
not
true in general that
T_{m}(\tau_{<c}x)\cong T_{m}(x)_{<c}
.

Definition 5.6
.

Denote by
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
the full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
containing objects
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{\prime}
as above such that the bases of all
C(\tau_{<c}x)
for
c\in T_{m}(x)
are strongly loop-free in the sense of
3.1
, so that
\tau_{<c}x
are strong Steiner complexes.

Example 5.7
.

The condition that all
C(\tau_{<c}x)
are strongly loop-free does not follows from only
C(x)
being strongly loop-free: for example consider the following 2-composable pair of 3-morphisms:
