We can view it as an object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{3}
, mark both
A
and
B
with the object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

and denote the resulting object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{3}
by
x
and the corresponding nodes of
T(x)
by
c_{A}
and
c_{B}
. Then the 3-category corresponding to
x
is an object of
\Theta_{3}
which we may depict as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

while
\tau_{<c_{A}}
corepresents composable pair of 3-morphisms of the form

\alpha\xrightarrow{A}b*\beta*a\xrightarrow{B}b*\gamma*a.

In particular,
a
and
b
lie in both the positive and the negative boundary of
\alpha
, meaning that
C(\tau_{<c_{A}}x)
cannot be strongly loop-free.

Warning 5.8
.

An object
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
may admit many presentations as a decorated tree as in
5.1
, for example if
x
is such that all elementary cells in
\theta_{c}
are marked, then such an object corresponds to a string of active morphisms
\theta^{0}\overset{a^{1}}{\twoheadrightarrow}...\overset{a^{m}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta^{m}
in
\Theta
and
C(x)
is isomorphic to
C(\theta^{m})
, where
\theta^{m}
is identified with an object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
as in
Remark

3.3
. In particular, every object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{1}
is isomorphic to one of this form, so we have
\mathpzc{Tree}_{1}\cong\Delta
.

Notation 5.9
.

Assume we have a cell
i:c_{j}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
in some
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
, if it is unmarked denote by
C(\{i,c\})
the subcomplex of
C(x)
containing the basis element
[i,c]
and basis elements appearing in various
d^{\pm}...d^{\pm}[i,c]
, if it is marked denote by
e
the corresponding edge and by
\{i,c\}
the subcomplex of
C(x)
generated by the basis elements
[i^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\in\tau_{\geq t(e)}x
. Note that in both cases it can be identified with an object of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
corresponding to the subtree of
T(x)
containing
c
and the nodes in
\tau_{\geq t(e^{\prime})}x
for all marked
i_{e^{\prime}}:c_{k}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
that factor through
i
such that
t(e^{\prime})
has markings induced from
x
and
c
is marked with
c_{j}
. It follows that the natural inclusion
C(\{i,c\})\hookrightarrow C(x)
can be identified with an inert morphism
\{i_{e},c\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
. We will call such inert morphisms
elementary
if the cell
i
is unmarked.

Proposition 5.10
.

The objects
\{i,c\}
of
5.9
lie in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
.

Proof.

Note that
\{i,c\}
satisfies (
(*)
‣
5.1
) since its marking is induced from the one on
x
, hence it suffices to show that for any
c_{0}\in T_{m}(\{i,c\})
the object
C(\tau_{<c_{0}}\{i,c\})
has a strongly loop-free basis. For this note that we can identify
C(\tau_{<c_{0}}\{i,c\})
with a subcomplex of
C(\tau_{<c_{0}}x)
, it has a strongly loop-free basis by
5.6
, hence the basis of
C(\tau_{<c_{0}}\{i,c\})
must also be loop-free since any loop in it would also be a loop in
C(\tau_{<c}x)
, and hence must be trivial.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Given
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
and an edge
e
of
T(x)
corresponding to
i_{e}:c_{j}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
such that
t(e)\in T_{m}(x)
, then we can also identify
i_{e}
with an unmarked cell in
\tau_{<t(e)}x
, denote it by
i_{0}
. With these notations we have a pushout diagram

(85)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i_{0},c\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{<t(e)}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i,c\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
which remains a pushout diagram in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

That all objects in the diagram
Equation

85
lie in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
follows from
5.10
and
5.4
. We now need to define the active morphism
\{i_{0},c\}\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}\{i,c\}
, we do so by sending each basis element in
d_{j}^{\pm}\{i_{0},c\}
to the corresponding element of
B_{\bullet}(\{i,c\})
and the basis element
[i_{0},c]
to the sum of all unmarked
j
-cells in
\{i,c\}
. It is easy to see that (
85
) is then a pushout square of the corresponding complexes, hence defines a pushout square in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
, that it remains a pushout square in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
follows from
4.21
.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

Any
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
admits an active morphism
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
, so that
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
.

Proof.

If
x\cong\theta\in\Theta_{n}
, then the claim follows from the elementary properties of
\Theta_{n}
, in the general case we may use induction on the size of
T(x)
and represent
x
as a pushout of the form (
85
). By induction there is an active morphism
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\tau_{<t(e)}x
and we can define the required morphism to be the composition
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\tau_{<t(e)}x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
.
∎

Definition 5.13
.

We will call an object
\theta\in\Theta_{n}

healthy
if every elementary
i
-cell in
\theta
for
i<n
lies in the boundary of a non-trivial elementary
n
-cell. We will call a morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
between healthy objects
healthy
if the image of any non-trivial
n
-cell of
\theta
in
\theta^{\prime}
is a healthy object.

Lemma 5.14
.

Assume
\theta
is healthy in the sense of
5.13
with
n>1
, then there are no elementary cells
c_{j}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta
that lie both in
d_{j}^{-}\theta
and
d_{j}^{+}\theta
.

Proof.

We will prove the claim by induction on
n
: in the case
n=1
healthy objects correspond to object
[m]
with
m>0
and their boundaries are just endpoints, which are distinct since
m\neq 0
. In the general case for
j>0
observe that it necessarily factors through some
\theta_{k}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(k,k+1)
(which are easily seen to also be healthy), from which the claim follows by induction, and for
j=0
we need to show that the endpoints of
\theta
are distinct, which follows since it contains at least one elementary
n
-cell.
∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Assume that
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{\prime}
is such that:

(1)

the objects
\theta_{*}
corresponding to the root node
*\in T_{0}(x)
is healthy;

(2)

for any edge
e
corresponding to an inert morphism of the form
c_{l}\overset{i_{e}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
we have
\theta_{t(e)}\in\Theta^{h}_{l}
,

then
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
.

Proof.

Note that all the subcomplexes
\tau_{<c}x
also satisfy the conditions of the proposition, so it suffices to show that
x
itself is loop-free. We will prove this by induction on the size of the underlying tree, starting with the case of a tree with a single root node. Such an objects is equivalent to an object of
\Theta_{n}
and we again use induction, this time on
n
, note that the case
n=0
is trivial. Assume we have shown that all
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{n-1}
correspond to strong Steiner complexes and take
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
, then observe that for two elementary cells
a
and
b
in
\theta
we have
a<_{\mathbb{N}}b
either if both
a
and
b
lie in
\theta^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i,i+1)\in\Theta_{n-1}
for some object
i
of
\theta
and
a<_{\mathbb{N}}b
as cells in
\theta^{\prime}
or if
a
and
b
are both 1-dimensional and
a
belongs to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(k-1,k)
while
b
belongs to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(k,k+1)
for some object
k
of
\theta
. It follows that any potential loop
a_{0}<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{1}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{0}
should either lie entirely in some
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i,i+1)
, which is impossible by induction, or induce a sequence
k_{0}<k_{0}+1<...<k_{0}
of objects of
\theta
, which is also clearly impossible.

Assume now that
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
is obtained from
x^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{\leq c}x
, for which we have shown that its basis is strongly loop-free, by marking a cell
c_{j}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
for some
c\in L(x^{\prime})
, adding an extra edge
e
with source
c
and decorating its target
t(e)
with
\theta\in\Theta^{h}_{j}
– it is easy to see that all object of
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
of the type described in the statement of the proposition can be inductively constructed like this starting with objects of
\Theta_{n}
, denote by
f:x^{\prime}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}x
the induced morphism. Assume we have a string
L\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(a_{0}<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{1}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{0})
of basis elements of
x
, note that if all
a_{j}
and
[a_{j}]^{\pm}_{i}
lie in
x^{\prime}
or
\theta
, then the string can be identified with a string in the corresponding subcomplex and hence must be trivial by inductive assumption. Without loss of generality we may therefore assume that
a_{0}
lies in the image of
x^{\prime}
and the string intersects the interior of
\theta
. In that case we may choose a minimal
k
such that
a_{k+1}
lies in
d^{-}_{j-1}\theta
, while
a_{k+1}
does not, and similarly we can pick a minimal
l>k
such that
a_{l+1}
does not lie in the image of
d_{j-1}^{+}\theta
, while
a_{l}
does. In this case we can write the segment between
a_{k}
and
a_{l+1}
as

(86)

a_{k}<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{k+1}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{s}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{t}<_{\mathbb{N}}...a_{l}<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{l+1},

where all elements in the segment between
a_{k+1}
and
a_{s-1}
lie in
d_{j-1}^{-}
, the segment from
a_{s}
and
a_{t}
lies in the interior of
\theta
and the segment between
a_{s+1}
and
a_{l}
lies in
d^{+}_{j-1}\theta
. Note that for any
a_{i}
with
k+1\leq i\leq s-1
there is a unique basis element
a^{\prime}_{i}
in
x^{\prime}
such that
a_{i}
appears in the decomposition of
f(a^{\prime}_{i})
which moreover lies in
d^{-}_{j-1}\{i^{\prime},c\}
. Indeed, if
i^{\prime}\circ i^{-}_{j-1}
is marked then we may identify
a_{i}
with a basis element of
x^{\prime}
, which would satisfy our requirements, and if it is unmarked then
a_{i}
appears in the decomposition of
a([i^{\prime}\circ i^{-}_{j-1}])
by construction. In both cases there are clearly no other basis elements in
d^{-}_{j-1}\{i^{\prime},c\}
that satisfy these requirements, so the only way there may be another basis element
a^{\prime\prime}_{i}
of
x
satisfying them is if
a_{i}
also lies in
d^{+}_{j-1}\theta
, however this is impossible by
Lemma

5.14
since
\theta
was assumed to be healthy. Using a similar statement for
a_{q}
with
t+1\leq q\leq l
and
d^{+}_{j-1}\{i^{\prime},c\}
we can lift the string (
86
) to a string

a_{k}<_{\mathbb{N}}a^{\prime}_{k+1}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a^{\prime}_{s}<[i^{\prime},c]<_{\mathbb{N}}...a^{\prime}_{l}<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{l+1}

in
x^{\prime}
, concatenating it with the remainder of the loop
a_{0}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{0}
we may produce a loop that intersects
\theta
one less time. Using this process for all other intersections of the original loop with
\theta
we can produce a loop
L^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(a_{0}<_{\mathbb{N}}a^{\prime}_{1}<_{\mathbb{N}}...<_{\mathbb{N}}a_{0})
that lies entirely in
x^{\prime}
, however it must be then trivial by our inductive assumption. Note that by construction the restriction of
L^{\prime}
to the complement of
\{i^{\prime},c\}
coincides with the corresponding restriction of
L
, which means that it must be trivial, hence
L
must lie entirely in the image of
\theta
, which once again violates out inductive assumption.
∎

Definition 5.16
.

Call an object
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}

healthy
if it satisfies the conditions of
5.15
, call a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
between healthy objects
healthy
if for every elementary
n
-cell
i:\{i_{e},c\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
(in the sense of
5.9
) the object
x^{\prime}
appearing in the factorization square in
\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
(which exists by
3.17
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i,c\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

lies in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
and is healthy.

Lemma 5.17
.

Assume that we have a healthy morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
and an inert morphism
\{i_{l},c\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
for some
c\in T_{0}(x)
and an unmarked cell
i_{l}:c_{l}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
, then the morphism
a_{i_{l}}
appearing in the factorization diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i_{l},c\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{i_{l}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{l}}

is also a healthy morphism in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{l}^{h}
.

Proof.

We can assume without the loss of generality that
f
is an active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
. If
l=n
, then the claim follows immediately from the definition, so we may assume that
l<n
. In that case, since
x
was assumed to be healthy, we can find some
i_{n}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
such that
i_{l}=d^{\pm}_{l}i_{n}
. In that case we also have
y_{i_{l}}\cong d^{\pm}_{l}y_{i_{n}}
, so it suffices to show that a boundary
d_{l}^{\pm}y
of a healthy object
y
is a healthy object. Note that the subtree
T(d^{\pm}_{l}y)\subset T(y)
contains the root node
*
marked with
d^{\pm}_{l}\theta_{*}
, where
\theta_{*}
is the marking of the root node in
y
, as well as all the nodes in
T(\tau_{\geq t(e)})
for all edges corresponding to
i_{e}:c_{q}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{*}
that factor as
c_{q}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}d^{\pm}_{l}\theta_{*}\overset{\theta}{\rightarrowtail}_{*}
with markings induced from
y
. It follows immediately from this that
d^{\pm}_{l}
satisfies condition (
2
) of
5.15
, so it suffices to show that a boundary of a healthy object
\theta_{*}\in\Theta^{h}_{n}
lies in
\Theta^{h}_{l}
. So assume that for some
q<l
we have
c_{q}\overset{i_{q}}{\rightarrowtail}d^{+}_{l}\theta_{*}
, pick some
c_{n}\overset{i_{n}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{*}
such that
i_{q}=d^{\pm}i_{n}
. Note that if
i^{\prime}_{n}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{*}
is such that
d^{=}_{l}i_{n}=d^{-}_{l}i^{\prime}_{n}
, then we still have
i_{q}=d^{\pm}_{q}i^{\prime}_{n}
, so we may assume that
i_{l}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}d^{+}_{l}i_{n}
lies in
d^{+}_{l}\theta_{*}
, but then
i_{q}=d^{\pm}_{q}i_{l}
, meaning that
d^{+}_{l}\theta_{*}
is healthy.
∎

Notation 5.18
.

Given
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
denote
\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(x)
the set of healthy active morphisms in the sense of
5.16
with source
x
.

Proposition 5.19
.

For
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
we have

(87)

\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(x)\cong\underset{(e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\lim}\mathrm{act}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}^{h}(e).

Proof.

Note that we have a function

(88)

F_{h}:\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(x)\rightarrow\underset{(e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\lim}\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(e)

that sends an active morphism
x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}z
to the family of morphisms
a_{i}:e\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}z_{i}
appearing in the factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

indexed over all inert morphisms
i
from elementary objects to
x
, the fact that
F
lands in
\underset{(e\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x)\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/x}}{\lim}\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(e)
follows directly from
Lemma

5.17
.

Our goal is to construct an inverse
G_{h}
to the morphism
F_{h}
of (
88
), so assume we have a compatible family of active morphisms
a_{i,c}:\{i,c\}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}z_{i,c}
indexed over unmarked cells
c_{l}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
over all
c\in T_{0}(x)
, we first define a tree
T^{\prime}
to be a tree whose nodes are either nodes of
T(x)
or nodes of some
z_{i,c}
, we set
c_{1}<_{T^{\prime}}c_{2}
if either both belong to
T_{0}(x)
and
c_{0}<_{T_{0}(x)}c_{1}
, both lie in some
T(z_{i,c})
and
c_{0}<_{T(z_{i,c})}c_{1}
or
c_{0}\in T_{0}(x)
,
c_{1}\in T(z_{i,c})
and
c_{0}<_{T_{0}(x)}c
, it is easy to see that this defines a rooted tree. It admits a decorations of nodes and edges induced from
x
and various
z_{i,c}
, since all of them were assumed to be healthy it is immediate that
T^{\prime}
with these decorations also satisfies (
1
) and (
2
), hence defines an object
z\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
. Moreover, we have a natural active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}z
taking an unmarked cell
\{i_{l}:c_{l}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c},c\}
to the composition of all
l
-morphisms in
z_{i_{l},c}\cong T(\tau_{\geq t_{i_{l},c}}z)
, where
t_{i_{l},c}
denotes the target of the edge in
T(z)
corresponding to
c_{l}\overset{i_{l}}{\rightarrowtail}\theta_{c}
, where
c
is viewed as a node in
T(z)
. This defines the required morphism
G_{h}
. immediately from construction we have
F_{h}\circ G_{h}
, to prove
G_{h}\circ F_{h}\cong\mathrm{id}
note that an active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
is uniquely determined by the images of the elementary cells
\{i,c\}
, hence
F_{h}
is injective, meaning that it is an isomorphism since it admits a section.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

For any healthy active morphism
a:x\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y
and any inert
x^{\prime}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
, the morphism
a^{\prime}
appearing in the factorization diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

is also healthy.

Proof.

We have already proved a special case of this claim for
x^{\prime}\cong\{i,c\}
in
Lemma

5.17
, so in particular for any
\{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x^{\prime}
we have healthy active morphisms
a^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}}:\{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}\}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}y_{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
. These morphisms define an element of
\underset{\{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}\}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x^{\prime}}{\lim}\mathrm{act}^{h}_{\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}}(\{i^{\prime},c^{\prime}\})
, hence we conclude by
5.19
.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

Healthy morphisms are closed under composition.

Proof.

Observe that all inert morphisms are healthy and more generally a morphism
f\cong i\circ a
is healthy if and only if its active part
a
is healthy. So it suffices to prove that the healthy active morphisms are closed under composition and stable under active/inert factorization.

Assume first that we have a composable pair
x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{\prime}\overset{a^{\prime\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}x^{\prime\prime}
where both morphisms are healthy, we must prove that
a^{\prime}\circ a
is also healthy. For that we need to show that for any elementary cell
i:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}x
it image
x_{i}^{\prime\prime}
in
x^{\prime\prime}
is healthy. Denote by
x^{\prime}_{i}
the image of
i
in
x^{\prime}
, then
x^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic to the image of
x^{\prime}_{i}
under
a^{\prime\prime}
, which is healthy by
5.20
.

Finally, we need to prove that for any factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

if
a_{1}
is healthy, then so is
a_{0}
, however this is easy to see since the restriction of
a_{0}
to any elementary
n
-cell of
x_{0}
coincides with the restriction of
a_{1}
to its image in
x_{1}
.
∎

Notation 5.22
.

We will denote by
\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
the subcategory (which is well-defined by
5.21
) of
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
on healthy objects and healthy morphisms.

Proposition 5.23
.

Assume that we have a span
y\overset{i}{\leftarrowtail}x\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}z
in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
, then there is a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
which remains a pushout in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

Note that we can always take the required pushout in the category
\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
by taking the pushout of the corresponding ADCs, we need to prove that the resulting object lies in
\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
, however under the conditions of the lemma this follows from
5.15
since all nodes of
w
are marked with healthy objects by construction. Finally, the fact that this remains a pushout in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
follows by iterated application of
Lemma

5.11
.
∎

6.
Twisted arrow categories

In this section we finally complete the definition of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
and prove that it coincides with the model
\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})
defined in
Section

4
. Accordingly, the first part of the section is dedicated to the definition of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
culminating in
6.11
, while the second half is dedicated to computing
\mathrm{TwAr}(x)
for
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, which is the key computation in the proof of the comparison isomorphism
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
. Finally, in
Theorem

6.19
and
6.21
we use the results of
[
12
]
on Postnikov towers of
(\infty,n)
-categories to connect our theorem to the deformation theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories.

Construction 6.1
.

Given
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})
the subfunctor of

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/-},\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})}\mathrm{Cat}

sending
[q]
to the subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
such that:

(1)

For any morphism
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])
the values
F([l]\overset{i_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}[q])
have the form
x_{i_{0}}\xrightarrow{f_{i_{0}}}\mathscr{E}
for some
x_{i_{0}}\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
of
5.22
, moreover for
i\in[q]
we have
x_{\{i\}}\cong c_{n}
, where
[0]\overset{\{i\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
is the inclusion of the element
\{i\}
;

(2)

given any morphism
[l]\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[m]
between
i_{0}:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
and
i_{1}:[m]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[q]
, if
i^{\prime}
preserves the minimal element, the corresponding morphism
h_{i^{\prime}}:x_{i_{0}}\rightarrow x_{i_{1}}
is inert, and if
i^{\prime}
preserves the maximal element, then the morphism
h_{i^{\prime}}
is an active healthy morphism in the sense of
5.16
;

(3)

any natural transformation
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([1]\times\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
that lies in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})
satisfies
\alpha_{[0]\overset{\{i\}}{\rightarrowtail}[q]}\cong\mathrm{id}
for all
i\in[q]
.

Note that this is indeed a subfunctor since both inert and active healthy morphisms are closed under composition (the latter by
5.21
). Finally, denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
the composition
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})}\mathrm{Cat}\xrightarrow{|-|}\mathscr{S}
, where the second functor is the geometric realization.

Proposition 6.2
.

\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})
satisfies the Segal condition.

Proof.

Temporarily denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([q])
the subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]},\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
satisfying the same conditions as in
6.1
. In other words, the objects of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([q])
are given by strings of cospans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{...}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{q-1,q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{q-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{q}}

over
\mathscr{E}
and morphisms are given by commutative diagrams

\hbox to508.48pt{\vbox to117.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 254.23602pt\lower-58.65483pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

It follows that
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])
satisfies the Segal condition and moreover we have

(89)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])

\displaystyle\cong|\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\mathscr{E})([1])\times_{\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\mathscr{E})([0])}...\times_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([0])}\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([1])|

(90)

\displaystyle\cong|\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\mathscr{E})([1])|\times_{|\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\mathscr{E})([0])|}...\times_{|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([0])|}|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([1])|

(91)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([1])\times_{\mathrm{TwAr}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([0])}...\times_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([0])}\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([1]),

where the first isomorphism follows from the Segal condition, the second from iterated application of
[
3
, Lemma 5.17.]
(which applies in our case since
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([0])
is an
\infty
-groupoid).

Denote by
j:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}
the natural inclusion, note that we have a restriction functor
j^{*}:\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([q])
given by a precomposition with
j
. We claim that it has a left adjoint which is given by
j_{!}
– the left Kan extension along
j
. Indeed, it suffices to prove that for
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([q])
the value

(92)

j_{!}F([l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\cong\underset{[e]\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}[l]}{\mathrm{colim}}F(i\circ i^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}

belongs to
\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
, however this follows since the colimit (
92
) is an iterated pushout of healthy active morphisms along inert morphisms, and those exist by
5.23
. Since left adjoints induce isomorphisms on geometric realizations, it follows that
|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])|\cong|\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}_{0}(\mathscr{E})([q])|
, and the claim follows from (
89
).
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

We have

\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})\cong\underset{(x\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E})\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(x).

Proof.

It suffices to show that

\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})\cong\underset{x\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(x).

To prove this it suffices to show that for
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F}\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])
the space of factorizations

\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/x}\xrightarrow{f_{!}}\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}

of
F
for
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
is contractible, however note that it in fact has an initial object given by

\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/s(F([q]=\joinrel=[q]))}\xrightarrow{F([q]=\joinrel=[q])_{!}}\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}},

where
s(F([q]=\joinrel=[q]))
denotes the source of the corresponding object of
\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}}
viewed as a morphism in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.
∎

Construction 6.4
.

We will describe a certain functor
D_{n}^{p}:\Delta^{p}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{\mathrm{act}}
for
p\leq n
, we will denote
D^{n}_{n}
simply by
D_{n}
, we will start by describing its value on objects. For that we will first need some notation: recall from
4.7
the categories
C_{n}^{j}(1)
for
0\leq j\leq n-1
: they can be described as a sequence of 3
n
-cells composable along their
j
-boundaries, we will denote those cells
\{-,*,+\}
in order of composition and define
i_{j,n}^{\sigma}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C^{j}_{n}(1)
to be the inclusion of the corresponding cell.

Define
D_{n}^{p}(0,q_{2},...,q_{p})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}c_{n}
and if
q_{1}>0
define
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
to be the poset of strings of symbols
S\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l^{\sigma_{N-1}}_{i_{N-1}}l_{i_{N}}
of finite length (including the empty string) such that for
0\leq k\leq N
we have
0\leq i_{k}\leq p-1
and
\sigma_{k}\in\{-,*,+\}
and such that the following conditions are satisfies:

(1)

i_{0}=0
;

(2)

if
\sigma_{k}\neq*
, then
i_{s}<i_{k}
for
s>k
;

(3)

i_{k+1}=i_{k}+1
unless
i_{k}=\underset{s<k,\sigma_{s}\neq*}{\inf}i_{s}-1
in which case
i_{k+1}=0
;

(4)

call a term
l^{\sigma_{k}}_{i_{k}}
with
i_{k}=j

extremal
if either
i_{k}=p-1
or there exists a term
l^{\pm}_{i_{s}}
with
s<k
and
i_{s}=j+1
, then the number of extremal terms
l^{\sigma_{k}}_{j}
is
\leq q_{p-j}
, if
q_{p-j}=0
we interpret this to mean that the string immediately terminates after the term
l^{\pm}_{j+1}
.

We declare that
S<_{D}S^{\prime}
is the string
S^{\prime}
can be obtained from
S
by adding symbols on the right. It is clear that
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
admits a minimal element (namely, the empty string) and that for all
S\in T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
the category
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))_{/S}
is a linearly ordered set
[\mathrm{len}(S)]
, so
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
is indeed a tree.

It remains to define markings on the nodes and edges of
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
, we mark the empty string with
c_{n}
and a string
l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}
with
C^{p-i_{N}-1}_{n}(1)
, the edge
\varnothing<l^{\sigma_{0}}_{0}
is marked with
c_{n}=\joinrel=c_{n}
and an edge
l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}<l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}^{\sigma_{N}}l_{i_{N+1}}
is marked with
i^{\sigma_{N}}_{p-i_{N}-1,n}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C^{p-i_{N}-1}_{n}(1)
.

Proposition 6.5
.

The object
D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{\prime}_{n}
described in
6.4
belongs to
\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
.

Proof.

The fact that
D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
would follow immediately if we show
D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
since all
C_{n}^{j}(1)
lie in
\Theta_{n}^{h}
. To prove
D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
we need to show that it satisfies the condition (
(*)
‣
5.1
), for that we first need figure out which marked cells in
T_{m}(D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
share a boundary. By definition such a marked cell corresponds to a string
S\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}
with
0\leq i_{j}\leq p-1
and
\sigma_{i}\in\{-,*,+\}
together with an
n
-cell in
C_{n}^{p-i_{N}-1}(1)
, by definition
C_{n}^{p-i_{N}-1}(1)
contains three non-trivial
n
-cells
i^{\sigma}_{p-i_{N}-1,n}
for
\sigma\in\{-,*,+\}
such that

(93)

d^{\pm}_{p-1-i_{N}}i^{\mp}_{i_{N}+n-p,n}=d^{\mp}_{p-1-i_{N}}i^{*}_{i_{N}+n-p,n},

cells in the
j
-boundary of
i^{\sigma}_{i_{N}+n-p,n}
are distinct for
j>p-1-i_{N}
and for
j<p-1-i_{N}
we have

(94)

d^{\pm}_{j}i^{-}_{i_{N}+n-p,n}=d^{\pm}_{j}i^{*}_{i_{N}+n-p,n}=d^{\pm}_{j}i^{+}_{i_{N}+n-p,n}.

Consequently, the claim would be proved if we could show that

d^{\pm}_{j}\tau_{\geq t([S,i^{\pm}_{p-i_{N}-1,n}])}D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})\cong c_{j}

for
j\leq p-1-i_{N}
, since then the condition (
(*)
‣
5.1
) would be vacuous. Note however that all the nodes in
T_{m}(\tau_{\geq t([S,i^{\pm}_{p-i_{N}-1,n}])}D_{n}^{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
are marked with
C^{p-k-1}_{n}(1)
for
k<i_{N}
, so in particular they all share a common
j
boundary for
j\leq p-1-i_{N}<p-1-k
by the observations above.
∎

Construction 6.6
.

Given a string
S=l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}},...,l_{i_{N}}
marked with
C_{n}^{n-i_{N}-1}(1)
and a cell
i_{\sigma_{N}}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}^{n-i_{N}-1}(1)
, which together define an edge in
T_{m}(D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
, denote by
\widetilde{S}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}},...,l_{i_{N}}^{\sigma_{N}}
the extended string and associate to it a triple
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),M)
, where
\delta_{i}\in\{-,*,+\}
and
\delta_{i}=\pm
if and only if
\widetilde{S}
contains the term
l^{\pm}_{n-i}
,
t_{0}=0
and the indices
t_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq v
are all the indices for which
\delta_{t_{k}}\neq*
,
s_{i}\leq q_{i+1}
counts the number of extremal terms
l^{*}_{n-i-1}
(note that this number is greater than 0 only if
\delta_{i}\neq*
) and
M\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\max(t_{v}-i_{N}-1,0)
. Given
m<n
and
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
as above denote by
\tau_{\leq m}x
to be the pair
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{m}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),M^{\prime})
, where
v^{\prime}
is the maximal index for which
t_{v^{\prime}}\leq m
and
M^{\prime}=\max(M-m,0)
.

Proposition 6.7
.

(1)

6.6
establishes an isomorphism between the set of edges of
T_{m}(D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
and the set of triples
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),M)
described in
6.6
such that additionally if for some
i
we have
s_{i}=q_{i}
, then all
\delta_{j}=*
for
j>i
and
M=0
;

(2)

a pair
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
corresponds to an elementary cell of
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
if
M=0
and either
s_{k}=q_{k+1}
for some
k
or
\delta_{n}\neq*
;

(3)

the set of elementary
(n-1)
-cells of
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
is isomorphic to the set of triples
(n-1,x,\sigma)
, where
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
corresponds to an elementary
n
-cell and
\sigma\in\{-,+\}
is such that
\sigma=\delta_{n}
if
\delta_{n}\neq*
;

(4)

more generally, the set of elementary
l
-cells of
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
is isomorphic to the set of elementary
l
-cells in
D_{l+1}(q_{1},...,q_{l+1})
;

(5)

given an elementary
l
-cell
y
and ann elementary
k
-cell
z
for
l<k
we have
y<z
if and only if
y
belongs to
d_{l}^{\pm}(\tau_{\leq l+1}z)
, where
\tau_{\leq l+1}z
denotes the subcomplex
\tau_{\geq t(\tau_{\leq l+1}z)}D_{l+1}(q_{1},...,q_{l+1})
of
D_{l+1}(q_{1},...,q_{l+1})
.

Proof.

We start by proving (
1
): note that any
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),M)
corresponding to an edge in
T_{m}(D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
satisfies the condition of (
1
) by (
4
). Conversely, given
x
we can define a string

S\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}s_{0}*S_{n-1}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(S^{\delta_{t_{j}}}_{n-t_{j}}+s_{t_{j}}*S_{n-t_{j}-1})+S_{+},

where
+
denotes the concatenation of strings,
m*S_{k}
denotes the string
l_{0}^{*}l_{!}^{*}...l_{k}^{*}
repeated
m
times,
S^{\pm}_{k}
denotes the string
l_{0}^{*}...l_{k}^{\pm}
and the term
S_{+}
is either
l_{0}^{\pm}
if
\delta_{n}=\pm
or the string
l_{0}^{*}...l^{*}_{t_{v}-M}
if
\delta_{n}=*
, it is easy to see that this defines an inverse to the map of
6.6
.

To prove (
2
) note that a string
\widetilde{S}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}l_{i_{0}}^{\sigma_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}^{\sigma_{N}}
corresponds to an elementary cell if and only if it cannot be extended to a string
S^{\prime}=l_{i_{0}}^{\sigma_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}^{\sigma_{N}}l_{i_{N+1}}
satisfying the conditions of
6.4
, note that this is only the case if either
i_{N}=0
and
\sigma_{N}\in\{-,+\}
(since then
2
can not be satisfied) or
l_{i_{N}}^{\sigma_{N}}
is the
q_{n-i_{N}}
th extremal term
l_{i_{N}}
(in which case the condition
4
fails), and in both cases we have
M=0
, under the isomorphism of (
1
) those conditions clearly correspond to the ones described in (
2
). In what follows we will drop
M=0
from the notation for an elementary
n
-cell and denote it simply
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{)},...,s_{t_{v}}))
.

For (
3
) note that by construction every unmarked
(n-1)
-cell in
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
lies in the boundary of an unmarked
n
-cell, recall from the proof of
6.5
that
C_{n}^{n-i_{k}-1}(1)
contains 3 elementary
n
-cells
\{-,*,+\}
and for
i_{k}>0
their
(n-1)
-boundaries are all distinct, while for
i_{k}=0
we have
d^{+}_{n-1}c_{n}^{-}=d^{-}_{n-1}c_{n}^{*}
and
d^{-}_{n-1}c_{n}^{+}=d^{+}_{n-1}c_{n}^{*}
. We may encode this information by a pair
(\sigma_{k},\sigma)
, where
\sigma\in\{-,+\}
such that
\sigma=\sigma_{k}
if
i_{k}=0
and
\sigma_{k}\in\{-,+\}
. Combining this with the previous description of
n
-cells we see that an elementary
(n-1)
-cell can be identified with the data
(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
satisfying the conditions of (
3
).

To prove the claim about the
l
-cells observe that

(95)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n}}(c_{l},C_{n}^{i}(1))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n}}(c_{l},c_{n})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{l+1}}(c_{l},c_{l+1})\text{ for $i>l$}

and

(96)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n}}(c_{l},C^{i}_{n}(1))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{l+1}}(c_{l},C^{i}_{l+1}(1))\text{ for $i\leq l$}.

Given an object
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
denote by
X_{l}\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}^{h}
the object with the same underlying tree such that the string
l^{\sigma_{0}}_{i_{0}}...l^{\sigma_{k}}_{l_{k}}
is marked with
C^{n-i_{k}-1}_{l+1}(1)
if
i_{k}\geq n-l-1
and with
c_{l+1}
otherwise, then it follows from (
95
) and (
96
) that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}^{\mathrm{int}}}(c_{l},D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Stein}_{l+1}^{\mathrm{int}}}(c_{l},X).

Note that by contracting all the edges of
X_{l}
marked with
c_{l+1}=c_{l+1}
we arrive at the isomorphism
X_{l}\cong D_{l+1}(q_{1},...,q_{l+1})
, which proves (
4
).

Finally, given an elementary
(k+1)
cell
z\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{k+1}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
denote by
z_{l+1}
the object
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{k+1}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),0)
, where by definition
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\leq l+1
and
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=*
for
i>(l+1)
and
v^{\prime}
is the maximal index for which
t_{v^{\prime}}\leq l+1
, then
z_{l+1}
defines a subcomplex
\tau_{\geq z_{l+1}}D_{k+1}(q_{1},...,q_{k+1})
and it follows from (
95
) that
d^{\pm}_{l}z=d^{\pm}_{l}\tau_{\geq z_{l+1}}D_{k+1}(q_{1},...,q_{k+1})
. It remains to prove that
d^{\pm}_{l+1}\tau_{\geq z_{l+1}}D_{k+1}(q_{1},...,q_{k+1})=d^{\pm}_{l}\tau_{\leq l+1}z
, which follows from (
96
).
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Given
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta_{1},..,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),M)
encoding an edge in
T_{m}(D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
we have an isomorphism

(97)

d^{\sigma}_{n-1}(\tau_{\geq t(x)}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))\cong D^{M}_{n-1}(1,0,...,0)

such that the elementary
(n-1)
-cells appearing in
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}(\tau_{\geq t(x)}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
are of the form
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}i_{n}
for elementary
n
-cells
i_{n}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
corresponding to pairs
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},..,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}))
such that either
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i\neq\sigma
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}=s_{t_{v}}+1
and
\delta_{n}=\sigma
if
s_{t_{v}}+1<q_{t_{v}}
and
\delta_{n}=*
otherwise or
v^{\prime}=v+1
,
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\notin\{t^{\prime}_{v+1},n\}
,
n-1-M\leq t^{\prime}_{v+1}\leq n-1
,
s_{t^{\prime}_{v+1}}=0
and
\delta_{n}=\sigma
.

Proof.

Note that
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}C_{n}^{n-i_{s}-1}(1)=C^{n-i_{s}-1}_{n-1}(1)
if
i_{s}>0
and
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)=c_{n-1}
, moreover note that if a node
c
is marked with
C_{n}^{n-1}(1)
, then

(98)

d^{\sigma}_{n-1}\tau_{\geq c}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})\cong d^{\sigma}_{n-1}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)\cong c_{n-1}

since the boundary
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}i_{n}^{*}
of the marked cell
i_{n}^{*}:c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)
is disjoint from the boundary
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}C_{n}^{n-1}(1)
. Denote by
X\in\mathpzc{Tree}_{n}
the object whose underlying subtree is obtained from
T\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}T_{m}(\tau_{\geq t(e_{S})}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))
by removing all nodes in
T_{m}(\tau_{\geq x}T)
marked with
C_{n}^{n-1}(1)
and whose markings are induced from
\tau_{\geq t(e_{S})}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
, then it follows from (
98
) that
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}(\tau_{\geq t(e_{S})}D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n}))\cong d^{\sigma}_{n-1}X.

Finally, observe that the underlying tree of
X
is a linear tree with
M
nodes
\{1,...,M\}
such that the node
s
is marked with
C_{n}^{M-s}(1)
, from which the claim easily follows.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

The partially ordered set
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})}
has objects given by triples
(l,x,\sigma)
, where
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{r(l)}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
and
r(l)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\min(n,l+1)
, given
(k,y,\sigma^{\prime})
with
k>l
and
y=((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{r(k)}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}))
, denote by
u
the maximal index
j
such that
t^{\prime}_{j}\leq r(l)
, then
x<y
if and only if one of the following mutually exclusive conditions holds:

(1)

x=(l,\tau_{\leq r(l)}y,\sigma)
;

(2)

\delta_{r(l)}=\sigma
,
v=u+1
,
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\neq t_{v}
and
s_{t_{i}}=s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}
for
i\leq u

(3)

v=u
,
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\leq r(l)
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=s_{t_{i}}
for
i<v
and
s_{t_{v}}=s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}+1
.

Moreover, for
p\leq n
we have
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D^{p}_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{p})}
is a Cartesian fibration over
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})}
whose fiber over
c_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}D_{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})
is
*
if
i<p
and
\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n-p/c_{n-p}}
if
i=p
.

Proof.

The first claim follows immediately from
Lemma

6.8
and (
5
) of
6.7
, to prove the second claim note that by definition we have
T_{m}(D^{p}_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))\cong T_{m}(D_{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p}))
, but in
D^{p}_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{p})
the cell
l_{i_{0}}^{\sigma_{0}}...l_{i_{n}}^{\sigma_{N}}
is marked with
C_{n}^{p-i_{N}-1}(1)
while in
D_{p}(q_{1},...,q_{p})
it is marked with
C_{p}^{p-i_{N}-1}(1)
, the claim now follows since the natural surjective morphism
C_{n}^{k}(1)\rightarrow C_{p}^{k}(1)
for
k<p
induces a Cartesian fibration
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/C_{n}^{k}(1)}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{p,/C_{p}^{k}(1)}
whose fiber over
c_{i}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}C_{p}^{k}(1)
is
*
if
i<p
and
\Theta^{\mathrm{el}}_{n-p/c_{n-p}}
if
i=p
.
∎

Definition/Proposition 6.10
.

The assignment
(q_{1},...,q_{p})\mapsto D^{p}_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{p})
extends to a functor
\Delta^{\times n}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, moreover
D_{n}(f_{1},...,f_{n})
is active unless
f_{1}(0)>0
and if
i^{r}
is an inert morphism preserving the maximal element, then
D_{n}(i^{r},\mathrm{id},...,\mathrm{id})
is inert.

Proof.

Our strategy for defining the morphism
D(f)
for any morphism
f\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(f_{1},...,f_{p}):(q_{1},...,q_{p})\rightarrow(d_{1},...,d_{p})
would be to first define a functor

D(f):\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/D^{p}_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{p})}\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/D^{p}_{n}(d_{1},...,d_{p})})

and then to prove it lies in the image of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,//D^{p}_{n}(d_{1},...,d_{p})}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/D^{p}_{n}(d_{1},...,d_{p})})
. It follows from the second claim of
6.9
that we may assume
n=p
, which we will do from now on.

Note that any morphism
f
in
\Delta^{\times n}
decomposes uniquely as
f\cong j\circ s
for a componentwise injective
j
and surjective
s
. We will start by describing
D_{n}(j)
for some injective
j
with components
j_{i}:[q_{i}]\rightarrow[p_{i}]
.
We will start by describing the image of an elementary
n
-cell
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
: assume first that
\delta_{n}=*
, then there is some index
k
such that
s_{k}=q_{k+1}
, we define its image under
D(j)_{\Omega}
to contain all
n
-cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\leq k
,
s^{\prime}_{i}=j(s_{i})
for
i<k
and
s^{\prime}_{k}\geq j(s_{k})
, as well as their
t
-boundaries for
t<n
. If
\delta_{n}=\pm
(so in particular
s_{i}<q_{i+1}
for all
i
) we define the image of
x
to contain all
n
-cells
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
and their
t
-boundaries for
t<n
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
and
j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}<j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}}+1)
for
0\leq i\leq v
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i^{\prime}}(0)
(if
j_{i^{\prime}}(0)=0
, we take it to mean
\delta_{i^{\prime}}=*
) for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
. We will now describe the image of an
(n-1)
-cell
x^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
: assume first that
\delta_{n}=*
and denote by
k
the index for which
s_{k}=q_{k+1}
, in that case if
j(q_{k+1})=p_{k+1}
we define
j(x^{\prime})
to be
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
, where
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
and
s^{\prime}_{i}=j(s_{i})
for all
i
; if
j(q_{k+1})<p_{k+1}
then we define its image to be
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
with
s^{\prime}_{i}=j(s_{i})
for all
i
and
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i<n
with
\delta^{\prime}_{n}=\sigma
. Assume now that
\delta_{n}=\sigma
, in that case we define its image to contain
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
and their
t
-boundaries for
t<(n-1)
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}=j(s_{t_{v}})
,
j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}<j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}}+1)
for
0\leq i<v
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i^{\prime}}(0)
for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
. Finally, for
l<(n-1)
an
l
-cell is defined by the data
(l,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{l+1}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
, this can be identified with an
l
-cell in
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{l+1})
, so we can use the formulas above wo define its image in
D_{l+1}(p_{1},...,p_{l+1})
, which has the same
l
-cells as
D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})
.

We first need to show that this indeed defines a morphism

D(j):\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})}\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})}),

in other words we need to show that if some
l
-cell
i_{l}
for
l<k
lies in the boundary of a
k
-cell
i_{k}
, then
D(j)(i_{l})<D(j)(i_{k})
. By construction it suffices to prove this for
l=(n-1)
and
k=n
, assume first that we have an
n
-cell
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},..,s_{t_{v}}))
as above such that
\delta_{n}=*
, its boundary consists of two
(n-1)
-cells
(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},..,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
, by construction
D(j)(x)
is just
\tau_{\geq j(x)}D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})
, where
j(x)
denotes the object
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(j_{0}(s_{0}),...,j_{t_{v}}(s_{t_{v}})))
viewed as a node in
T_{n}(D(p_{1},...,p_{n}))
, then it follows from
Lemma

6.8
that
D(j)(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},..,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
is exactly the
\sigma
-boundary of
D(j)(x)
. Now assume that
\delta_{n}\neq*
and
\sigma=\delta_{n}
, in that case note that the
(n-1)
-cell
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}=j(s_{t_{v}})
,
j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}<j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}}+1)
for
i<v
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i^{\prime}}(0)
for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
that lies in the image of
(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},..,s_{t_{v}}),\sigma)
is isomorphic to
d^{\sigma}_{n-1}y^{\prime}
for the
n
-cell
y^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}})
which lies in the image of
x
by construction. Finally, by
6.9
it remains to consider the cases of cells
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime\prime}_{0},..,s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime\prime}}}),\sigma)
such that either
v^{\prime\prime}=v+1
,
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\neq t_{v^{\prime\prime}}
,
s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{i}}=s_{t_{i}}
for
i\leq v
and
s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime\prime}}}=0
or
v^{\prime\prime}=v
,
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for all
i
and
s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{i}}=s_{t_{i}}
for
i<v
and
s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v}}=s_{t_{v}}+1
with
s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v}}=q_{t_{v}}
or
\delta_{n}=\sigma
. Consider the first case, then the image of
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime\prime}_{0},..,s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime\prime}}}),\sigma)
would contain
(n-1)
-cells
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
for
i\leq v+1
,
j_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{k}}<j_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}}+1)
for
k\leq v
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{v+1}}=j_{t_{v+1}}(0)
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i^{\prime}}(0)
for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
, note that such an
(n-1)
-cell lies in the boundary of an
n
-cell
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
which lies in the image of
x
. In the last case the image of
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime\prime}_{0},..,s^{\prime\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime\prime}}}),\sigma)
contains
(n-1)
-cells
(n-1,(\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}),\sigma)
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
for
i\leq v
,
j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}<j_{t_{i}}(s_{t_{i}}+1)
for
i<v
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}=j_{t_{v}}(s_{t_{v}}+1)
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<j_{i^{\prime}}(0)
for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
, note that such an
(n-1)
-cell lies in the boundary of an
n
-cell
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
which lies in the image of
x
.

We have thus shown that
j
defines a morphism
D(j):\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})}\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})})
, denote by
D(j)_{\Omega}:\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})})\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\Omega}(\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})})
its unique pushout-preserving extensions, we will now show that
D(j)_{\Omega}
is functorial injective morphisms, i.e. that for a composable pair
(q_{1},...,q_{n})\xrightarrow{j_{0}}(p_{1},...,p_{n})\xrightarrow{j_{1}}(d_{1},...,d_{n})
we have
D(j_{1}\circ j_{0})_{\Omega}\cong D(j_{1})_{\Omega}\circ D(j_{0})_{\Omega}
. More explicitly, we need to show that for any
l
-cell
x
we have

D(j_{1}\circ j_{0})(x)\cong\bigcup_{y\in D(j_{0})(x)}D(j_{1})(y),

where the union is taken over all elementary cells in
D(j_{0})(x)
. By construction it suffices to prove the claim for
l=n
and
l=(n-1)
, assume first that
x
is an
n
-cell
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
, by construction it suffices to show that every
n
-cell in
D(j_{1}\circ j_{0})(x)
is in the image of some
n
-cell in
D(j_{0})(x)
. Assume first that
\delta_{n}=*
and fix
k
such that
s_{k}=q_{k+1}
, then the image of
x
contains the
n
-cells
z\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
such that
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\leq k
,
s^{\prime}_{i}=j_{1}\circ j_{0}(s_{i})
for
i<k
and
s^{\prime}_{k}\geq j_{1}\circ j_{0}(s_{k})
, given such a cell define
y
to be the
n
-cell
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s^{\prime\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime\prime}_{t(v)}))
such that
s^{\prime\prime}_{i}=j_{0}(s_{i})
for
i\leq k
, then clearly
z
lies in the image of
y
. Now assume that
\delta_{n}=\pm
, then by construction the image of
x
under
j_{1}\circ j_{0}
contains
z
as above such that
\delta^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=\delta_{t_{i}}
and
(j_{1}\circ j_{0})_{i}(s_{t_{i}})\leq s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}<(j_{1}\circ j_{0})_{i}(s_{t_{I}}+1)
for
i\leq v
and
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}<(j_{1}\circ j_{0})_{i^{\prime}}(0)
for
i^{\prime}\neq t_{i}
. In that case denote by
t^{\prime}_{k}
for
0\leq k\leq v^{\prime}
the indices for which
s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}\geq j_{1}(0)
(in particular they include all
t_{i}
), note that for each
k
we also have
s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}\leq j_{1}(p_{t^{\prime}_{k}})
, so in particular we can find
s^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}
such that
j_{1}(s^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}})_{t^{\prime}_{k}}\leq s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}<j_{1}(s^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}+1)_{t^{\prime}_{k}}
, we can then define
y\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}((\delta^{\prime\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}))
, where
s^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}
are as defined above and
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}=\delta^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}_{k}}
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{i^{\prime}}=*
for
i^{\prime}\neq t^{\prime}_{k}
, then
z
lies in the image of
y
. In the case of
(n-1)
-cell
x^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(n-1,x,\sigma)
, where
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...s_{t_{v}}))
and
z^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(n-1,z,\sigma)
in the image of
x^{\prime}
under
D(j_{1}\circ j_{0})
, it is easy to see that
y^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(n-1,y,\sigma)
, where
y
is an
n
-cell constructed above such that
D(j_{1})(y)
contains
z
, defines an
(n-1)
-cell such that
z^{\prime}
belongs to
D(j_{1}(y^{\prime})
.

We now need to prove that
D(j)
factors through
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})}
, for which we will represent its image as an iterated pushout of representables. Note that we can decompose
j
as
j_{1}\circ...\circ j_{n}
, where each
j_{k}
denotes the morphism
(\mathrm{id},...,j_{k},...,\mathrm{id})
with the unique non-trivial component in position
k
, by the functoriality proved in the previous paragraph it suffices to prove the claim for each
j_{i}
individually. We can further decompose
j_{k}\cong i^{l}_{k}\circ i^{t}_{k}\circ a_{k}
, where
a_{k}
is an active morphism,
i_{k}^{r}
is the inert morphism preserving the maximal element and
i_{k}^{l}
an inert morphism preserving the minimal element, again it suffices to prove the claim for each of them. We will start with
i^{l}_{k}
, in that case by definition the image of an elementary
l
-cell is either an elementary
l
-cell or
\tau_{\geq c}D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})
for some node
c
of
T_{m}(D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{q}))
, in both cases those lie in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})}
– in the first case this is immediate, in the second follows from
5.12
. Also note that the induced morphism is active: it suffices to prove that every elementary
n
-cell lies in the image of
D(i^{l})
, for that note that
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
lies in the image of
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
if
k\neq t_{i}
for any
i
and in the image of
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,\min(s_{k},q_{k}),...,s_{t_{v}}))
otherwise. Now consider the case of an active morphism: given
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
with
\delta_{n}=*
, its image is again representable, so we may assume
\delta_{n}\in\{-,+\}
. In that case its image contains
n
-cells
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}))
such that
s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=a(s_{t_{i}})
unless
t_{i}=k
, in which case
a(s_{k})\leq s^{\prime}_{k}<a(s_{k}+1)
, as well as all their boundaries. Note that this object is representable if
a(s_{k}+1)=a(s_{k})+1
, in general we may argue by induction on
(a(s_{k}+1)-a(s_{k}))
: denote
y\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}a(x)
and
y^{\prime}
the subset of
y^{\prime}
containing
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(a(s_{0}),...,s^{\prime}_{k},...,a(s_{t_{v}})))
with
a(s_{k})\leq s^{\prime}_{k}<a(s_{k}+1)-1
, then
y^{\prime}\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,=_{n})}
by induction and
y\cong x^{\prime}\coprod_{d^{\sigma}_{n-1}x^{\prime}}y^{\prime}
, where
x^{\prime}
denotes the
n
-cell
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(a(s_{0}),...,a(s_{k}+1)-1,...,a(s_{t_{v}})))
, hence
y\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,=_{n})}
as well. Given an
(n-1)
-cell
(n-1,x,\delta_{n})
, note that its image is representable by
(n-1,((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{1}),(a(s_{0}),...,a(s_{k}),...,a(s_{t_{v}}))),\sigma)
. Note again that the image of
D(a)
contains all the elementary
n
-cells:
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
lies in the image of
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
if
k\neq t_{i}
for any
i
, if not then we can find some index
s^{\prime}_{k}
such that
a(s^{\prime}_{k})\leq s_{k}<a(s_{k}+1)
(since
a
is active), and then
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
lies in the image of
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{k},...,s_{t_{v}}))
. Finally, consider the case of
i^{r}
, then the image of
x
is again representable if
\delta_{n}=*
, so we may assume
\delta_{n}=\pm
. In that case the image
y
of
x
contains
n
-cells
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{k},...,s_{t_{v}}))
with
s^{\prime}_{k}<i^{r}(0)
, once again we can prove the claim by induction on
i^{r}(0)
: if it is 0, then this object is representable, if not we can consider
y^{\prime}
containing
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s^{\prime\prime}_{k},...,s_{t_{v}}))
with
s^{\prime\prime}_{k}<i^{r}(0)-1
, so that we have
y\cong x^{\prime}\coprod_{d^{\sigma}_{n-1}x^{\prime}}y^{\prime}
, where
x^{\prime}
denotes the cell
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,i^{r}(0),...,s_{t_{v}}))
. Similarly, for the
(n-1)
-cell
(n-1,x,\delta_{n})
we see that its image under
D(i^{r})
is again representable. Note also that if
k>0
, then
D(i^{r})
is active:
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
lies in the image of
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
if either
k\neq t_{i}
for any
i
or
s_{k}\geq i^{r}(0)
, if not then it lies in the image of
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},...,s^{\prime}_{t_{v-1}}))
, where
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\neq k
,
\delta_{k}=*
and
s^{\prime}_{t_{j}}=s_{t_{j}}
for all
t_{j}\neq k
. Finally, note that if
k=0
, then
D(i^{r})
is isomorphic to the inert inclusion
\tau_{\geq x}D_{n}(q_{1},q_{2},...,q_{n})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n}(p_{1},q_{2},...,q_{n})
, where
x=((*,...,*),(i^{r}(0),0,...,0))
.

This concludes the proof in the special case of injective morphisms, assume now that
f=b=(b_{1},...,b_{n})
with surjective
b_{i}
, once again it suffices to define the image of
n
and
(n-1)
-cells. Given an
n
-cell
x=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(s_{0},...,s_{t_{v}}))
denote by
k
the minimal index for which
(s_{t_{k}}+1)\leq q_{t_{k}}
and
b_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}}+1)=b_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}})
- if such an index does not exist we define the image of
x
to be
((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(b_{0}(s_{0}),...,b_{t_{v}}(s_{t_{v}})))
. Assuming it does, note that there must be some index
r>k
such that
\delta_{r}\neq*
, define
t
to be the minimal such index, we define the image of
x
to be the
(t-1)
-cell
(t-1,((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{t}),(b_{0}(s_{0}),...,b_{k}(s_{k})+1)),\delta_{t})
. Similarly, for the
(n-1)
-cell
(n-1,x,\sigma)
we define its image to be
(n-1,b(x),\sigma)
if there is no
k
such that
b_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}}+1)=b_{t_{k}}(s_{t_{k}})
and
b(x)
otherwise. We need to prove that it is well-defined: it is clear from construction that for an
(n-1)
-cell
(n-1,x,\sigma)
we have
b(n-1,x,\sigma)\leq b(x)
, so by
6.9
it suffices to consider two other cases: if
\delta_{n}=\sigma
and
y=(n-1,x^{\prime},\sigma)
with
((\delta^{\prime}_{1},...,\delta^{\prime}_{n}),(s^{\prime}_{0},..,s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}))
such that
v^{\prime}=v+1
,
\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\delta_{i}
for
i\neq t_{v^{\prime}}
,
s^{\prime}_{t_{i}}=s_{t_{i}}
for
i\leq v
and
s^{\prime}_{t_{v^{\prime}}}=0
, then by construction
b(y)=b(x)
since
t_{v^{\prime}}>t_{v}\geq t
. In the other case we have
v^{\prime}=v
,
\delta_{i}=\delta^{\prime}_{i}
and
s^{\prime}_{t_{v}}=s_{t_{v}}+1
, in that case we immediately have
b(y)=b(x)
if
t_{n}>k
, in the case
k=t_{v}
we must consider two separate cases: if
b_{k}(s_{k}+1)=b_{k}(s_{k}+2)
, then immediately by construction we see that
b(x)=b(x^{\prime})=b(y)
, if on the other hand
b_{k}(s_{k}+2)=b_{k}(s_{k})+1
we see that
b(x^{\prime})=((\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(b_{0}(s_{0}),...,b_{k}(s_{k})+1))
and
b(x)=b(y)=(n-1,(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{n}),(b_{0}(s_{0}),...,b_{k}(s_{k})+1),\delta_{n})
. Since the images of elementary cells are representable, it automatically follows that
b
factors through
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/D_{n}(p_{1},...,p_{n})}
and it is easy to see that
D(b\circ b^{\prime})=D(b^{\prime})\circ D(b)
. Finally, it is easy to see by construction that
D(b)
is active.

It remains to complete the proof of functoriality of
D(f)
, to do that it remains to prove that for any factorization
s\circ j=j^{\prime}\circ s^{\prime}
we have
D(s\circ j)=D(j^{\prime}\circ s^{\prime})
, however using the construction of
D(s)
and
D(j)
we see that this claim follows from the commutativity of the following square for all
l<n
and any injective
j
:

Construction 6.11
.

Denote by
F_{s_{1}}(s_{2},...,s_{n});\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[s_{1}]}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
the functor sending
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[s_{1}]
to
D_{n}(l,s_{2},...,s_{n})
and an inclusion
[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m]
to the induced functor
D(i):D_{n}(l,...,s_{n})\rightarrow D_{n}(l,...,s_{n})
, note that it follows from
6.10
that
D(i)
is inert if
i
preserves the minimal element. Given a morphism
f:[s_{1}]\rightarrow[s^{\prime}_{1}]
we have an induced functor
f_{!}:\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[s_{1}]}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[s^{\prime}_{1}]}
together with a natural transformation
\alpha_{f}:F_{s_{1}}(s_{1},...,s_{n})\rightarrow f^{*}F_{s^{\prime}_{1}}(s^{\prime}_{1},...,s_{n})
whose component at
F_{s_{1}}(s_{1},...,s_{n})([l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[m])\cong D_{n}(l,...,s_{n})
is
D(a):D(l,...,s_{n})\rightarrow D(l^{\prime},...,s_{n})
, where
a
appears in the factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[s_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[s^{\prime}_{1}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We can view
F_{s_{1}}(s_{2},...,s_{n})
as a value at
(s_{2},...,s_{n})
of a functor
F_{s_{1}}:\Delta^{\times(n-1)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[s_{1}]},\mathpzc{Stein}_{n})
, where a morphism
g:(s_{2},...,s_{n})\rightarrow(s^{\prime}_{2},...,s^{\prime}_{n})
gets sent to the natural transformation
\beta_{g}
with components
D_{n}(l,s_{2},...,s_{n})\xrightarrow{D_{n}(\mathrm{id},g_{2},...,g_{n})}D_{n}(l,s^{\prime}_{2},...,s^{\prime}_{n})
- that this defines a natural transformation follows easily from the functoriality of
6.10
. Similarly. it is easy to see that for any
f
and
g
as above and
i:[l]\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}[s_{1}]
we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(l,s_{2},...,s_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(l^{\prime},s_{2},...,s_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(l,s^{\prime}_{2},...,s^{\prime}_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(l^{\prime},s^{\prime}_{2},...,s^{\prime}_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}

Given
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
and
[q]\in\Delta
denote by
*_{\mathscr{E}}
the constant functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
with value
\mathscr{E}
, then we define
\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})([q])\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-1),\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
to be the functor sending
(s_{2},...,s_{n})
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}.\mathrm{Cat}_{n})}(F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n}),*_{\mathscr{E}})
, where
\Delta^{\times(n-1),\mathrm{op}}
acts by precomposition. By functoriality in the first variable described above we can view
\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})
as a functor

\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-1)},\mathscr{S}),

we define
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
as a composition

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-1)},\mathscr{S})\xrightarrow{\underset{\Delta^{\times(n-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}}\mathscr{S}.

Proposition 6.12
.

\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
satisfies the Segal condition.

and the construction of
D_{n}(q_{1},...,q_{n})
that we have pushout diagrams

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

By an iterated application of (
99
) we see that the functor
F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n}):\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
described in
6.11
is the left Kan extension of its restriction to
\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}
, since that also holds for
*_{\mathscr{E}}
we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})([q])(s_{2},...,s_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat})}(i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}i^{\mathrm{el},*}F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n}),i^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}i^{\mathrm{el},*}*_{\mathscr{E}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat})}(i^{\mathrm{el},*}F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n}),i^{\mathrm{el},*}*_{\mathscr{E}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]},\mathrm{Cat})}(i^{*}i^{\mathrm{el},*}F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n}),i^{*}i^{\mathrm{el},*}*_{\mathscr{E}})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}}{\lim}\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})([e])(s_{2},...,s_{n}),

where the first isomorphism follows by definition and our preceding observations, the second since
i^{\mathrm{el}}
is fully faithful, the third since

\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[q])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[q]}}{\mathrm{colim}}\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[e]}

and the last again by definition. Finally, note that
\mathrm{TwAr}_{D}(\mathscr{E})([0]):\Delta^{\times(n-1)}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is a constant functor, so we can conclude by the same argument as in
6.2
.
∎

Proposition 6.13
.

For any pair of morphisms
f:c_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
and
g:c_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})}(f,g)
is the geometric realization of an
(n-1)
-tuple Segal space.

Proof.

By construction
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})}(f,g)
is the colimit of a functor
\Delta^{\times(n-1)}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
(s_{2},...,s_{n})
to the space of cospans

We need to prove that it satisfies the Segal condition separately in each variable, for that it suffices to prove that

D_{n}(1,i_{2},...,i_{k-1},s_{k},...,s_{n})\cong\underset{([e]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[s_{k}])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[s_{k}]}}{\mathrm{colim}}D_{n}(1,i_{2},...,i_{k-1},e,...,s_{n})

for every
k
and all
i_{j}\in\{0,1\}
. This clearly follows by an iterated application of the following observation: for every
2\leq t\leq n
we have a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(1,s_{2},...,1,...,s_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(1,s_{2},...,s_{t}-1,...,s_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}(1,s_{2},...,s_{t},...,s_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

in
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
, which in turn follows from the definition of
D_{n}(-)
and
Lemma

5.11
.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Assume we are given a
t
-active morphism
f:c_{t+1}\rightarrow x
for some
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
, denote by
C
the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
containing
y\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
for which
d^{-}_{t+1}y=\mathrm{Im}(f)
and
d^{+}_{t+1}y=d^{+}_{t+1}x
, then
C
is contractible.

Proof.

We have seen in the proof of
Lemma

3.11
that there is a morphism
F:c_{n}\rightarrow x
whose image contains all elementary cells
b
for which there exists a basis element
b^{\prime}
in the image of
f
such that
b\geq_{t+1}b^{\prime}
, it is easy to see that
\mathrm{Im}(F)\in C
and that it is a final object of this category.
∎

Proposition 6.15
.

For
x\in\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
we have

\mathrm{TwAr}(x)\cong\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}.

Proof.

First, note that for any pair of morphisms
f:c_{n}\rightarrow x
and
g:c_{n}\rightarrow x
such that
\mathrm{Im}(f)\leq\mathrm{Im}(g)
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n/x}
the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(x)}(f,g)
is non-empty – this follows from
3.12
. It remains to prove that it is contractible.

We will first need to introduce some notation. Call two morphisms
f:c_{n}\rightarrow x
and
g:c_{n}\rightarrow x

k
-parallel for
0\leq k<n
if they have the same
k
-boundary, we will also call any two morphisms (-1)-parallel by convention. Next, define
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})
for
(s_{1},...,s_{p})\in\Delta^{\times p}
and
p\leq l
by induction on
k
as follows: for
k=0
we set
\Sigma^{0}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})
, for
k\geq 1
we define
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})
to be the
(k+l)
-category with two objects
\{0,1\}
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})}(0,1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Sigma^{k-1}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p}).

Note that
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}c_{l}\cong c_{k+l}
. It follows from the definition and
6.10
that the assignment
(s_{1},...,s_{p})\mapsto\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{p})
extends to a functor from
\Delta^{\times p}
to
\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
. Note also that
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{l}(s_{1},...,s_{l})\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
: indeed, it can be represented by a tree with nodes given by strings
S=l_{i_{0}}^{\sigma_{0}}...l_{i_{N}}
with
0\leq i_{j}<p
and
\sigma_{j}\in\{-,*,+\}
satisfying the conditions described in
6.4
such that the node corresponding to
S
is marked with
C^{k+p-i_{N}-1}_{n}(1)
.

Define

F^{k}_{s_{1}}([m]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[s_{1}],s_{2},...,s_{n-k})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(m,s_{2},...,s_{n-k}),

then by the same argument as in
6.11
this extends to a functor
F^{k}_{s_{1}}:\Delta^{(n-k-1)}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[s_{1}]}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Stein}_{n}
and we define
\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}_{D}(x)([q])\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-k-1),\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
to be the functor sending
(s_{2},...,s_{n-k})
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}.\mathrm{Cat}_{n})}(F_{q}(s_{2},...,s_{n-k}),*_{x})
, where
\Delta^{\times(n-1),\mathrm{op}}
acts by precomposition. By functoriality in the first variable we can view
\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}_{D}(x)
as a functor

\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}_{D}(x):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-k-1)},\mathscr{S}),

we define
\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
as a composition

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}_{D}(x)}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\times(n-k-1)},\mathscr{S})\xrightarrow{\underset{\Delta^{\times(n-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}}\mathscr{S}.

The same argument as in
6.12
then shows that this is a Segal space.

It is easy to see by construction that for a pair of
n
-morphisms
f
and
g
as above we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x)}(f,g)=\varnothing

unless they are
(k-1)
-parallel, we will prove by downward induction on
k
that for any
(k-1)
-parallel morphisms
f
and
g

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x)}(f,g)
is either empty or contractible, and the latter holds if and only if
\mathrm{Im}(f)\leq\mathrm{Im}(g)
in
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
- for
k=0
this will prove the proposition. We start with the case
k=n-1
, in that case it is easy to see that
\Sigma^{n-1}_{\theta}D_{1}(s)\cong C_{n}^{n-1}(s)
, so that we have
\mathrm{TwAr}^{n-1}(x)=\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}^{n-1}(x)
in the notation of
4.7
. The claim now follows either by untangling the definitions or using
4.17
.

Assume we have proved the claim for
(k+1)
, we will proceed to prove it for
k
. First, we will need some more notation: observe that
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,0,...,0)
has
(2(n-k)+1)
elementary cells corresponding to the strings
l_{0}^{*}...l_{t}^{\pm}
for
t\leq(n-k-1)
and
l_{0}^{*}...l_{n-k-1}^{*}
, we will denote by
i_{t}^{\sigma}:e_{t}^{\sigma}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,...,0)
for
0\leq t\leq n-k-1
with
\sigma\in\{-,+\}
for
t<n-k-1
and
\sigma\in\{-,*,+\}
otherwise the inclusions of those elementary cells. It follows from
Lemma

6.8
that for
t<(n-k-1)
we have a pushout square

(100)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n-1-t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-1-t}(1,0,...,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{t}^{\pm}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n-k-1-t}^{\mp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

and for
t=(n-k-1)
we have
e^{\sigma}_{n-k-1}=c_{n}
. For any
(s_{2},...,s_{n-k})
we have an active morphism

a:\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n}(1,...,0)\overset{(\mathrm{id},\{0\},...,\{0\})}{\twoheadrightarrow}\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,s_{2},...,s_{n-k}),

it is easy to see from the construction in
6.10
that the restriction of
a
to the elementary cells of dimension
<n
is equal to identity, denote by
A^{\sigma}_{t}(s_{n-k-t+1},...,s_{n-k})
the image of the elementary cell
e^{\sigma}_{t}
under
a
, we then have the pushout diagram

(101)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n-1-t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-1-t}(1,0,...,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{n-t}_{\theta}D_{t}(s_{n-k-t+1},...,s_{n-k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{t}^{\pm}(s_{n-k-t+1},...,s_{n-k})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n-k-1-t}^{\mp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

By
3.21
we see that

(102)

\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,...,s_{n-k})\cong\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,...,0))\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n}(1,...,0)}}{\mathrm{colim}}X_{e},

where
X_{e}
denotes the image of
e
under
a
, so
X_{e}\cong e
if
\dim(e)<n
and
X_{e_{t}^{\sigma}}=A_{t}^{\sigma}(s_{n-k-t+1},...,s_{n-k})
.

Note that for any
p\leq n-k
we have an inert morphism
\mathscr{I}_{p}:D^{p}_{n-k}(1,0,...,0)\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}D_{n-k}(1,...,0)
whose image contains the cells
e^{\sigma}_{t}
with
t\geq n-k-p
for
p\geq 1
and just the cell
e^{*}_{n-k-1}
for
p=0
, we will denote

Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\underset{(e\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{n-k}(1,...,0))\in\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{n}(1,...,0)}}{\mathrm{colim}}X_{e},

where we identify
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D^{p}_{n}(1,...,0)}
with a full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n}(1,...,0)}
by means of
\mathscr{I}_{p}
described above, note that any morphism
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(\mathrm{id},g):\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,...,s_{n-k})\rightarrow\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,...,s_{n-k})
restricts to an
(k+p-1)
-active morphism
Y_{p}(\mathrm{id},g):Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})\rightarrow Y_{p}(1,...,s^{\prime}_{n-k})
. By definition
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x)}(f,g)
is a geometric realization of the category with objects given by diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(1,s_{2},...,s_{n-k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with morphisms induced by morphisms
\Sigma^{k}_{\theta}D_{n-k}(\mathrm{id},g)
for
g:(s_{2},...,s_{n-k})\rightarrow(s^{\prime}_{2},...,s^{\prime}_{n-k})
making the diagram commute. We will define
Z_{p}(f,g)
to be the geometric realization of the category with objects given by commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{p}(1,s_{2},...,s_{n-k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{p}}

for which the composition
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y_{p}(1,s_{2},...,s_{n-k})\xrightarrow{F}x
is
(k+p-1)
-parallel to
g
, with morphisms given by
Y(\mathrm{id},g)
that make the diagram commute. Note that by definition
Z_{n-k}(f,g)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x)}(f,g)
and that the inclusion
Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}Y_{p^{\prime}}(1,...,s_{n-k})
for
p^{\prime}>p
induce morphisms
Z_{p^{\prime}}(f,g)\xrightarrow{\gamma_{p,p^{\prime}}}Z_{p}(f,g)
. We will prove that all
Z_{p}(f,g)
are isomorphic to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{k}(x)}(f,g)
by downward induction on
p
, starting with the trivial case
p=n-k
. Note that
Y_{0}(1,...,s_{n-k})\cong c_{n}
, meaning that
Z_{0}(f,g)
is a singleton, so proving this claim would conclude the proof of the proposition.

Assume we have proved the claim for
p
, observe that there is an active morphism
a_{p}:C_{n}^{k+p-1}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\Sigma^{k}D^{p}_{n-k}(1,0,...,0)
sending the cells
i^{\pm}_{k+p-1}
to the elementary cells corresponding to the strings
l_{0}^{\pm}
and
i^{*}_{k+p-1}
to the composition of all other cells, composing it with
\Sigma^{k}D^{p}_{n-k}(1,0,...,0)\overset{a\circ\mathscr{I}_{p}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})
gives us a morphism
C_{n}^{k+p-1}(1)\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})
, using
3.21
we see that
Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})
is isomorphic to the colimit of the diagram

(103)
