Note that by definition
Z_{p}(f,g)
is the colimit over
(s_{2},...,s_{n-k})\in\Delta^{\times(n-k-1)}
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p}(Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k}),x)
, where
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p}(Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k}),x)
denotes the subset of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k}),x)
containing morphisms
h
for which the composition
c_{n}\overset{}{\rightarrowtail}Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})\xrightarrow{h}x
is isomorphic to
f
and
c_{n}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y_{p}(1,...,s_{n-k})\xrightarrow{h}x
is
(k+p-1)
-parallel to
g
. Using (
103
) we can rewrite it as

(104)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p},x)\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(D_{p+k-1},x)}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1},x)\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(D_{p+k-1},x)}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p},x)

(where we have omitted the variables
s_{i}
for typographical reasons), where
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{\pm}(A^{\pm}_{n-k-p},x)
denotes the subset of morphisms

A^{-}_{n-k-p}(s_{p+1},...,s_{n-k})\xrightarrow{h_{\pm}}x

whose image contains
d^{\pm}_{k+p-1}\mathrm{Im}(g)
and
d^{\mp}_{k+p-1}\mathrm{Im}(Y_{p-1}(1,...,s_{n-k}))
. Note that for any morphism
v:(s_{p+1},...,s_{n-k})\rightarrow(s^{\prime}_{p+1},...,s^{\prime}_{n-k})
the morphisms
A^{\pm}_{n-k-p}(v)
and
Y_{p-1}(\mathrm{id},v)
define a natural transformation of diagrams (
103
). Denote
\overline{s}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(s_{2},...,s_{p})
,
\widetilde{s}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(s_{p+1},...,s_{n-k})
and by
B^{\pm}
the terms
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(D_{p+k-1}(1,...,0),x)
appearing in (
104
), we will now consider the variables
\overline{s}
as fixed and calculate the colimit of (
104
) over
\widetilde{s}
:

\displaystyle\underset{\widetilde{s}\in\Delta^{\times(n-k-p-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s}),x)\times_{B^{-}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1}(\overline{s},\widetilde{s}),x)\times_{B^{+}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s}),x)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(\widetilde{s^{-}},\widetilde{s^{*}},\widetilde{s^{+}})\in\Delta^{\times 3(n-k-p-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{-}}),x)\times_{B^{-}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1}(\overline{s},\widetilde{s^{*}}),x)\times_{B^{+}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{+}}),x)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\widetilde{s^{-}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{-}}),x)\times_{B^{-}}\underset{\widetilde{s^{*}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1}(\overline{s},\widetilde{s^{*}}),x)\times_{B^{=}+}\underset{\widetilde{s^{+}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{-}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{+}}),x),

where the first isomorphism follows since
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is sifted and the second since products distribute over colimits in the topos
\mathscr{S}_{/B_{0}\times B_{1}}
. It now remains to prove that

\underset{\widetilde{s^{\pm}}\in\Delta^{\times(n-k-p-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{\pm}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{\pm}}),x)\cong B^{\pm},

since then using the previous equation we would get

\displaystyle Z_{p}(f,g)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(\widetilde{s},\overline{s})\in\Delta^{\times(n-k),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}B^{-}\times_{B^{-}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1}(\overline{s},\widetilde{s^{*}}),x)\times_{B^{+}}B^{+}

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(\widetilde{s},\overline{s})\in\Delta^{\times(n-k),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{p-1}(Y_{p-1}(\overline{s},\widetilde{s^{*}}),x)\cong Z_{p-1}(f,g).

Now fix some
g^{\prime}_{\pm}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(D_{p+k-1}(1,0,...,0),x)
and denote by
X^{\pm}
the fiber of

\underset{\widetilde{s^{\pm}}\in\Delta^{\times(n-k-p-1),\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}^{-}(A^{\pm}_{n-k-p}(\widetilde{s^{\pm}}),x)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(D_{p+k-1}(1,0,...,0),x)

over
g^{\prime}_{\pm}
, so we now need to prove that
X^{\pm}
is contractible. Denote by
g_{\pm}
the composition

c_{p+k-1}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}D_{p+k-1}(1,0,...,0)\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}_{\pm}}x,

note that
g_{\pm}
is
(p+k-2)
-active. It follows from (
101
) and the definitions that
X^{\pm}
is isomorphic to the geometric realization of the subcategory of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{k+p+1}(x)
(which we identify with a subcategory of
\mathpzc{Stein}^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/x}
using the inductive assumption on
k
) containing morphisms
y\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}x
for which
d^{\pm}_{p+k-1}y\cong d^{\pm}_{p+k-1}\mathrm{Im}(g)
and
d^{\mp}_{p+k-1}y\cong\mathrm{Im}(g_{\pm})
, however we have seen in
Lemma

6.14
that this category is contractible.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})

for any
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

Sending the natural transformation
\alpha:F_{s_{1}}(s_{2},...,s_{n})\rightarrow*_{\mathscr{E}}
from
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})([s_{1}])
to itself viewed as an object of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([s_{1}])
defines a functor
F:\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
, we need to prove that this defines an isomorphism. Since both the source and target of
F
satisfy the Segal condition, it suffices to prove that it induces an isomorphism on the spaces of objects and of morphisms. That
F
induces an isomorphism on the space of objects is clear, to prove the second claim recall that the space of morphisms
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})}(f,g)
between
f,g:c_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is isomorphic to the geometric realization of the category
C(f,g)
of cospans

(105)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

over
\mathscr{E}
with
x\in\mathpzc{Tree}^{h}_{n}
and morphisms are induced by
f:x\rightarrow y
over
\mathscr{E}
, while
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})}(f,g)
is the geometric realization of the subcategory
\mathscr{I}:c^{\prime}\hookrightarrow C
containing objects given by diagrams (
105
) with
x=D_{n}(1,s_{2},...,s_{n})
and morphisms by
D(\mathrm{id}_{[1]},f_{2},...,f_{n})
with
f_{i}:[s_{i}]\rightarrow[s^{\prime}_{i}]
, to prove the claim it suffices to prove that
\mathscr{I}
is cofinal. Fix some object
G
represented by diagram (
105
), then it is easy to see that
|(\mathscr{I}/G)|
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(x)}(i,a)
, and this latter space is contractible by
6.15
.
∎

Corollary 6.17
.

We have

\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta).

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

6.3
,
6.16
and
4.18
.
∎

Definition 6.18
.

For
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}
define
L_{\mathscr{E}}
to be the constant functor
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
with value
\mathbb{S}
, given
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
define

L_{f}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{CoKer}(f_{!}L_{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow L_{\mathscr{D}}).

Theorem 6.19
.

A morphism
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is an isomorphism if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

\tau_{\leq n+1}f:\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{D}
is an isomorphism;

(2)

L_{f}\cong 0
.

Proof.

It follows from
4.19
that it suffices to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp})\cong\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\mathscr{E}})
and that the cotangent complex
\mathscr{E}
of
6.18
is isomorphic to the one defined in
4.19
. To prove the first claim note that it follows from
4.12
that it suffices to prove that

(106)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp}),

6.16
further implies that we may replace
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
with
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})
in (
106
). Note that there is a natural morphism
F:\mathrm{TwAr}_{\theta}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})
sending the natural transformation
\alpha:C_{q}^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow*_{\mathscr{E}}
of functors
\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[q]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
, where
*_{\mathscr{E}}
is the constant functor with value
\mathscr{E}
and
C^{\mathrm{int}}_{q}
is defined in
4.3
, corresponding to an element of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\theta}(\mathscr{E})([q])
, to itself considered as an element of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\prime}(\mathscr{E})([q])
. We need to prove that this is an isomorphism, by the combination of
Lemma

4.2
,
Lemma

6.3
,
6.15
and
4.18
it suffices to prove this if
\mathscr{E}\cong\theta
, in which case it follows from
Lemma

4.11
. To prove the second claim observe that under the isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E}),\mathrm{Sp})\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{E}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta),\mathrm{Sp})

L_{\mathscr{E}}
corresponds to the collection of
f^{*}L_{\mathscr{E}}\cong L_{\theta}:\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta)\cong\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}
of constant functors with values
\mathbb{S}
, it is easy to see by tracing through various isomorphisms that this is isomorphic to
L_{\theta}
defined in
4.19
.
∎

Corollary 6.20
.

Given a morphism
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
we have
L_{f}\cong 0
if
f
is coinitial.

Proof.

By construction
L_{\mathscr{E}}\cong f^{*}L_{\mathscr{D}}
, hence

L_{f}\cong\mathrm{CoKer}(f_{!}f^{*}L_{\mathscr{D}}\rightarrow L_{\mathscr{D}}),

the claim now follows because
f_{!}f^{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
for coinitial morphisms.
∎

Proposition 6.21
.

Assume that
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is such that
L_{f}\cong 0
and
\tau_{\leq n+1}f
induces a monomorphism

\tau_{\leq n+1}f^{*}:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}}(\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{D},A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}}(\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{E},A)

for any
A\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}
, then
f^{*}
is also a monomorphism and moreover we have a pullback square

(107)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}}(\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{D},\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(n+1,n)}}(\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{E},\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathscr{A})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\leq n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\leq n+1}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\leq n+1}}

for any
\mathscr{A}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
.

Proof.

We will prove that (
107
) is a pullback square and
f^{*}
is a monomorphism for
A\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(m,n)}
by induction on
m
, starting with the case
m=n+1
where this follows by assumption. Assume we have proved the claim for
m
and
A\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(m+1,n)}
, then we can form a pullback square

(108)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\leq n+1}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\leq m}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{m+1}\mathrm{H}\pi_{m}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

using
[
12
, Theorem 5.2.]
. We can then form the following diagram

(109)

we will prove that every square in it is a pullback, for that we will use the
\infty
-categorical pasting law for pullbacks of
[
20
, Lemma 4.4.2.1.]
without further mention. The square
(d)
in (
109
) is obtained by applying
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{E},-)
to (
108
), hence it is a pullback. Applying
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},-)
to it we see that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},\tau_{\leq n+1}A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},\tau_{\leq m}A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}(L_{\mathscr{D}},\Sigma^{m+1}\mathrm{H}\pi_{m}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

is a pullback, however using that
L_{\mathscr{E}}\cong L_{\mathscr{D}}
by assumption and
\tau_{\leq m}f^{*}
is a monomorphism by induction, we see that the rectangle
(bd)
is also a pullback, meaning that the square
(b)
must now be a pullback too. This prove that (
107
) is a pullback and also immediately implies that the rectangle
(ce)
in (
109
) is a pullback, the square
(e)
is a pullback by induction, hence the square
(c)
must be a pullback too. Note that the outer square
(abcd)
is a pullback since it is obtained by applying
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{n}}(\mathscr{D},-)
to (
107
), since we have already seen that
(bd)
is a pullback this implies that the rectangle
(ac)
is a pullback. Finally, combining this with the earlier observation that the square
(c)
is a pullback we obtain that the square
(a)
is a pullback, which means exactly that
f^{*}
is a monomorphism.
∎

Example 6.22
.

Despite the formidable definition of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
, the idea behind it is quite simple: its objects are
n
-morphisms
X
in
\mathscr{E}
and the space of morphisms is such that its points correspond to decompositions

X\cong A^{n-1}_{-}*_{n-1}(A^{n-2}_{-}*_{n-2}(...*_{1}(A^{0}_{-}*_{0}Y*_{0}A^{0}_{+})*_{1}...)*_{n-2}A^{n-2}_{+})*_{n-1}A^{n-1}_{+}

and the paths in this space correspond to similar decompositions of various
A^{k}_{\pm}
. In this example we will provide explicit descriptions of this category in low dimensions:

(1)

if
\dim(\mathscr{E})=1
, then
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
is simply the ordinary twisted arrows category with objects given by morphisms
f:x\rightarrow y
and morphisms by diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with source
g
and target
f
;

(2)

in dimension 2 the objects of
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{E})
are 2-morphisms
\alpha
and morphisms are given by the geometric realization of a category with objects given by diagrams

(110)

\hbox to241.94pt{\vbox to60.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 120.96996pt\lower-28.88055pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-120.96996pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{}
{

7.
Lax-idempotent monads

Given a monad on an
\infty
-category, it is generally quite difficult to describe its category of algebras since giving an object a structure of an algebra required an infinite amount of ”coherence data”. One exception to this are idempotent monads – the category of algebras for an idempotent monad is simply a full subcategory of the category in question. As similar effect can be observed in
(\infty,2)
-category – consider for example the category
\mathrm{Cart}(\mathscr{C})
of Cartesian fibrations over
\mathscr{C}
, it admits a forgetful functor to
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
and this functor is monadic. Yet the category
\mathrm{Cart}(\mathscr{C})
admits a relatively simple description – by the results of
[
20
, Section 2.4.]
it includes those functors
F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
for which every morphism
x\xrightarrow{g}F(y)
lefts to a Cartesian morphism
F^{*}x\xrightarrow{g_{*}}y
and a functor
G:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
between the objects of
\mathrm{Cart}(\mathscr{C})
defines a morphism of algebras precisely if it preserves Cartesian morphisms.

This features are possessed more generally by
lax-idempotent
monads introduced in
[
16
]
, which include free (co)fibrations monads as well as free (co)completions. A monad
T
is lax idempotent in the unit morphism
T\xrightarrow{Ts}TT
is left adjoint to the multiplication
m:TT\rightarrow T
and an object
x
admits a (unique) structure of an algebra if and only if
x\xrightarrow{s}Tx
admits a left adjoint. All of those facts are proved in
[
16
]
in the setting of ordinary categories, however to the knowledge of the author none of those facts have been generalized to
\infty
-categories.

In this final section we will use deformation theory developed in the previous section to generalize some of the results mentioned above. A proper treatment of this subject will probably require a separate work, so we will limit ourselves to characterizing lax-idemoptent monads as those for which multiplication is adjoint to units.

Lemma 7.1
.

The categories of the form
[n]\in\Delta
admit all limits and colimits and moreover any morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
preserves limits and colimits of non-empty categories.

Proof.

Note that
[n]
admits both an initial and a final object, meaning that it admits both a limit and a colimit of an empty diagram. Now suppose we have a functor
F:S\rightarrow[n]
, in that case
\mathrm{colim}F
must be an object of
[n]
such that for
i\in[n]
we have
i\geq\mathrm{colim}F
if and only if
i\geq F(s)
for all
s\in S
. It follows that we can set

(115)

\mathrm{colim}F\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sup_{s\in S}F(s),

it is easy to see that such an object always exists. Similarly, we have

(116)

\lim F\cong\inf_{s\in S}F(s),

note that any order preserving functor necessarily preserves maxima and minima, from which the last claim of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Given a morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
, it admits a left adjoint (as a functor in
\mathrm{Cat}
) if and only if it preserves the minimal element and a right adjoint if and only if it preserves the minimal element, moreover when they exist the adjoints are given by

(117)

f^{L}(x)\cong\underset{x\leq f(i)}{\inf}i

and

(118)

f^{R}(x)\cong\underset{x\geq f(i)}{\sup}i.

Proof.

Note that for all categories of the form
[n],[m]\in\Delta
and any functor
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
, it satisfies the solution set condition of
[
23
, Definition 3.2.1.]
(since
[n]
and
[m]
are themselves small, and hence so are all
f_{i/}
for
i\in[m]
). Since
[n]
and
[m]
are moreover complete and cocomplete by
Lemma

7.1
, we can apply the generalized adjoint functor theorem of
[
23
]
which states that
f
admits a left (resp. right) adjoint if and only if it preserves all (co)limits. BY the second claim of
Lemma

7.1
,
f
always preserves non-empty limits and colimits, so it preserves all (co)limits if and only if it preserves the maximal (minimal) object.

Finally, to prove the last claim observe (by untangling the constructions of loc. cit.) that for
F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
satisfying the conditions of
[
23
, Theorem 3.2.5.]
, its left adjoint is given by

(119)

F^{L}(c)\cong\underset{(c\xrightarrow{f}Fd)\in F^{\prime}_{c/}}{\mathrm{colim}}d,

where
F^{\prime}_{c/}\hookrightarrow F_{c/}
is the small weakly initial subcategory (which exists by the solution set condition), and a dual statement holds for the right adjoint. Equations (
117
) and (
118
) now follow from (
119
) and its dual together with (
116
) and (
115
).
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

For a morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
the following claims are equivalent:

(1)

f
is surjective;

(2)

f
admits a left adjoint and
f\circ f^{L}\cong\mathrm{id}
;

(3)

f
admits a right adjoint and
f\circ f^{R}\cong\mathrm{id}
;

(4)

f
admits a left adjoint and
f^{L}
is injective;

(5)

f
admits a right adjoint and
f^{R}
is injective;

(6)

f
induces a comonadic adjunction;

(7)

f
induces a monadic adjunction.

Proof.

Assume we have a left-adjoint morphism
j:[l]\rightarrow[p]
, we claim that it is injective if and only if
j^{R}\circ j\cong\mathrm{id}
. Indeed, if
j
is injective then using (
118
) we get

j^{R}j(x)\cong\underset{j(i)\geq j(x)}{\sup}j(i)\cong\underset{i\in j^{-1}(j(x))}{\sup}j(i)\cong j(x).

Conversely, if
j^{R}\circ j\cong\mathrm{id}
, then for all
x\in[l]
the fiber
j^{-1}(j(x))
is non-empty and
x
is its maximal element, which is obviously equivalent to
j
being injective. Dualizing this argument, we see that a right-adjoint morphism
j
is injective if and only if
j^{L}\circ j\cong\mathrm{id}
.

If follows from this (applied to
f^{L}
and
f^{R}
respectively) that
\eqref{it:sur2}\Leftrightarrow\eqref{it:sur3}
and
\eqref{it:sur2'}\Leftrightarrow\eqref{it:sur3'}
. We will now prove the equivalence of
(
5
)
and (
7
): by
[
18
, Theorem 4.7.3.5.]

f^{R}
is monadic if and only if it is conservative and preserves colimits of
f^{R}
-split simplicial object. The second condition is automatic in light of
Lemma

7.1
, so it remains to observe that a functor
g:[m]\rightarrow[n]
is conservative if and only if it is injective. Dualizing this argument we obtain
\eqref{it:sur3}\Leftrightarrow\eqref{it:sur4}
.

Finally, note that if
f
is surjective, then it admits both a left and a right adjoint by
Lemma

7.2
and moreover we have

f\circ f^{R}(x)\cong\underset{i\in f^{-1}(x)}{\sup}f(i)\cong x

and

f\circ f^{L}(x)\cong\underset{i\in f^{-1}(x)}{\inf}f(i)\cong x,

so (
1
) implies (
2
) and (
3
). Conversely, if
f
admits a left (resp. right) adjoint and
f\circ f^{L}\cong\mathrm{id}
(resp.
f\circ f^{R}\cong\mathrm{id}
), it follows that the fibers
f^{-1}(x)
are non-empty for
x\in[m]
, meaning that
f
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Given a pair
f,g:[n]\rightrightarrows[m]
of active morphisms and a pair
u,v:[p]\rightrightarrows[q]
of active morphisms together with
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
and
\beta:u\rightarrow v
, denote by
C
the category with object given by diagrams of active morphisms

(120)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[p]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[q]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

such that
f\leq a\circ u\circ s\leq b\circ v\circ t\leq g
and morphisms given by pairs

(121)

(s\leq s^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t,a\leq a^{\prime}\leq b^{\prime}\leq b),

then the geometric realization of
C
is either empty or contractible.

Proof.

Note that
C
admits a natural forgetful functor to
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],[p]))
given by sending the diagram (
120
) to
s\leq t
and a morphism (
121
) to
s\leq s^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
, we claim that it is a Cartesian fibration. Indeed, given (
120
) and
(s\leq s^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t)
, it is clear that it admits a Cartesian lifting with source

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We now claim that the fibers of this fibration are either empty or contractible. Indeed, assume we are given a diagram (
120
), note first that since all morphisms are presumed active, they admit left and right adjoints by
Lemma

7.2
. In particular, we can consider
f\circ s^{R}\circ u^{R}:[q]\rightarrow[m]
, however it is not in general active, so we define

u^{\prime}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}0\text{ if $i=0$}\\
u^{R}(i)\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

and

s^{\prime}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}0\text{ if $i=0$}\\
s^{R}(i)\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(note that
s^{R}
and
u^{R}
preserve maximal elements as right adjoints). In that case
a_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}f\circ s^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}
is an active morphism, moreover if for
x\in[n]
we have
u\circ s(x)=0
, then

f(x)\leq a\circ u\circ s(x)=0=a_{0}\circ s\circ u(x),

and if
u\circ s(x)>0
, then

f(x)\leq f\circ(u\circ s)^{R}\circ(u\circ s)(x)=f\circ s^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ u\circ s(x),

where the first inequality follows by adjointness and the second by definition, so in general
f\leq a_{0}\circ u\circ s
. Similarly we have
a_{0}(0)=0=a(0)
and for
i>0
we have

a_{0}(i)=f\circ s^{R}\circ u^{R}(i)\leq a\circ u\circ s\circ s^{R}\circ u^{R}\leq a,

where the first equality follows by definition, the first inequality since
f\leq a\circ u\circ s
by assumption and the last inequality by adjointness. Similarly, we can define

v^{\prime}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}p\text{ if $i=q$}\\
v^{L}(i)\text{ otherwise}\end{cases},

t^{\prime}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}n\text{ if $i=p$}\\
t^{L}(i)\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

and set
b_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}g\circ t^{\prime}\circ v^{\prime}
, then by dual arguments one can show
g\geq b_{0}\circ v\circ t
and
b\leq b_{0}
. It follows that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[p]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[q]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

defines the final object of the fiber over
s\leq t
, so in particular it is contractible.

The geometric realization of
C
is then isomorphic to the geometric realization of the full subcategory
D\hookrightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],[p]))
on such
s\leq t
that there exist some diagram of the form (
120
), our next goal is to determine the conditions on
s\leq t
for this fiber to be non-empty. The above considerations imply that if such a diagram exists, then there is one with
a\leq b
substituted with
a_{0}\leq b_{0}
in the notation above, so
(s\leq t)\in D
if and only if
f\leq a_{0}\circ u\circ s
,
g\geq b_{0}\circ v\circ t
and
a_{0}\leq b_{0}
. We claim that this is equivalent to the condition

(122)

g^{L}fs^{R}u^{R}v\leq t^{L}\leq s^{L}.

Indeed, by definition
a_{0}\leq b_{0}
is equivalent to the condition

(123)

fs^{R}u^{R}(i)\leq gt^{L}v^{L}(i)

for
0<i<q
. Additionally, to ensure
f\leq a_{0}\circ u\circ s
we must have
f(x)=0
for
x\leq s^{R}u^{R}(0)
and to ensure
g\geq b_{0}\circ v\circ t
we similarly need
g(y)=m
for
y\geq gt^{L}v^{L}(q)
, however those two conditions are equivalent to requiring that (
123
) holds for all
i
. Finally, note that (
123
) is equivalent to the first inequality in (
122
) by properties of adjoints and
s\leq t
is equivalent to the second.

We first focus on the condition for the category of
(s,t)
satisfying (
122
) to be non-empty, note that this is equivalent to

(124)

g^{L}fs^{R}u^{R}v\leq s^{L}

since if (
124
) holds, then the pair
(s,s)
satisfies (
122
). Denote
F\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}u^{R}v:[p]\rightarrow[p]
and
G\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}g^{L}f:[n]\rightarrow[n]
, note that
F=u^{R}v\geq u^{R}u\geq\mathrm{id}
and
G=g^{L}f\leq f^{l}f\leq\mathrm{id}
. Define a sequence
a_{i}\in[p]
inductively by
a_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}0
and
a_{i+1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\min(p,F(a_{i})+1)
; since
F\geq\mathrm{id}
we have
a_{i+1}>a_{i}
unless
a_{i}=p
, define
N_{F}
to be the least index such that
F(a_{N_{F}})=p
. Similarly, define
b_{0}=0\in[n]
and
b_{i+1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\min(n,G^{R}(b_{i})+1)
(note that
G\leq\mathrm{id}
preserves the minimal element, hence admits a right adjoint by
Lemma

7.2
), again define
N_{G}
to be the least index such that
G^{R}(b_{N_{G}})=n
. We claim that:

(*)

Category
D
is non-empty if and only if
N_{G}\leq N_{F}
.

We will also need the following observation: assume we have

(125)

s^{R}(x)\leq y

for some
x\in[p]
and
y\in[n]
, if
s^{R}(x)=p
then (
125
) is equivalent to
y=n
, we claim that for
s^{R}(x)<p
(
125
) is equivalent to

(126)

x<s(y+1)\Leftrightarrow x+1\leq s(y+1).

Indeed, using (
118
) we immediately see that
s^{R}(x)<p
is the least
z\in[n]
such that
s(z+1)>x
, from which the claim follows.

We claim that we have

(127)

a_{i}\leq s(b_{i})

if
s^{R}F(a_{i-1})<n
: we will prove the claim by induction, it holds for
i=0
since
s
is active. Assume we have proved (
127
) for some
i
, then we have a series of inequalities

(128)

s^{R}F(a_{i})\leq s^{R}(F(s(b_{i})))\leq G^{R}s^{L}s(b_{i})\leq G^{R}b_{i},

where the first inequality follows from (
127
), the second from (
124
) (and the definition of
G
and
F
) and the last one is the counit for
s
. Since we have assumed that
s^{R}F(a_{i})<n
, this is equivalent to

a_{i+1}=F(a_{i})+1\leq s(G^{R}(b_{i})+1)=s(b_{i+1})

by (
126
). Assume now that
s^{R}F(a_{i})=n
, note that this is equivalent to
F(a_{i})=p
, then we still have (
128
), but now they imply
G^{R}(b_{i})=n
. It follows that
N_{G}\leq N_{F}
, so the ”only if” part of (
(*)
‣
7
) holds.

Conversely, assume
N_{G}\leq N_{F}
, then define
\epsilon_{F}\in\{0,1\}
to be 0 if
a_{N_{F}}=p
and 1 otherwise and denote
m_{F}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}N_{F}+\epsilon_{F}
, define
j_{F}:[m_{F}]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[p]
to be the morphism such that
j_{F}(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}a_{k}
, note in particular that it is injective and active, and similarly define
j_{G}:[N_{G}+\epsilon_{G}]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[n]
, also denote
i_{0}:[m_{G}]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m_{F}]
the morphism such that
i_{0}(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}k
for
k<m_{G}
and
i_{0}(m_{G})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}m_{F}
, so in particular it is injective and active; we claim that
s_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}j_{F}\circ i_{0}\circ j^{R}_{G}
belongs to
D
(note that
j_{G}^{R}(0)=j_{G}^{R}(j_{G}(0))=0
since
j_{G}
is injective, so in particular
s_{0}
is active). We need to check that

(129)

G\circ s_{0}^{R}\circ F(x)\leq s_{0}^{L}(x)

for all
x\in[p]
. Denote
k\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}j_{F}^{R}(F(x))
, then
i_{0}^{R}\circ j_{F}^{R}(x)\leq k
and hence

j_{G}^{RR}\circ i_{0}^{R}\circ j_{F}^{R}(x)\leq j_{G}^{RR}(k)=b_{k+1}-1=G^{R}(b_{k}),

so

(130)

G\circ s_{0}^{R}\circ F(x)\leq G(G^{R}(b_{k}))\leq b_{k}.

We claim that
x>a_{k-1}
: indeed, if
x\leq a_{k-1}
, then

F(x)\leq F(a_{k-1})<F(a_{k-1})+1=a_{k},

which contradicts
j_{F}^{R}(F(x))=k
. It follows that
j_{F}^{L}(x)\geq k
, hence

s_{0}^{L}(x)=j_{G}\circ i_{0}^{L}\circ j_{F}^{L}(x)\geq j_{G}\circ i_{0}^{L}(k)\geq j_{G}(k)=b_{k},

combined with (
130
) this implies that (
129
) holds, proving the ”if” direction of (
(*)
‣
7
).

We conclude the proof by showing that
D
is contractible whenever it is non-empty. Assume we have
(s\leq t)\in D
, then in particular we have
s(0)=t(0)=0=s_{0}(0)
in the notation above, denote by
D_{i}
for
i\in[n]
the full subcategory of
D
on
s
and
t
such that
s(k)=t(k)=s_{0}(k)
for
k\leq i
, we will prove by induction that
|D_{i}|\cong|D|
- we have seen that this holds for
i=0
, observe that
D_{n}\cong\{s_{0}\}
, so this will prove that
D
is contractible. Assume first that
b_{k}<i<b_{k+1}
for some
k
, denote by
D_{i}^{S}
the subcategory of
D_{i-1}
on
(s,t)
such that additionally
s(i)=s_{0}(i)
, we claim that
D_{i}^{s}\hookrightarrow D_{i-1}
admits a right adjoint. Indeed, define
s^{\prime}\leq t
by setting
s^{\prime}(x)=s(x)
for
x\neq i
and
s^{\prime}(i)=a_{k}
, we need to show that it still satisfies (
122
). we have
t^{L}\leq s^{{}^{\prime},L}
since
s^{\prime}\leq t
and
s^{{}^{\prime},R}(x)=s^{R}(x)
unless
x\in[a_{k},s(i)]
, in which case
s^{{}^{\prime},R}(x)=i\geq s^{R}(x)
, where
s^{R}(x)
is either
i
or
i-1
. Assume now
F(y)\in[a_{k},s(i)]
, then
y>a_{k-1}
, so in particular
t^{L}(y)\geq i>b_{k}
, while
Gs^{{}^{\prime},R}F(y)=G(i)\leq G(b_{k+1}-1)=G(G^{R}(b_{k}))\leq b_{k}
, where the first inequality follows from
i<b_{k+1}
and the second is the counit. This implies
(s^{\prime}\leq t)\in D
, by construction it further belongs to
D^{s}_{i}
, we have a morphism
s^{\prime}\leq s\leq t=t
and it is clearly a final morphism in
D^{s}_{i,/(s,t)}
, so
(s,t)\mapsto(s^{\prime},t)
is indeed a right adjoint in this case. Assume now that
i=b_{k+1}
, by (
127
) we have
s(i)\geq a_{k+1}
, once again define
s^{\prime}(i)=a_{k+1}
and
s^{\prime}(x)=s(x)
for
x\neq i
. We have
s^{\prime R}(x)=s^{R}(x)
unless
x\in[a_{k+1},s(i)]
, in which case
s^{{}^{\prime},R}(x)=b_{k+1}\geq s^{R}(x)
, where
s^{R}(x)
is either
i
or
i-1
. Assume now
F(y)\in[a_{k+1},s(i)]
, then
y>a_{k}
, so in particular
t^{L}(y)\geq b_{k+1}
, while
Gs^{{}^{\prime},R}F(y)=G(b_{k+1})\leq b_{k+1}
since
G\leq\mathrm{id}
. By the same reasoning, we see that
(s,t)\mapsto(s^{\prime},t)
defines the required right adjoint.We will now prove that
D_{i}^{s}\hookrightarrow D_{i}
admits a left adjoint: given
(s\leq t)
define
t^{\prime}(i)=s_{0}(i)
and
t^{\prime}(x)=t(x)
for
x\neq i
, then we have
t^{\prime}(i)\leq t(i)
, so in particular
t^{{}^{\prime},L}\geq t^{L}\geq G\circ s^{R}\circ F
, but we also have
t^{\prime}\geq s
since
s(i)=s_{0}(i)
because
s\in D_{i}^{s}
. It follows that
(s\leq t^{\prime})\in D_{i}
and we have a morphism
s=s\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
, it is easy to see this defines a left adjoint. Finally, since left and right adjoints induce isomorphisms on geometric realizations, we have
|D|\cong|D^{s}_{i}|\cong|D_{i}|
concluding the proof.
∎

Notation 7.5
.

Denote by
\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}
the monoidal category
\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}
with monoidal structure given by
[n]\otimes[m]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[n+m]
viewed as a bicategory with one object and by
\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}}
the monoidal
bicategory

\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}
with the same monoidal structure and bicategory structure induced from
\mathrm{Cat}
viewed as a tricategory with one object.

Proposition 7.6
.

The category
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}})
is a singleton.

Proof.

By using (
6.22
) in dimension 3 we see that
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}})
has objects given by parallel morphisms
f,g:[n]\rightrightarrows[m]
with
f\leq g
and that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}})}((u,v),(f,g))
is the geometric realization of a double category

for which

is the category with objects given by diagrams

(131)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[r^{0}_{0}+p+r^{1}_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[r^{2}_{0}+q+r^{3}_{0}]}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1,0}^{0}+v+c_{1,0}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0,0}^{0}+u+c_{0,0}^{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that

Morphisms in

are given by pairs of strings
(s^{\prime}\leq s\leq t\leq t^{\prime},a^{\prime}\leq a\leq b\leq b^{\prime})

. Morphisms in

are given by collections of strings

and

.

Denote by
p_{1}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
the projection to the second factor, in order to describe
B^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}p_{1,!}B:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
we will first need to introduce some notation: given
f,g:[n]\rightrightarrows[m]
with
f\leq g
, denote
F_{f,g}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}f^{R}\circ g:[n]\rightarrow[n]
and set
a_{0}^{f,g}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}0
and

, define
N_{F_{f,g}}
to be the minimal
i
such that
F(a_{i}^{f,g})=n
. In that case using

7.4
and specifically condition (
(*)
‣
7
) we see that

is in fact a poset whose objects are pairs
(c_{0},d_{0}:[r_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w_{0}],c_{1},d_{1}:[r_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w_{1}])
such that
c_{i}\leq d_{i}
and
N_{f,g}\leq N_{c_{0}+u+d_{0},c_{1}+v+d_{1}}
and such that
(c_{0},d_{0}:[r_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w_{0}],c_{1},d_{1}:[r_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w_{1}])\leq(c^{\prime}_{0},d^{\prime}_{0}:[r^{\prime}_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w^{\prime}_{0}],c^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{1}:[r^{\prime}_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w^{\prime}_{1}])
if and only if
N_{c_{0},d_{0}}\leq N_{c^{\prime}_{0},d^{\prime}_{0}}
and
N_{c_{1},d_{1}}\leq N_{c^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{1}}
.

To complete the proof of the claim it suffices to show that
B^{\prime}
is contractible: indeed, this would imply that the space of morphisms between any two objects in
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}})
is contractible, which proves that it is isomorphic to
*
. To show this we will in fact prove that
B^{\prime}
is filtered. Since
B^{\prime}
is a poset, it suffices to show that for any two objects
x_{0}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c_{0},d_{0}:[r_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w_{0}],c_{1},d_{1}:[r_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w_{1}])
and
x_{1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c^{\prime}_{0},d^{\prime}_{0}:[r^{\prime}_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w^{\prime}_{0}],c^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{1}:[r^{\prime}_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w^{\prime}_{1}])
there is some
y
such that
y\geq x_{0}
and
y\geq x_{1}
, however it is easy to see that we can take
y
to be
(c_{0}+c^{\prime}_{0},d_{0}+d^{\prime}_{0}:[r_{0}+r^{\prime}_{0}]\rightrightarrows[w_{0}+w^{\prime}_{0}],c_{1}+c^{\prime}_{1},d_{1}+d^{\prime}_{1}:[r_{1}+r^{\prime}_{1}]\rightrightarrows[w_{1}+w^{\prime}_{1}])
.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

For any functor
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}}
the induced functor
\mathrm{TwAr}(F):\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}})
is coinitial if and only if
\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{C})
is contractible.∎

Lemma 7.8
.

For any
\mathscr{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{n}
we have

|\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{D})|\cong\mathrm{TwAr}(|\mathscr{D}|)\cong|\mathscr{D}|.

Proof.

Note that
|-|
preserves colimits as a left adjoint, hence it follows from
6.17
that

\displaystyle|\mathrm{TwAr}(\mathscr{D})|

\displaystyle\cong|\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{D})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{D}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{D})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{D}}}{\mathrm{colim}}|\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{D})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{D}}}{\mathrm{colim}}|\Theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,/\theta}|

\displaystyle\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{D})\in\Theta_{n,/\mathscr{D}}}{\mathrm{colim}}*\cong|\mathscr{D}|,

where the non-trivial implication uses
Theorem

2.21
. Applying
6.17
to
|\mathscr{D}|
we also get

\mathrm{TwAr}(|\mathscr{D}|)\cong\underset{(\theta\xrightarrow{f}|\mathscr{D}|)\in\Theta_{n,/|\mathscr{D}|}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathrm{TwAr}(\theta).

Note that
\Theta_{n,/|\mathscr{D}|}
contains a cofinal subcategory on
c_{0}\rightarrow|\mathscr{D}|
which is isomorphic to
|\mathscr{D}|
, so we can rewrite the expression above as

|\mathscr{D}|\otimes\mathrm{TwAr}(c_{0})\cong|\mathscr{D}|.

∎

Theorem 7.9
.

For
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{3}
the space of morphisms
F:\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is isomorphic to the subspace of
\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
for which the image of the 2-morphism
\delta^{2}_{1}:[1]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[2]
is left adjoint to
\sigma_{0}^{1}:[2]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[1]
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathscr{I}:\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}\rightarrow\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{\mathrm{lax}}
the natural inclusion, it now follows from
6.20
,
7.7
,
Lemma

7.8
and the contractibility of
\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}
that
L_{\mathscr{I}}\cong 0
. It now follows from
6.21
that to prove the claim it suffices to prove it for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(4,3)}
, in which case the result is classical – see
[
16
]
.
∎

Appendix A
Dold-Kan correspondence for
\Theta_{n}

Dold-Kan correspondence is a classical result of
[
8
]
and
[
15
]
providing an isomorphism between the category of simplicial abelian groups and chain complexes. Numerous generalizations of this result have been described since, in this paper we will be interested in describing the generalization of this result to stable
\infty
-categories.

The principal idea behind the Dold-Kan correspondence is the observation that the simplex category
\Delta
admits many split idempotents, and in an abelian category
A
every such idempotent on an object
X
gives rise to a direct sum decomposition
X=X_{0}\oplus X_{1}
. Using this observation then allows us to locate a smaller subcategory
C\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},A)
isomorphic to the category of chain complexes such that every object in the original category decomposes as a direct sum of objects in
C
. Exploiting this insight, the paper
[
17
]
introduced a combinatorial structure that gives rise to equivalences of similar type and provided a number of examples. Lastly, this notion has been generalized to
\infty
-categories in
[
28
]
under the name of
DK-triples
.

The paper
[
28
]
provides a number of examples of DK-triples, however the one we need in the present work is missing, namely the structure of a DK-triple on
\Theta_{n}
and more generally on
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
for
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
. The goal of the present section is to provide an explicit description of this structure and the resulting DK-equivalence which will take the form

\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})\cong\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta})

whose terms will be defined below. Sadly, as the reader will soon find out, working with
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta})
is hardly any easier than with the original category
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})
. Nevertheless it does help in our particular case since as will be demonstrated in
Section

2
the subcategory
\mathrm{Stab}(\mathrm{Cat}_{n,/\theta})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})
admits a much simpler description after the application of the Dold-Kan equivalence.

Notation A.1
.

Given
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
and
i\in\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)
we will denote in this section
\theta_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i,i+1)\in\Theta_{n-1}
. Additionally, for a morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
and
f(i)\leq k<f(i+1)
we will denote
f_{i}^{k}:\theta_{i}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{k}
the induced functor of morphism categories.

Definition A.2
.

Call a morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
in
\Theta_{n}

injective
if it is injective on the set of
n
-morphisms and
surjective
if it is surjective on the set of
n
-morphisms.

Proposition A.3
.

(1)

If
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
is injective, then it is also injective on the set of
j
-morphisms for all
j<n
;

(2)

if
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
is surjective, then it is also surjective on the set of
j
-morphisms for all
j<n
;

(3)

injective and surjective morphisms form a factorization system on
\Theta_{n}
.

Proof.

Assume that a morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
is injective on the set of
n
-morphisms, but not on the set of
j
-morphisms for some
j<n
, then there is a pair of
j
-morphisms
(a,a^{\prime})
such that
f(a)=f(a^{\prime})
, but then we also have
f(\mathrm{id}^{n}_{a})=f(\mathrm{id}^{n}_{a^{\prime}})
, where
\mathrm{id}^{n}_{a}
denotes the identity
n
-morphism on
a
, which means
\mathrm{id}^{n}_{a}=\mathrm{id}^{n}_{a^{\prime}}
by injectivity of
f
, which in turn implies
a=a^{\prime}
, contradicting our assumption. This proves the first claim, the second one follows since for any
j
-morphism
b
the morphism
\mathrm{id}^{n}_{b}
must lie in the image of
f
, which implies that
b
also lies in the image of
f
.

Finally, we will prove the last claim by induction on
n
. More specifically, we will show that any morphism
f:\theta\rightarrow\prod_{0\leq j\leq m}\theta^{\prime}_{j}
factors uniquely as

\theta\xrightarrow{s}\theta_{0}\xrightarrow{q}\prod_{0\leq j\leq m}\theta^{\prime}_{j},

where
s
is surjective and
q
is injective (by which we mean that it is injective on
n
-morphisms). We start with
n=1
, in this case a morphism
[n]\xrightarrow{f}\prod_{j}[m_{j}]
is injective (resp. surjective) if and only if it is injective (resp. surjective) on the set of objects. We can factor the induced morphism of sets
|n+1|\rightarrow\prod_{j}|m_{j}+1|
as

|n+1|\xrightarrow{s_{)}}|l+1|\xrightarrow{i_{0}}\prod_{j}|m_{j}+1|,

where
s_{0}
is surjective and
i_{0}
is injective. Note that the linear order on
|n+1|
induces one on
|l+1|
and hence we can factor
f
as

[n]\xrightarrow{s}[l]\xrightarrow{i}\prod_{j}[m_{j}],

where
s
is surjective and
i
injective, the uniqueness of such factorization follows from the uniqueness of the surjective/injective factorization of sets. In the general case, We can again factor the induced morphism on objects as

(133)

\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)\xrightarrow{s_{0}}S\xrightarrow{i_{0}}\prod_{0\leq j\leq m}\mathrm{Ob}(\theta^{\prime}_{j}),

where
s_{0}
is surjective and
i_{0}
is injective; note that the linear order on
\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)
induces one on
S
. We define an object
\theta_{s}\in\Theta_{n}
as follows: its set of objects is the set
S
of (
133
), for
j\in S
we define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{s}}(j,j+1)
to be
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta}(i_{m},i_{m}+1)
, where
i_{m}
is the maximal element in
s^{-1}(j)
. With this definition we have a factorization

\theta\xrightarrow{s^{\prime}}\theta_{s}\xrightarrow{j^{\prime}}\prod_{j}\theta^{\prime}_{j},

where
s^{\prime}
is surjective on objects and
j^{\prime}
is injective on objects. Now, fix
i\in\mathrm{Ob}(\theta_{s})
, then the morphism
j^{\prime}
induces a morphism

j^{\prime}_{i}:\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{s}}(i,i+1)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\prod_{j}\theta^{\prime}_{j}}(j^{\prime}(i),j^{\prime}(i+1))\cong\prod_{k}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k}

for some
\theta_{k}^{\prime\prime}\in\Theta_{n-1}
. By induction,
j^{\prime}_{i}
admits an injective/surjective factorization of the form

\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{s}}(i,i+1)\xrightarrow{s_{i}}\theta_{i}^{0}\xrightarrow{q_{i}}\prod_{k}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k},

we then define
\theta^{\prime}_{s}
to have the same set of objects as
\theta_{s}
and set
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta^{\prime}_{s}}(i,i+1)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\theta_{i}^{0}
, then the morphisms
s_{i}
and
q_{i}
induce a factorization of
j^{\prime}
as

\theta_{s}\xrightarrow{s}\theta^{\prime}_{s}\xrightarrow{j}\prod_{j}\theta^{\prime}_{j},

where
s
is surjective and identity-on-objects and
j
is injective. Precomposing it with
\theta\xrightarrow{s}\theta_{s}
gives us the required factorization, the fact that it is unique again follows from the uniqueness of the injective/surjective factorization on sets.
∎

Notation A.4
.

Denote by
\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{k,/\theta}
the subcategory of
\Theta_{k,/\theta}
on injective morphisms and by
p_{\mathrm{inj}}:\Theta_{k,/\theta}\rightarrow\Theta^{\mathrm{inj}}_{k,/\theta}
the functor obtained by sending
f:\theta^{\prime}\rightarrow\theta
to the injective part of its injective/surjective factorization of
A.3
.

Construction A.5
.

Denote by
E_{n}([0])
the subcategory of
\Theta_{n}
on surjective morphisms (in the sense of
A.2
). We will define the subcategory
E^{\lor}_{n}([0])
of
\Theta_{n}
by induction on
n
as follows: for
n=1
we define
E_{1}^{\lor}([0])
to be the category of injective morphisms preserving the minimal element, for general
n
we define a morphism
j:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
to be in
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
if
j(0)=0
and the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)_{j}

j
is injective;

(2)_{j}

j
preserves the minimal element;

(3)_{j}

for
k<j(i+1)-1
the morphism
j_{i}^{k}
factors as

\theta_{i}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime}_{k},

where the second morphism is the inclusion of the minimal element, and the morphism
j_{i}^{j(i+1)-1}
lies in
E_{n-1}^{\lor}([0])
.

Finally, we define the set of morphisms
M_{n}
by induction, starting with
M_{1}
which contains all identity morphisms as well as
[n-1]\overset{\lambda^{n}_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}[n]
- the inert morphism preserving the maximal element. In general, define a morphism
u:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime\prime}
to be in
M_{n}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)_{M}

u
is injective;

(2)_{M}

either
u(0)=0
or
u(0)=1
, in the latter case
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta^{\prime\prime}}(0,1)=[0]
, in the former
\dim(\theta)>1
;

(3)_{M}

the induced morphisms
u_{i}^{k}:\theta_{i}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k}
for

all have the form
v_{i}^{k}\circ s_{i}^{k}
, where
s_{i}^{k}
are surjective and
v_{i}^{k}
lie in
M_{n-1}
, additionally either none of
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k}
equal
[0]
or
u(i+1)=u(i)+1
and the morphism
u_{i}^{i}
is
[0]=\joinrel=[0]
.

We will now define the subcategories
E_{n}(\theta)
and
E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
of
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
as well as a set of morphisms
M_{n}(\theta)
in
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
for all
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
, we will do so by induction on
\dim(\theta)
with the base case
\theta=[0]
having been treated above. Now, assume that we have defined
F(\theta^{\prime})
for
F\in\{E_{n},E_{n}^{\lor},M_{n}\}
and
\dim(\theta^{\prime})<m
, define
F(\prod_{k\in K}\theta^{\prime}_{k})
with
\dim(\theta^{\prime}_{k})<m
for a finite set
K
to be the subcategory of
\Theta_{n,/\prod_{K}\theta^{\prime}_{k}}
containing all objects and only those morphisms whose image in
\Theta_{n,/\theta^{\prime}_{k}}
for all
k\in K
lies in
F(\theta^{\prime}_{k})
. We will now define
F(\theta)
assuming
\dim(\theta)=m
. We define
E_{n}(\theta)
to be the subcategory of surjective morphisms, we define
E_{n}^{\lor}(\theta)
to consists of injective morphisms
j:\theta_{f}\rightarrow\theta_{g}
such that:

(1)_{j}^{\theta}

viewed as a morphism in
\Theta_{n}
,
j
lies in
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
;

(2)_{j}^{\theta}

for any

we have
g(j(i))=g(j(i+1)-1)
;

(3)_{j}^{\theta}

for any

the morphism
j_{i}^{j(i+1)-1}
belongs to
E^{\lor}_{n-1}(\prod_{s=f(i)}^{f(i+1)-1}\theta_{s})
.

Finally, define
M_{n}(\theta)
to be the set of morphisms
u:\theta_{f}\rightarrow\theta_{g}
such that:

(1)_{M}^{\theta}

u
is injective;

(2)_{M}^{\theta}

either
u(0)=0
or
u(0)=1
, in the latter case
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{g}}(0,1)=[0]
, in the former
\dim(\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{g}}(0,1))>1
;

(3)_{M}^{\theta}

the induced morphisms
u_{i}^{k}:\theta_{f,i}\rightarrow\theta_{k,g}
for
u(i)\leq k<u(i+1)-1
all have the form
v_{i}^{k}\circ s_{i}^{k}
, where
s_{i}^{k}
are surjective and
v_{i}^{k}
lie in
M_{n-1}(\Theta_{n,/\prod_{s=g(k)}^{g(k+1)-1}\theta_{s}})
;

(4)_{M}^{\theta}

if the target of some
u_{i}^{k}
equals
[0]
, then either
g(k)<g(k+1)
or
u(i+1)=u(i)+1
and and the morphism
u_{i}^{i}
is
[0]=\joinrel=[0]
.

We will also denote by
\mathrm{Mono}_{n}(\theta)
the category of injective morphisms, by
\mathrm{Reg}_{n}(\theta)
the set of morphisms of the form
m\circ e
, where
m\in M_{n}(\theta)
and
e\in E_{n}(\theta)
and by
\mathrm{Sing}_{n}(\theta)
the category of morphisms of the form
e^{\prime}\circ g
for some
e^{\prime}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
that is not an isomorphism.

Lemma A.6
.

There are inverse equivalences

G_{n}^{\theta}:E_{n}(\theta)\overset{\sim}{\rightleftarrows}E_{n}^{\lor}(\theta)^{\mathrm{op}}:H_{n}^{\theta}.

Proof.

We will prove the claim by double induction on
n
and the dimension of
\theta
. For the base case of
n=1
and
\theta=[0]
note that any surjective
s:[l]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[q]
admits a left adjoint
j(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\min_{s(t)=k}t
that preserves the minimal element and is injective and conversely any injective minimal-element-preserving morphism
j
admits a left adjoint
s(k)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\max_{j(t)<k}t
, which is surjective, this correspondence is functorial by functoriality of adjoints and defines the required equivalence.

To prove the equivalence for general
n
we first need to show that
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
is a category: given a composable pair
\theta\xrightarrow{j}\theta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}\theta^{\prime\prime}
we need to show that
(w\circ j)_{i}^{k}
satisfies conditions

(
1
)

j

,

(
2
)

j

and

(
3
)

j

. The first two are easy since injective and minimum-preserving morphisms are closed under composition, to prove

(
3
)

j

note that for
k<w\circ j(i+1)-1
it is either given by the composition

\theta_{i}\rightarrow[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime}_{s}\xrightarrow{w_{s}^{k}}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k},

where
w_{s}^{k}
preserves the minimal element or by

\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{j_{i}^{j(i+1)-1}}\theta^{\prime}_{j(i+1)-1}\rightarrow[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k},

in either case it factors through
[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{k}
, and for
k=w\circ j(i+1)-1
it is given by the composition
\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{j_{i}^{j(i+1)-1}}\theta^{\prime}_{j(i+1)-1}\xrightarrow{w_{j(i+1)-1}^{w(j(i+1))-1}}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{w(j(i+1))-1}
in which both morphisms lie in
E^{\lor}_{n-1}([0])
, hence the composition itself also belongs to it by inductive assumption. Assume now that we have a surjective morphism
\theta\overset{s}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta^{\prime}
, assume
|\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)|=(l+1)
and
|\mathrm{Ob}(\theta^{\prime})|=m+1
, then we get an induced morphism
\widetilde{s}:[l]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[m]
which admits a section
\widetilde{j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{1}^{[0]}(\widetilde{s})
. Note that for
\widetilde{j}(i)\leq k<\widetilde{j}(i+1)
we have
\widetilde{s}(k)=i
, denote by
s_{i}
the surjective morphism
\theta_{\widetilde{j}(i+1)-1}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta^{\prime}_{i}
induced by
s
. To upgrade
\widetilde{j}
to an element of
E^{\lor}_{n}([0])
we need to define morphisms
j_{i}^{k}:\theta^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow\theta_{k}
for
\widetilde{j}(i)\leq k<\widetilde{j}(i+1)
, we define it to be the composition
\theta^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta_{k}
for
k<\widetilde{j}(i+1)-1
and for
k=\widetilde{j}(i+1)-1
we define it to be the section
G^{[0]}_{n-1}(s_{i})
. Conversely, given a morphism
j:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime\prime}
which induces
\widetilde{j}
on objects, denote
\widetilde{s}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}H^{[0]}_{1}(\widetilde{j})
, in order to define a surjective section
s
of
j
it suffices to define surjective morphisms
s_{i}:\theta^{\prime\prime}_{\widetilde{j}(i+1)-1}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta_{i}
, we define them to be the image of
H_{n}^{[0]}(j_{i}^{j(i+1)-1})
(note that those morphisms lie in
E^{\lor}_{n-1}([0])
by definition). It now immediately follows from the inductive assumptions and definition of
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
that those functors are inverse to each other.

Assume now that we have constructed the equivalence
G_{n}^{\theta^{\prime}}
for all
\theta^{\prime}
with
\dim(\theta^{\prime})<m
and moreover assume that the underlying morphism of
G_{n}^{\theta^{\prime}}(s)
coincides with
G_{n}^{[0]}(s)
for any
s\in E_{n}(\theta)
and similarly for
H^{\theta^{\prime}}_{n}
. Assume we have
\theta\in\Theta_{n}
with
\dim(\theta)=m
and a surjective
s:\theta_{f}\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}\theta_{g}
over
\theta
we define
j\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{n}^{\theta}(s)
to be
G_{n}^{[0]}(s)
, note that
f\circ j\cong g\circ s\circ j\cong g
since
j
is a section of
s
, hence
j
is automatically a morphism over
\theta
, it remains to show that it lies in
E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
.

(
1
)

j

θ

follows since
j
lies in
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
by construction, for

(
2
)

j

θ

note that
f(j(i+1)-1)=g(s(j(i+1)-1))=g(s(j(i)))=g(i)=f(j(i))
and

(
3
)

j

θ

follows from the inductive assumption. Conversely, given
j:\theta_{f}\rightarrow\theta_{h}
with
j\in E_{n}^{\lor}(\theta)
we need to prove that
s\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}H^{[0]}_{n}(j)
lies in
E_{n}(\theta)
. Since
s
is surjective by construction, it suffices to show that it is a morphism over
\theta
, which follows immediately from

(
2
)

j

θ

,

(
3
)

j

θ

and the inductive assumption.
∎

Proposition A.7
.

Every morphism
\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{h}\theta_{g}
in
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
factors uniquely as

\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e\in E_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{s}\xrightarrow{m\in M_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{t}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{g}.

Proof.

We have already seen in
A.3
that any morphism uniquely factors as
j\circ s
with surjective
s
and injective
j
, so it remains to show that any injective morphism uniquely factors as
e^{\prime}\circ m
as above. We will once again prove it by induction on
n
and
\dim(\theta)
. For the base case
n=1
,
\theta=[0]
observe that any injective morphism
j:[p]\rightarrow[l]
that does not lie in
E^{\lor}_{1}([0])
(i.e. does not preserve the minimal element) can be uniquely factored as

[p]\overset{\lambda_{0}^{p}}{\rightarrowtail}[p+1]\xrightarrow{j^{\prime}}[l],

where
j^{\prime}(0)=0
and
j^{\prime}(k)=j(k-1)
for
k>0
, so the claim holds in this case. For the general
n
we will first construct a factorization and then show that it is unique: given
j:\theta\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
denote by
j_{i}^{k}:\theta_{i}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{k}
the induced functors on morphism categories. By induction we can factor each
j_{k}^{i}
as

(134)

\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{e_{i}^{k}}\widetilde{\theta}_{i}^{k}\xrightarrow{m_{i}^{k}}\widehat{\theta}_{i}^{k}\xrightarrow{e_{i}^{{}^{\prime},k}}\theta^{\prime}_{k}.

Denote by
\widehat{\theta}
the object of
\Theta_{n}
constructed as follows: if
j
preserves the minimal object, then the objects of
\widehat{\theta}
are pairs
(i,k)
with
i\in\mathrm{Ob}(\theta)
and
j(i)\leq k<j(i+1)
, otherwise we add an additional minimal object
*
, we also set
\widetilde{\theta}_{(i,k)}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\widehat{\theta}_{i}^{k}
in the notation of (
134
) and
\widehat{\theta}_{*}=[0]
if applicable, then
j
factors as

(135)

\theta\xrightarrow{j^{\prime}}\widehat{\theta}\xrightarrow{j^{\prime\prime}}\theta^{\prime},

where
j^{\prime}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(i,j(i))
and
j_{i}^{{}^{\prime},k}
is the composition
m_{i}^{k}\circ e_{i}^{k}
while
j^{\prime\prime}(i,k)=k
and
j_{(i,k)}^{k}
is
e_{i}^{{}^{\prime},k}
in the notation of (
134
), if
j
does not preserve the minimal element we also set
j^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
and define all the morphisms
j_{*}^{{}^{\prime\prime},r}
to be the inclusions
[0]\hookrightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{r}
of the minimal element. The morphism
j^{\prime\prime}
lies in
E^{\lor}_{n}([0])
, but
j^{\prime}
does not belong to
M_{n}([0])
since it does not satisfy the second part of

(
3
)

M

, so we will factor it further. Assume that
|\mathrm{Ob}(\widehat{\theta})\setminus\{*\}|=m+1
, then we can define a surjective morphism
s^{\prime}:[m]\overset{}{\twoheadrightarrow}[q]
as follows: to define such a morphism it suffices to describe which elementary intervals
q<q+1
are sent to identity morphisms, we define
s^{\prime}(i,k)=s^{\prime}(i,k+1)
if
\widetilde{\theta}_{(i,k)}=[0]
, denote by
l:[q]\rightarrow[m]
the left adjoint of
s^{\prime}
. Now, denote by
\widetilde{\theta}\in\Theta_{n}
the
n
-category with the set of object given by
\{0,...,q\}
or
\{*,0,...,q\}
such that
\widetilde{\theta}_{i}=\widehat{\theta}_{l(i+1)-1}
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{*}=[0]
, note that
j^{\prime}
factors as
\theta\xrightarrow{m^{\prime}}\widetilde{\theta}\xrightarrow{w}\widehat{\theta}
, where
m^{\prime}
sends
i
to
s^{\prime}(j^{\prime}(i))
and
m_{i}^{{}^{\prime},k}=j_{i}^{{}^{\prime},l(k+1)-1}
, while
w
sends
k
to
l(k)
and
w_{k}^{t}
are the unique morphisms
\widetilde{\theta}_{k}\rightarrow[0]
for
t\neq l(k+1)-1
and the identity morphism otherwise (and sends
*
to the minimal element if it is an object of
\widetilde{\theta}
). It is easy to see from this description that the morphism
m^{\prime}
now satisfies the second part of

(
3
)

M

and that
w
lies in
E_{n}^{\lor}([0])
, concluding our construction.

We now need to show that such a factorization is unique, so assume we have a different factorization
\theta\xrightarrow{n^{\prime}}\overline{\theta}\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\theta^{\prime}
of
j
. We start by showing that
n^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}
agree with
m^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}
on objects: first, note that
n^{\prime}
preserves the minimal object if and only if
j
does (since
w^{\prime}
preserves it by definition) and if it does not, then
w^{\prime}(0)=0
and the components
w_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime},k}
are all inclusions of the minimal element since
w^{\prime}
lies in
E-n^{\lor}([0])
. Now fix some
i\in\theta
, we claim that

n^{\prime}(i+1)-n^{\prime}(i)=\max(1,|k:\;\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{j^{k}_{i}}\theta^{\prime}_{k}\text{ does not factor through }[0]\xhookrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime}_{k}|).

indeed, if all
j_{i}^{k}
factor through
[0]
, then by

(
3
)

M

we must have
n^{\prime}(i+1)=n^{\prime}(i)+1
, so assume this is not the case. Note that
j_{i}^{k}=w_{t}^{{}^{\prime},k}\circ n_{i}^{{}^{\prime},t}
, none of the
n_{i}^{{}^{\prime},t}
factor through
[0]
and by

(
3
)

j

for a given
t
there is exactly one
k
such that
w_{t}^{{}^{\prime\prime},k}
does not factor through
[0]
, from which the claim follows. This also uniquely defines the value of
n^{\prime}
on objects and the value of
w^{\prime}
on objects is now uniquely determined by

(
3
)

j

. It remains to show that the values of
n^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}
on morphism categories are uniquely defined, however this follows from

(
3
)

M

,

(
3
)

j

and the inductive hypothesis.

It remains to construct the factorization over general
\theta
, assuming it has been constructed over
\theta^{\prime}
with
\dim(\theta^{\prime})<\dim(\theta)
. First, note that the factorization of
A.3
also exists in
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
, so it suffices to provide a factorization of an injective morphism. The construction in the relative case will be very similar to the construction in the absolute case described above, so we will give slightly less details. First, we can factor the underlying morphism of
j:\theta_{f}\rightarrow\theta_{g}
as
j^{\prime\prime}\circ j^{\prime}
as in (
135
), it is easy to see by construction that
j^{\prime\prime}
lies in
E_{n}^{\lor}(\theta)
, so it remains to factor
j^{\prime}
. Assume that
|\mathrm{Ob}(\widehat{\theta})\setminus\{*\}|=m+1
, then we define a surjective morphism
s:[m]\rightarrow[q]
by sending
(i,k)<(i,k+1)
to identity if
\theta_{g\circ j,(i,k)}=[0]
and
g\circ j(i,k)=g\circ j(i,k+1)
. After this the construction goes through unchanged, the arguments proving the uniqueness of the factorization also works upon replacing references to

(
3
)

M

with

(
4
)

M

θ

.
∎

Proposition A.8
.

The data of
(E_{n}(\theta),E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta),M_{n}(\theta))
of
A.5
defines a DK-triple in the sense of
[
28
]
.

Proof.

Our construction of the subcategories
M_{n}(\theta)
,
\mathrm{Mono}_{n}(\theta)
and
\mathrm{Reg}_{n}(\theta)
is different from the one given in
[
28
]
, however it follows from
A.7
that they coincide. More specifically, for a category
B
endowed with subcategories
E
and
E^{\lor}

\mathrm{Mono}
is defined as a subset of arrows not of the form
f\circ e
for some arrow
f
and
e\in E
,
\mathrm{Sing}
as a set of arrows of the form
e^{\prime}\circ g
for
e^{\prime}\neq\mathrm{id}\in E^{\lor}
,
\mathrm{Reg}
as the complement to
\mathrm{Sing}
and
M\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Reg}\bigcap\mathrm{Mono}
, it is easy to see from the existence of the factorization
A.7
that this coincides with our definition.

It follows that we need to prove the conditions outlined in
[
28
, Definition 3.1.1.]
, we reproduce them here in our notation for convenience of the reader:

(T1)

every morphism
f
in
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
uniquely decomposes as

\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e\in E_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{s}\xrightarrow{m\in M_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{t}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)}\theta_{g};

(T2)

for any
\theta_{f}\in\Theta_{,/\theta}
the pairing
E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)_{/\theta_{f}}\times E_{n}(\theta)_{\theta_{f}/}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})
given by sending
\theta_{g}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}}\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e}\theta_{h}
can be described by a square matrix of the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}=&?&\dots&?\\
\neq&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&?\\
\neq&\neq&\neq&=\end{array}\right)

with isomorphisms on the diagonal and non-isomorphisms below it;

(T3)

the set
(E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)\circ E_{n}(\theta))
is closed under composition;

(T4)

the set
M_{n}(\theta)\circ M_{n}(\theta)
belongs to
\mathrm{Mono}_{n}(\theta)
;

(T5)

\mathrm{Mono}_{n}(\theta)\circ\mathrm{Sing}_{n}(\theta)\subset\mathrm{Sing}_{n}(\theta)
.

The claims
(T4)
and
(T5)
are immediate since injective morphisms are closed under composition and
(T1)
is just
A.7
, so it remains to prove
(T2)
and
(T3)
. For
(T3)
it suffices to show that given a surjective/injective factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta_{h}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}^{\prime}}

with
e_{0}\in E_{n}(\theta)
and
e^{\prime}_{0}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
we also have
e_{1}\in E_{n}(\theta)
and
e_{1}^{\prime}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
. That
e_{1}\in E_{n}(\theta)
follows immediately from the definition, so it remains to prove
e_{1}^{\prime}\in E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
, which we will do by induction on
n
and
\dim(\theta)
. For
n=1
the claim follows since the composition of a surjective and minimum-preserving morphism preserves minimum, so assume we have proved it for
n-1
. In this case
e_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies

(
1
)

j

by construction and

(
2
)

j

by earlier observation, so it remains to show

(
3
)

j

. Take an object
i\in\theta_{f}
such that
e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i<i+1)
is not an identity, in this case
e_{1}(i+1)=e_{1}(i)+1
, denote
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{s}}(e_{1}(i),e_{1}(i)+1)
. Denote by

\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{\prod w_{k}}\prod^{e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i+1)-1}_{k=e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i)}\theta^{\prime}_{k}

the morphism induced by
e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}
on morphism categories, so by construction and

(
3
)

j

we have that
w_{k}
factor through
[0]\xrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime}_{k}
for
k<e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{i}(i+1)-1
and
w_{e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{i}(i+1)-1}\cong s_{i}\circ j_{i}
with surjective
s_{i}
and
j_{i}\in E^{\lor}_{n-1}([0])
. We can factor
\prod_{k}w_{k}
as

\theta_{i}\xrightarrow{s^{\prime}}\theta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{\prod w^{\prime}_{k}}\prod^{e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i+1)-1}_{k=e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i)}\theta^{\prime}_{k},

where
s^{\prime}
is surjective and
w^{\prime}_{i}
are injective, so to prove

(
3
)

j

it remains to show that
w^{\prime}_{k}
factors through
[0]\xrightarrow{\{0\}}\theta^{\prime}_{k}
for
k<e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i+1)-1
and lies in
E^{\lor}_{n-1}([0])
otherwise. For that note that
w_{k}
factors through the inclusion of the minimal element for
k<e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i+1)-1
, which implies the first part of the claim, and the second follows by inductive assumption since
w^{\prime}_{e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{0}(i+1)-1}\circ s^{\prime}
is the injective/surjective factorization of
w_{e_{0}\circ e^{\prime}_{i}(i+1)-1}\cong s_{i}\circ j_{i}
. Finally, in the case of general
\theta

(
1
)

j

θ

follows by what we just proved,

(
3
)

j

θ

by induction and

(
2
)

j

θ

follows since for any object
i\in\theta_{g}
such that
e_{0}(i)=e_{0}(i+1)
we also have
g(i)=g(i+1)
.

It remains to prove
(T2)
. Given
\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e}\theta_{g}
we will denote by
e^{\lor}
the image of
e
under the isomorphism of
Lemma

A.6
. We will prove the claim by induction on
n
, starting with
n=1
; the object
\theta
over which the construction is performed will play no role, so we will suppress mentioning it. Note that if we have two morphisms
f,g:[m]\rightarrow[l]
in
E_{1}
such that for some
i
we have
f(i)<g(i)
, then
f^{\lor}\circ g(i)>i
, so in particular it is not an isomorphism. It follows that if we order
E^{\lor}_{1,/[l]}
lexicographically (which is a linear order), then the matrix of the pairing from
(T2)
has the required form. More generally, assume we have provided a linear order on
E^{\lor}_{n-1,/\theta^{\prime\prime}}
with the required property. Any
\theta^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}}\theta^{\prime\prime}
in
E_{n}^{\lor}
such that
|\mathrm{Ob}(\theta^{\prime})|=\{0,...,m\}
and
|\mathrm{Ob}(\theta^{\prime\prime})|=\{0,...,l\}
is uniquely determined by the underlying morphism
e^{\prime}_{\mathrm{Ob}}:[m]\rightarrow[l]
on objects together with morphisms
e^{\prime}_{i}:\theta^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow\theta^{\prime\prime}_{e_{\mathrm{Ob}}^{\prime}(i+1)-1}
, we have a lexicographical order on
E^{\lor}_{1,/[l]}
and linear orders on
E^{\lor}_{n-1,/\theta^{\prime\prime}_{e_{\mathrm{Ob}}^{\prime}(i+1)-1}}
by induction, so we can order the set of tuples
(e^{\prime}_{\mathrm{Ob}},e^{\prime}_{0},...,e^{\prime}_{m-1})
lexicographically, it follows by induction that with this ordering the matrix in
(T2)
has the required property.
∎

Notation A.9
.

Given
\theta_{f}\in\Theta_{n,/\theta}
denote

\widetilde{\theta}_{f}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{CoKer}(\underset{\theta_{g}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}}\theta_{f}}{\mathrm{colim}}\theta_{g}\rightarrow\theta_{f}),

where the colimit is taken over all non-identity morphisms
\theta_{g}\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}}\theta_{f}
in
E^{\lor}_{n}(\theta)
. Also denote by
\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta}
the pointed category with the same objects as
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta}}(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})\cong\{0\}\bigcup\{M_{n}(\theta)(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})\}
, where
M_{n}(\theta)(\theta_{f},\theta_{g})
denotes the set of morphisms in
M_{n}(\theta)
with source
\theta_{f}
and target
\theta_{g}
such that for a composable pair
m^{\prime}\circ m^{\prime\prime}\cong m
if their composition in
\Theta_{n,/\theta}
equals
m
and
m\in M_{n}(\theta)
and
m^{\prime}\circ m^{\prime\prime}\cong 0
otherwise.

Corollary A.10
.

There is an equivalence

\widetilde{\theta}_{f}\cong\ker(\theta_{f}\rightarrow\underset{\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e}\theta_{h}}{\lim}\theta_{h}),

where the limit is taken over non-identity morphisms
\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e}\theta_{h}
in
E_{n}(\theta)
, moreover

(136)

\theta_{f}\cong\bigoplus_{\theta_{f}\xrightarrow{e}\theta_{h}}\widetilde{\theta}_{h},

where the sum is taken over all morphisms in
E_{n}(\theta)
(including the identity morphism). Additionally, there exists a morphism
F:\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})
sending
\theta_{f}
to
\widetilde{\theta}_{f}
which induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{DK}:\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\Theta_{n,/\theta})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,/\theta}):\mathrm{DK}^{\prime}

Proof.

In light of
A.8
, this follows from the main result of
[
28
]
.
∎
